# Changelog

## Unreleased

### Fixed

- **A `--only` filter that matches nothing no longer prints a results line (#434).** The exit code was already 1 — that was never the defect. The defect was the line printed immediately above it: `=== Results: 0 passed, 0 failed ===`. `AGENTS.md`'s own rule is that **a verdict comes from the suite's own `=== Results:` line, never from an exit code**, so a driver following the documented rule read a typo'd filter as a clean run — and a mutation campaign whose filter silently stopped matching would have reported a table of greens.

  **The fix prints no results line rather than a different one**, because `AGENTS.md` already defines the semantics that makes that correct: *"a run with no `=== Results:` line is VOID rather than red."* A zero-match run **is** void — nothing was measured — so suppressing the line makes an existing rule do the work instead of adding a second one readers must learn. An unmistakable `=== VOID: nothing ran …` line goes to stderr so the run does not merely fall silent.

  **Consumers were checked, not assumed.** `.github/workflows/flake-hunt.yml:134` counts completed runs with `grep -l '=== Results:'`, and a void run correctly stops counting as completed — it also never takes this path, being unfiltered. Both in-suite parsers spawn filters that **match**; the second had already been migrated off the zero-match shape by #437's review, explicitly so this fix would not break it silently. The pinning assertion is that the line is **absent** — asserting the exit code again would re-prove the half that already worked.

### Changed

- **`AGENTS.md` § "Testing discipline": two amendments, each carrying a measured instance (#440).**

  - **A mutation must have LANDED and must MEAN something — two claims, and the second has no cheap check.** `String.replace` with a pattern that does not occur returns the string unchanged without throwing, so `mutated !== original` is required. **That is necessary and not sufficient**: a row replacing `id: notes` with `id: notesX` changed the file — so the check held — while the assertion under test does `indexOf("id: notes")`, which **still matches the longer string**. Zero red, and a row with no red reads exactly like *"this assertion is redundant"*. New rule: **the replacement must not contain the original**, and ask how the code under test **reads** the string. Plus the sharpest form: **ask whether running the row performs the act the guard prevents** — a row that removed a test's containment to prove the containment mattered ran a real installer through to `launchctl bootout`/`bootstrap` and redefined a live service, twice. Replace such rows with assertions that are **each other's control**.
  - **The registration-count grep is a cross-check, not the authority, and widening it keeps failing.** Measured against four registrations (flat `test`, indented `test`, indented `ltTest`, flat `testAsync`): the shipped `^\+(test|ltTest)\(` counts **1**; adding `testAsync` alone counts **2**; **#440's own proposed `^\+\s*(test|ltTest)\(` counts 3 — it fixes indentation and still misses `testAsync`**; only `^\+\s*(test|ltTest|testAsync)\(` counts 4. Three successive widenings, each short — and **a grep over a diff cannot see a registration a conditional adds at all**, so no pattern closes it. The authoritative derivation is behavioural: `--only "<filter>"` against base and head. Same ceiling the ADR 0012 marker grep hit before #346 replaced the mechanism rather than the pattern.

## v3.30.0 — 2026-08-24

### Fixed

- **The declared Node prerequisite admitted Nodes on which OCP cannot start, and seven files disagreed about what it was.** `package.json` declared `"node": ">=22.5"` and `setup.mjs` gated on `parseInt(process.versions.node) < 18` — while `keys.mjs` does `import { DatabaseSync } from "node:sqlite"` at **module load** and `server.mjs` imports `keys.mjs` at module load. Nothing on any launch path passes `--experimental-sqlite`: `npm start` is `node server.mjs`, and the only two occurrences of that flag in the entire repo were **prose** (a docs sentence and a CI comment). So the installer reported *"prerequisites OK"* on 18.x, 20.x and 22.0–22.12, and the operator met the real constraint later — as an opaque `node:sqlite` error at first boot rather than as a prerequisite failure at install time.

  **The repo's own documentation was wrong too, twice in one sentence.** `docs/lan-mode.md` said *"Node 23+ recommended — `node:sqlite` is fully stable without flags from 23.0; on 22.5–22.x it works behind `--experimental-sqlite`"*. Checked against [nodejs.org/api/sqlite.html § History](https://nodejs.org/api/sqlite.html) rather than against the repo: the flag came off at **v22.13.0 and v23.4.0**, not 23.0 — so 22.13–22.x needs no flag — and *"fully stable"* is wrong for every Node in existence below **v25.7.0**, where it becomes a Release Candidate. **Unflagged** is the property that decides whether OCP boots; *stable* is not.

  - **A single lower bound cannot express this rule, and the obvious fix was wrong.** `>=22.13.0` — this change's own first draft — admits **23.0–23.3**, which still need the flag. That is the same defect one version down, and it was caught by the new boundary cases before it shipped. Node's history is now **data**: `scripts/lib/node-floor.mjs` holds `NODE_SQLITE_UNFLAGGED` (the two rows Node documents), `ENGINES_NODE` is **derived** from it as `>=22.13.0 <23.0.0 || >=23.4.0`, and a test fails if `package.json` disagrees — so adding a row moves the range rather than leaving a second copy to drift.
  - **`setup.mjs` now consults that predicate instead of a literal**, and refuses with a message that says *why* (`node:sqlite`, and the derived range) rather than naming a number. Pinned **behaviourally**, not by grepping the source: a child overrides `process.versions.node` before importing `setup.mjs`. What makes that safe is the containment described below — **not** the gate's position, and not a scratch `HOME`, which does not prevent either consequence.
  - **Seven files stated the prerequisite, and fixing the two that were pointed at would have left five untouched**: `package.json`, `setup.mjs`, `README.md`, `docs/troubleshooting.md`, `docs/lan-mode.md`, `.github/workflows/test.yml` (whose comment justified the Node 24 pin by calling `node:sqlite` "stable"), and `AGENTS.md` — which said `Node.js >=18`, **the exact number this change removes from `setup.mjs`**, in the file `AGENTS.md` § "Handoff expectations" names as read #1 for a fresh session. That one was found by independent review, not by the sweep. **A file list rather than a count of claims:** the first draft stated three different totals and none matched the diff, because nothing connected the number to what it counted — and `git diff | grep '^-[^-]'`, the obvious way to recount, silently drops markdown list items, which is how `AGENTS.md` read as zero even after it was fixed.
  - **Two of those were invisible to the first sweep**, which is why this ships with a guard rather than with care: one is spelled `≥ v22.5` and one had wrapped onto its own line as `22.5+, git, Claude CLI`. A single-line grep is structurally blind to both, so the docs test flattens newlines before matching — and the final manual sweep ran the *same pipeline* against a pattern known to be present, because a zero from a pipeline that never ran is indistinguishable from a zero that means clean.

  **Twelve mutations, 12 landed, 12 reddened, restores byte-identical to a file backup**, re-measured in full after each review changed the predicate or the guard — a table taken under a superseded predicate is not evidence about what ships.

  **One row was deleted because running it was itself the harm, and that is worth stating rather than quietly dropping.** The row removed the wiring test's empty-`PATH` containment in order to prove the containment was load-bearing. It proved it — by letting the child run the real `setup.mjs` through to **Step 7's `launchctl bootout` / `bootstrap`**, which redefined the operator's live service under the same job label, for the **second** time in this change's history. **A mutation that disables a safety guard to demonstrate the guard matters performs the very act the guard prevents.** It is replaced by something strictly better, which needs no mutation at all: the test's two drives are now **each other's control** — 22.12 must stop *at* the version gate and therefore must **not** carry the next check's message, while 24.0 must stop *at* that next check and therefore must. Either assertion going vacuous makes the other fail, on every run, rather than once in a campaign. Verified after the change by comparing `launchctl print`'s `path` immediately before and after the test: unchanged.

  **A second, external review closed the last unstated invariant.** `supportsUnflaggedSqlite` had been generalised for non-adjacent majors, but the **range** half still rested on "the rows happen to be strictly increasing". Reproduced before fixing: with `[{22,13},{22,20},{23,4}]` the first arm takes its upper bound from the next row's major and becomes `>=22.13.0 <22.0.0` — **an empty set** — so the range drops 22.13–22.19 while the predicate still answers `true` for 22.15.0, because its scan keeps the first row of a tied major. A repeated major is **malformed input** (two rows cannot both say when major 22 became unflagged), so `deriveEnginesRange` now **refuses** it rather than picking a winner, and refuses at module load so a bad table stops the build instead of shipping a range nobody checked. Its own mutation row removes the guard and reddens.

  **Reviewer provenance, stated as the backend's own script requires rather than summarised as "externally reviewed":** the finding came from `prime` (`stealth/ox-alpha` via OpenRouter, `cost=0`). `stealth/*` is an **anonymous evaluation slot — the vendor behind it is undisclosed**, so this is "not the author's harness", **not** an established cross-vendor review; it could be an unreleased model from the author's own vendor, in which case its independence tier is 2 (correlated) rather than 0, and that cannot be distinguished from here. Verdict was 0 P0, 0 P1, 1 P2. The P2 was reproduced locally before being acted on.

  **Independent review found that the docs guard was blind to the exact spelling its own entry says it was built for.** The `≥\s*v?` alternative was added to cover `≥ v22.5` — one of the two spellings that had defeated the first manual sweep — and then a **mandatory** trailing `\s*\+` was required, which that spelling does not have. Demonstrated by reverting `docs/troubleshooting.md`'s `node --version` line to `should be ≥ v22.5`: **the test stayed green.** The `+` is now optional, and that revert is a mutation row. Compounding it, the premise asserted a **total** (`found.length >= D22_DOCS.length`) while its message promised "at least one per doc", so one document could go entirely unmatched and still clear the bar on the others' matches; the premise is now per-document, and neutering *both* of `docs/troubleshooting.md`'s statements now reddens with exactly that message.

  **And an unstated invariant the predicate depended on.** `supportsUnflaggedSqlite` and `ENGINES_NODE` are two derivations of one table, and they agreed only because the two shipped rows happen to be **adjacent majors**. With `[22.13, 23.4, 26.2]` the derived range admits 24.x and 25.x while the old predicate answered `false` for both. Rather than assert the adjacency invariant, the predicate was **generalised** so it is correct for any table — the row scan now takes the highest row at or below the major — and `rows` is a parameter so a test measures that non-adjacent case instead of asserting about it.

  **One mutation proved nothing and is recorded as such.** Neutering only *one* of `docs/troubleshooting.md`'s two statements left the per-document premise satisfied by the other, so the row came back green; the driver prints `NO RED — proves nothing` for exactly this, and the row was re-run against both statements before being counted.

  **Two of those rows exist because the first draft of this test was itself a defect, and it is recorded rather than quietly fixed.** The supported-version control ran the real `setup.mjs` — **an installer** — which cleared the version gate and then kept going: into the claude auth probe (`claude -p … "ping"`, which `setup.mjs`'s own comment says **costs a metered credit**), on to Step 4, which writes `start.sh` to `join(__dirname, "start.sh")` i.e. **into the repository** where `cwd` cannot move it, and on to **Step 7**, which calls `installAutoStart` → `launchctl bootout` + `launchctl bootstrap` and therefore **took down and reloaded the operator's production service**. Because the job label is the same `dev.ocp.proxy`, the bootstrap loaded the plist `setup.mjs` had just written **inside the scratch HOME** — so the live proxy ran from a job definition in a temp directory this test deletes in its own `finally`, logging to a path that no longer existed. `/health` reported `ok` throughout; the only symptoms were the real log silently ceasing to grow and `launchctl print`'s `path`. It would not have survived a reboot. This is the same shape as the incident `AGENTS.md` already records — *"#217's review took production OCP down"* — and the same remedy applies: **the constraint has to be unreachable by construction, not stated as a prohibition.** It ran roughly ten times across the mutation campaign and two full suites before an untracked `start.sh` gave it away. A scratch `HOME` does **not** prevent the spend: on a host whose credentials live in the keychain, auth is HOME-independent. The child is now contained by an empty `PATH`, so the `claude --version` check immediately after the gate fails and exits before either consequence, with `OCP_SKIP_AUTH_TEST=1` as belt-and-braces for a future reordering; the control asserts it stopped at that next check, and a **pollution guard** asserts `start.sh` did not appear. The last mutation row removes the containment and confirms the guard reddens instead of the suite quietly dirtying the tree.

  **Recorded because it corrects an assumption in the test itself:** the "`setup.mjs` stops consulting the predicate" row reddens on the *"must say WHY"* assertion and **not** on the exit-status one — with the gate disabled, `setup.mjs` still exits 1 on a later prerequisite. An exit-status assertion alone would not have caught it.

  **Not measured, stated rather than implied:** CI runs Node 24 and only 24, so 22.13–23.x is a range derived from Node's documentation and not from a run. `test.yml`'s comment now says exactly that, in place of the claim it used to make. **Not endpoint-touching** — `server.mjs`, `lib/` and `keys.mjs` are 0 lines changed.
### Security

- **A request carrying a foreign `Origin` is now refused `403` on any method except `GET`/`HEAD`, before auth and before routing (ADR 0019).** `server.mjs` had **no CSRF defense of any kind, and none had been considered**: `git grep -niE 'csrf|cross-site request'` over the repo returned **zero** hits (exit 1), with `Allow-Origin` matching in **two** files as a positive control — `server.mjs` and one shipped plan doc. The single `Origin`-related line decided which value went into the *response* header and never rejected anything.

  **The chain was measured end to end against a live instance running the shipped defaults** (`CLAUDE_AUTH_MODE=none`, `CLAUDE_BIND=127.0.0.1`, no `CLAUDE_ALLOWED_TOOLS`, no `CLAUDE_SKIP_PERMISSIONS`), not reasoned about:

  - the server does not check the inbound `Content-Type` — `text/plain` with a malformed body answers `400 Invalid JSON`, **not `415`**, so the body reached `JSON.parse`; and `text/plain` is CORS-safelisted, so a cross-origin POST carrying it needs **no preflight**;
  - the same request carrying `Origin: https://evil.example.com` was processed identically;
  - the localhost branch admits it unconditionally — its own comment reads *"Localhost always allowed … never reject"*;
  - `ALLOWED_TOOLS` defaults to `Bash,Read,Write,Edit,Glob,Grep,WebSearch,WebFetch,Agent` and is passed as `--allowedTools`; and
  - **the spawned `claude` executes those tools without prompting** — a nonce file was written locally and a request through the live proxy asking it to `cat` that file came back carrying the nonce, an output only execution can produce.

  So the exposure was not "a web page can burn quota". It was **a web page running commands as the operator, blind** — and it is **not** gated by the frozen LAN/multi-user mode; it applies to the default single-user loopback deployment. The one link deliberately **not** measured, stated so it is not mistaken for one that was: whether a browser lets a public page reach `127.0.0.1` at all (Chrome's Private Network Access, version-dependent). The direction that matters is the other one — the server had no defense of its own, and the only thing standing between a visited page and command execution was a browser mitigation this project neither implements nor can depend on.

  **An absent `Origin` changes nothing, and that is what makes the gate free.** `curl`, the OpenAI SDKs and `ocp-connect` send none and are unaffected; no client changes, and no request shape moves. `Origin: null` (sandboxed iframe, `file://`) matches no allowlist entry and is refused — the direction that fails closed. `OPTIONS` is **not** exempt, so a foreign preflight fails at the preflight rather than one round trip later.

  **A `Content-Type: application/json` requirement was designed and deliberately NOT taken**, and ADR 0019 records why rather than leaving the omission to look like an oversight: it is unnecessary for this chain (a cross-origin POST always carries `Origin`, so the gate fires first), and its cost was measured — **31** `POST`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` senders in `test-features.mjs` of which **4** set a `Content-Type` at the call site, many deliberately sending malformed bodies to assert `400 Invalid JSON` that content-type gating would turn into `415`; and `dashboard.html` issues `DELETE` with **no body and no `Content-Type`**, which any method-based rule refuses.

  **Independent review found a regression this change introduced, and it is the reason the gate has a second arm.** The first version reasoned: `test-features.mjs` references `Origin` 0 times and `dashboard.html` is same-origin, therefore nothing breaks. Both measurements are true; **the inference is not.** Browsers send `Origin` on **same-origin** non-`GET`/`HEAD` requests too (Fetch Standard, *append a request `Origin` header*, step 3), and `dashboard.html` sets `BASE = window.location.origin` and `POST`s/`DELETE`s to it. So an operator reaching the dashboard at `ocp-host.local`, `[::1]`, a Tailscale CGNAT address, a public IP, or through a TLS reverse proxy — every one of which `README.md` and `docs/lan-mode.md` tell them to do — would have had "add key" and "revoke key" start returning 403. **Silently**: `apiPost`/`apiDelete` return `resp.json()` without reading the status, so revoking a compromised key would show a confirm, refresh the list, and leave the key there with no error anywhere. A security control failing invisibly on the operator's side is the wrong failure. Same-origin is now admitted by comparing the `Origin`'s host to the request's `Host` — **strictly stronger** than the allowlist, since a browser sets both itself and `Host` is a forbidden header name, so an attacker page cannot make them match unless it *is* that origin.

  **Ten mutations, 10 landed, 10 reddened**, restores byte-identical to a file backup, re-measured in full after each review moved the predicate (nine through the driver, one run standalone). **The most useful row is the one that simulates the fix this change does NOT make**: adding `Host` validation to close rebinding reddens the boundary test *and* the hostname-dashboard test **together** — which turns the tension between the two reviews' findings into an executable fact rather than a sentence in the ADR. The rest: removing the gate (0/4 — the pre-ADR-0019 behaviour restored), dropping the `GET`/`HEAD` exemption, inverting the allowlist (0/4), dropping the `origin &&` presence guard (**the control assertion** plus the body-less `DELETE`), exempting `OPTIONS`, dropping the same-origin arm (the regression above), changing the machine-readable `forbidden_origin` type, and two that isolate the host comparison. **Recorded because the eighth row did not prove what it was aimed at:** loosening the comparison to hostname-only reddens on the `[::1]` case rather than on the different-port case, because splitting an IPv6 authority on `:` is itself broken and that assertion runs first — so a ninth row, parsing both sides correctly and ignoring only the port, was written to reach *"a different port is a different origin"*. Two other review notes were closed the same way: the `forbidden_origin` body assertion had no row of its own (it does now), and the ADR index row was ordered between 0017 and 0018.

  Every test carries its own control, because *"the request was refused"* and *"the server never booted"* are the same observation from outside — and the same-origin test's control is the same hosts with a **foreign** `Origin`, without which "admitted" would be indistinguishable from a gate that had been removed.

  **It does not stop DNS rebinding, and that is the one boundary the same-origin arm newly creates.** An attacker serving their page on this port who flips the A record to `127.0.0.1` produces a request the browser considers **genuinely same-origin** — `Host` and `Origin` both carry the attacker's domain and are equal by construction. Measured: that pair on the same port returns **200**; on different ports (a real cross-origin request) **403**. **No `Origin` check can close it**, because the property being checked is genuinely true. Closing it needs `Host` validated against hostnames the operator has **declared** — configuration this project does not have, and reusing the existing allowlist for `Host` would refuse exactly the hostname dashboards the same-origin arm exists to keep working. Recorded as a boundary and tracked as follow-up rather than folded in, because this change is a **strict reduction**: before it every cross-origin request executed; after it, only this shape does. Found by external review, which also corrected the first draft's comment — *under rebinding the attacker page genuinely **is** that origin*, so "cannot match unless it genuinely is that origin" was not the reassurance it read as.

  **Two further honest boundaries the first draft did not state.** `GET`/`HEAD` are exempt **not** because GET is side-effect-free here — `GET /usage` and `GET /status` reach a real (haiku, `max_tokens: 1`) call to `api.anthropic.com` and can trigger an OAuth refresh — but because an `Origin` gate **cannot** defend that surface: the same effect is reachable with `<img src="…/usage">`, which sends no `Origin` at all. And the gate does not wall off a page served from an origin the allowlist *admits* — any other loopback port, or any host on the operator's LAN — which is both admitted here and echoed into `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`. That is pre-existing, but it is the honest boundary of "a page you merely visit cannot drive the proxy": a page on your LAN can.

  **Class Hybrid**, each half answered separately in ADR 0019: the gate **cannot affect any request `cli.js` would make** (`Origin` is browser-set; OCP's own audited Class A outbound call to `api.anthropic.com/v1/messages` sends `Authorization`, `anthropic-version`, `anthropic-beta`, `Content-Type` — and no `Origin`), so Rules 2 and 3 are not implicated; B.1 adds no field OpenAI's spec defines; B.2 is a **semantics change** and therefore ADR 0006 route **(b)** with its own ADR, not the grandfather clause and not ADR 0012.
### Fixed

- **A release body that exceeds GitHub's 125 000-character cap is now truncated to a block boundary plus a pointer, instead of failing the release job outright (#441).** `release.yml` piped the CHANGELOG section verbatim into `gh release create --notes-file`. v3.29.3's section is **169 670 bytes — 36 % over** — so the API answered `422 Validation Failed: body is too long` and **no Release was created**; the Releases page showed v3.29.2 as *Latest* for a day while the tag, the code and the whole fleet were correct. It stayed invisible because every mechanism that could have noticed looks elsewhere: `scripts/doctor.mjs` resolves `latest_version` from `git show origin/main:package.json` and never from Releases, so `ocp update` was unaffected and the fleet took 3.29.3 normally; the only trace was a red workflow run on a **tag ref**, which nothing routinely reads. The last observer standing was a human reading the Releases page.

  **The fix is not to shorten the record.** The section is 5× v3.29.2's 31 964 bytes because this cycle's entries carry the measurements behind each change; that convention is working, and the next cycle has no reason to be smaller. So the body is the section **byte-identical whenever it fits** — every release before this one did, and their bodies do not move — and otherwise a verbatim prefix plus a pointer to `CHANGELOG.md` at the tag.

  - **The cut snaps to a block start** (`^#{1,6} ` or a top-level list marker), and the three candidates were measured on the real section rather than argued: snapping to a **block** keeps up to byte **123 714** (1 286 unused); snapping to a **heading** falls back to `### Changed` at byte **89 829**, throwing away **33 885 bytes** for nothing; **not snapping** lands mid-sentence, and in this CHANGELOG mid-bold-run, which renders as a broken entry rather than a short one. Entries here are single enormous `- **…**` bullets, so a bullet start *is* the semantic boundary and headings are far too coarse to be the unit.
  - **The budget is counted in UTF-8 BYTES although the cap is in characters**, deliberately. A byte count is never smaller than a character count under either meaning of the word, so `bytes <= 125000` implies the body is under the cap however GitHub counts it, while the reverse does not hold. Measured on the v3.29.3 section: **169 670 bytes** against **168 661** code points and **168 661** UTF-16 units. This costs headroom, never correctness — it can refuse a body GitHub would have taken; it can never accept one GitHub will reject. It is also the one instrument that agrees with itself in `wc -c` and in `Buffer.byteLength`.
  - **The pointer links to `CHANGELOG.md` at the tag, not to an anchor inside it.** A heading anchor would require this repo to keep its own copy of GitHub's slug algorithm, with no test able to notice when that algorithm changes; a silently-wrong anchor lands the reader at the top of the file, which is where the plain link lands them without the pretence.
  - **`assertWithinLimit` is a pre-flight backstop that fails with a named, local error** rather than letting the GitHub API be the only size check, and it is **reachable**: `buildReleaseBody`'s three `minimal:*` arms do not consult `limit` at all — there is no honest body shorter than naming the release — so `--limit 10` produces a 15-byte body and the guard fires. It is pinned twice: directly as a unit, and through the real CLI, which is what pins the **call site**.
  - **Four divergences from the awk, not one, and three of them are fixes.** (1) The awk's `$0 ~ "^## " ver` is a regex with an unbounded right edge, so `v3.29.3` also matched `## v3.29.30`; the character after the version must now not continue it, and the class is `[0-9.+-]` so that `v1.0.0` does not select `## v1.0.0-rc1` either — reachable, because `release.yml` triggers on `v*`. (2) `ver` went in as a regex, so `.` was a wildcard and `v1.0.0` matched `## v1X0Y0`; `startsWith` is literal. (3) A repeated heading for one version was absorbed by the awk and terminates here — immaterial, that CHANGELOG is broken either way. (4) **The awk's `print` appends a trailing newline this does not, so its byte count is always exactly one higher.** That is why both `169 670` and `169 669` appear for the same section: the first is `wc -c` on the awk's output, which is what #441 quotes; the second is `Buffer.byteLength` on what ships. Both are correct, and every figure in the new module is the second kind unless it says otherwise. The section terminator stays `^## v` for exact fidelity — a further behaviour change on a release-critical path, in the same commit, with nothing asking for it, is not a bargain.
  - **`release_kit.release_channel` now records `tag_style: annotated`,** discharging #441's secondary observation rather than leaving it to whoever runs the next release. This writes down the convention already in force rather than inventing one: `git cat-file -t` reports `tag` for v3.28.0/v3.29.0/v3.29.1/v3.29.2 and `commit` for v3.29.3, so four of the last five are annotated and v3.29.3 is the deviation. The shared root with #441 is that `git grep 'git tag'` returns **nothing** anywhere in this repo (positive control: `git push` matches two files) — every tag is hand-typed and nothing enforces either property.
  - **`release.yml` now pins `actions/setup-node@v4` at the same version `test.yml` pins.** The release job runs `node` for the first time, and every other workflow here that runs node pins it. An unpinned one would leave a release at the mercy of a runner-image bump — a failure on the one path nobody watches, which is the whole of #441. Pinned to the node the suite proves these scripts on, so production and CI agree.

  **`release.yml`'s own `run:` body is now executed verbatim by `npm test`.** This is the part that makes the rest mean anything: a release step cannot be exercised except by cutting a tag, and a tag is not re-cuttable, so before this the *only* test of that step was the release itself — which is exactly the position #441 left the project in. The step's values now arrive through `env:` and its body contains **no `${{ }}`**, so the suite can slice it out between two anchors and run it as plain bash against a temp `$GITHUB_OUTPUT`. Everything else added here proves a helper works; this one is the only thing that can fail if `release.yml` stops calling it — the #343 call-site shape the wiring-pin ledger exists for. The anchors are asserted **by index** before the slice, per the #347 rule that `indexOf` returns `-1` and `slice(a, -1)` is neither an error nor empty.

  **Seventeen mutations: 17 landed, 17 reddened, all restores byte-identical against a file backup** (`mutated !== original` asserted per #440). Never truncating — the original bug, restored — reddens 6; every other row reddens one or two, each attributed to the test whose name carries the claim. **The whole table was re-measured against the final tree rather than carried forward**: the two reviews changed both the module and the tests, and a table measured under a superseded predicate is not evidence about what ships.

  **Two of those rows exist because independent review found the tests that were missing, and in both cases the gap was created by a sentence rather than by an oversight** — which is this repo's most expensive defect class, and it landed on the PR whose subject is guarding a release:

  - A comment asserted `assertWithinLimit` was *"unreachable through the builder"*. It is not — the `minimal:*` arms never consult `limit`. Believing it unreachable is why only the unit was tested, and **deleting the CLI's call to it left the suite green**: the headline guard of this PR had no wiring pin, the exact #343 shape the ledger in `AGENTS.md` exists for. Now pinned by a CLI run at `--limit 10`.
  - A comment asserted *"both arms are tested directly"* of the `line` and `hard` cut arms. The `line` arm had **no test at all**, and deleting its whole loop left the suite green because control fell through to `hard`, which still produced a fitting body. Review's fuzz reached that arm 1 503 times out of 52 820 truncations, so it is live, not theoretical.

  A third was a false claim in a test **name**: *"v1.0.0 must not select `## v1.0.0-rc1`"*, while the body exercised only the numeric case and the prerelease case was measurably **true** at the time. The guard was widened, the test split into two bodies so one mutation can redden one row, and the round trip is pinned — `v1.0.0-rc1` must still select its own section, because a guard may narrow and never blind. **And the same defect recurred while fixing it**: the new `line`-arm test claimed to pin *"indented continuations are not boundaries"* with a fixture of indented **plain text**, which relaxing the marker regex leaves green. Caught by its own mutation row, fixed by making the fixture indented **bullets**.

  **Recorded because it is a real gap rather than a clean table:** forgetting the footer in the budget does **not** redden the v3.29.3 regression test, only the two synthetic-limit tests — on this particular CHANGELOG the last block boundary happens to sit 1 286 bytes under the cap, so the overflow fits by luck. Review independently confirmed that reading. Separately, one mutation was a **false green that looked landed**: changing the workflow's `id: notes` to `id: notesX` changed the file, so `mutated !== original` held, while `indexOf("id: notes")` still matched the longer string — `#440`'s check catches a pattern that did not occur, not a mutation that occurred and meant nothing.

  **The other half: the failure now has a consumer.** `CLAUDE.md`'s `release_kit` gains `post_release_checks` — confirm the Release exists for the tag, *and* confirm the release job went green. Measured together on v3.29.3 they answer differently: the first passes (the release was created by hand as a recovery) while the second prints `v3.29.3 failure`. **The second is the load-bearing one**, and the first draft of that clause said the opposite — v3.29.3 is the counterexample sitting four lines beneath it, and a releaser who trusted the wrong rationale would run only check 1 and land where #441 started. Check 2 also carries its own warning to **read the output, not the exit code**: `gh run list` exits 0 while printing `failure`. And re-running a failed release job does not pick up a fix — the workflow file is read from the **tag** ref, so a corrected `release.yml` on `main` only ever reaches the next tag. `AGENTS.md` § "Release protocol" was updated in the same pass: it enumerated the release walk as a closed list of two pre-tag items and told the reader *"do not create the release manually"* — the very sentence this commit's `CLAUDE.md` names as the assumption that failed. Left as it was, the consumer added here would have been unreachable from the document a releaser actually reads.

  **Does not touch `server.mjs`** — no request handler, no response shape, no Class A/B surface, and the Class B.2 key-set snapshot is unchanged. `.github/workflows/release.yml`, `scripts/release-notes.mjs` (new), `scripts/lib/release-notes.mjs` (new), `test-features.mjs`, `CLAUDE.md`, `AGENTS.md`.

### Added

- **A stall now carries its own per-window paging evidence, and every stall is recorded — not only the ones a drain happened to be watching (#374). Observational only; no budget was widened and no verdict can change.** #374's recorded next lever was *per-window swap deltas, sampled at suite start and differenced at the stall*. **The counter changed on measurement and the reason is the finding.** The two reproduction campaigns' external 10 s `vm_stat` samplers still existed and were re-read: across **1 787 ten-second intervals** spanning campaigns that produced **zero** stalls in 45 attempts, `dSwapins` was **nonzero in 99.8 %** of intervals (p50 183 and 443 pages, max 101 727) and `dSwapouts` nonzero in 13 %/30 %. **This host swaps continuously**, so a nonzero host-swap delta across a stall window is the base state and carries no information — it would have "fired" on essentially every window in two campaigns that never stalled. The host counters are also unattributable: every other process on the box contributes to them, including the sibling suites that *are* the contention.

  **What is discriminating is this process's OWN major page faults**, sampled onto the existing 500 ms tracer tick via `process.resourceUsage()` — measured at **0.0003 ms/call** against **1.1262 ms** for a `vm_stat` spawn, so it rides the tick with no fork. Its quiet-state delta is a measured **zero** (2 s `Atomics.wait`: majflt 0), which is the property the host counters lack and the reason a *delta* is the right shape here. Each gap the tracer already computes now carries `majflt`/`invcsw`/`volcsw` bracketed by the **same two samples that define the gap**, so the window is the stall's own — not the suite's. A **periodic host-counter ring was designed and rejected**: bracketing with host samples needs a spawn every ~10 s, and a fork/exec is the single most likely operation to block under the host thrash being investigated — an instrument that stalls the loop it watches would manufacture the reading it exists to explain. `ru_nswap` (`swappedOut`) is excluded: measured 0 in every staged condition, which is BSD not populating the field, and a structurally-always-zero field would read as "no swapping" on the one capture that mattered.

  **The LEVEL was the other candidate and it is recorded but deliberately not classified on.** #374's single positive is characterised by a level — *"host swap ~96 % used"* — but macOS grows and shrinks the swap **file**, so used/total is a fraction of a **moving denominator**: sampled every 5 s for 27.6 minutes on the same host, the denominator took **three distinct values (10 240 / 11 264 / 12 288 MB)** while used% ranged **84.0–92.4 %**, and **0 of 330 samples** reached 96 %. The snapshot therefore records `swapUsedBytes` **with** `swapTotalBytes`, plus `kern.memorystatus_vm_pressure_level` and compressor occupancy (on modern macOS pressure lands in the compressor before it reaches swap), as context beside the verdict rather than as the verdict.

  **The calibration reproduced the phenomenon and corrected the instrument twice.** A solo, guarded, otherwise-green run (**1 273 passed, 0 failed**) contained **two contiguous gaps of 89 620 ms and 76 260 ms** — the exact 76–120 s `STALL-NO-CPU` shape — carrying 318 ms and 644 ms of CPU. **(1)** Both produced **zero** drain records. The reason is **structural, not statistical**: `_ltObserveDrainTimeout` is reachable from exactly six places — `ltDrain`'s two branches plus four direct calls in the controls — so the only production path to a record runs through `ltDrain`, and **a stall that does not overlap a live drain window is unrecordable by construction**. #419 found one layer of this (the `ok=true` face, hence `_ltLateDrains`); this is the same finding one layer further out. So the tick itself now ledgers **every** gap ≥ 1 200 ms with both verdicts, reported at end of run — and an empty ledger prints `NONE`, because silence that cannot be told from "nobody ran it" is what the release-kit's B.2 audit was rebuilt to avoid. What this does **not** claim: that landing outside a drain window is the *usual* case. Most measured stalls did, and at least one landed inside one and was caught by `_ltLateDrains` — but that is a handful of runs on two hosts, not a property of the phenomenon. **(2)** The fault floor was first set to 32 by reasoning from a 2 s busy block's 16 first-touch faults; the run's measured distribution (147 gaps: zero in 130, p90 1, p99 52, **max 68**) showed the two largest readings were **ordinary ~500 ms gaps at 68 and 52**, while the two *real* 76–90 s stalls measured **majflt 5 and 31**. A floor of 32 would have called a routine half-second window "faulting" and a 76-second stall "quiet" — exactly backwards. The floor is **128**: above the measured non-stall maximum, far below a genuine fault-back of this process's ~68 MB RSS (~4 150 pages at 16 KiB).

  **What the lever says on the stalls it has seen: `NO-PAGE-FAULTS`.** Every stall measured so far, on either host, reads majflt **well under the floor of 128** — the largest observed to date is 31 — at swap **86.8–90.4 %** used and pressure level 1–2. (Stated as an invariant rather than as a tally of readings: the count grows with every suite run, and a list plus a total is the same claim maintained twice. Any run's `=== [#374] loop stalls` block prints the current evidence.) That is what *excludes swap of this process* for those windows rather than confirming it, and narrows `STALL-NO-CPU`'s four candidates to descheduled, QoS-throttled, or blocked in a syscall. **That narrowing carries one unsettled caveat, recorded in the diagnosis string itself:** whether `ru_majflt` counts **compressor** decompressions on Apple Silicon is not established here, and if it does not, a window served entirely from the compressor would read quiet while still being memory-bound. The "2 GB alloc + re-touch → 0" result fits both readings, and staging real memory pressure on this host was refused. **Two other honest limits:** `SELF-PAGE-FAULTING` has **no staged control** — it is proven by the band table's mapping and by nothing else, and its diagnosis is tagged `[reasoned]`, not `[measured]`, with a test pinning that tag. And the floor's discriminating evidence (ordinary gaps reaching majflt 68/52) **rests on a single run on a single host**: independent review could not re-derive it — no ordinary gap there exceeded 2, so 32 and 128 are indistinguishable on that host. Pooled across all six stalls every reading is ≤ 31, so the floor is the right way round on both; the specific value is not two-host evidence. **It cannot be retro-fired on the 08-15 positive**: that record carries a single cumulative counter reading with no second sample to difference and no process-local counters, so neither the delta nor the level is reconstructible for it, and the "~96 %" figure was prose rather than a recorded field. The mechanism stays open; `invcsw`/`volcsw` are recorded as evidence and explicitly **not** classified on, because a name is a claim and those axes are not calibrated. **Not endpoint-touching** — `test-features.mjs` and this file only; `git diff origin/main -- server.mjs lib/ keys.mjs` is **0 lines**.

- **`node test-features.mjs --only <substring>[,<substring>...]` runs only the tests whose registered NAME contains any of the comma-separated substrings (case-sensitive; comma = OR).** The defense-cost directive's targeted test-run mode: a filtered run registers only the matching subset, so a mutation campaign or reviewer spot-check costs seconds instead of the ~4-minute full run, while the FULL suite remains the final gate (implementer final run + CI). The filter lives at the registration layer — `test` / `testAsync` / `ltTest` / `testSkipped` short-circuit a non-matching name before any counting, `pendingAsync` queueing, or ✓/✗/⊘ logging (a non-matching platform-gated skip is suppressed entirely; a matching one still records its skip) — so a run with no flag (npm test / CI) is byte-identical to today. A filter that matches NOTHING exits non-zero (exit 1) with `no tests matched --only filter: <subs>`, so a typo'd `--only` can never masquerade as a green run; the no-flag contract is untouched. Three self-checks spawn the real runner as a subprocess and assert the contract end to end — exit 0 + the exact count for a matching filter, non-zero + the message for a zero-match filter, no non-matching test name in the output, and a platform-gated test still accounted for (run or skipped) — and each is mutation-proven. **Not endpoint-touching** — `test-features.mjs`, `AGENTS.md`, and this file only; `server.mjs` / `lib/` / `keys.mjs` are 0 lines changed.

## v3.29.3 — 2026-08-16

### Fixed

- **`ocp update` now probes sibling instances' running versions and executes same-user sibling updates, and the multi-instance report no longer disappears on the real upgrade path (#327 part 6).** The outer `plan` (the `[doctor]` and `[multi-instance]` lines) was dropped on the full-upgrade and fresh-install paths — only dry-run/noop/light printed it — so a real `ocp update` on a multi-instance host reported nothing about its siblings; it is now threaded through both paths. Each sibling's `/health` `version` is probed (unless `--skip-network`) and annotated `behind`/`current`/`ahead` against the primary's `current_version`; a failed probe degrades to `version unknown (probe failed: …)`. Per the part-4 decision, the update now **executes only same-user siblings** — a user-scope sibling, or a system-scope sibling whose `User=` is the invoking user/uid (or unset and the invoking uid is root) — by spawning `./ocp update` in each sibling's own tree under an `OCP_NO_SIBLING_EXEC=1` guard so a child never re-execs *its* siblings; a cross-user sibling is still reported, never run, and a sibling's failure never fails the primary. Exec is scoped to the full-upgrade path only; noop/light/restart/dry-run keep the print-only report. **Not endpoint-touching** — `scripts/doctor.mjs` (unit records gain a `user` field), `scripts/upgrade.mjs`, and `test-features.mjs`.

- **An `unhandledRejection` now names the request that produced it (#411).** The global handler logged only the error — no method, no path — which is why four live hangs in this family went unnoticed until probing found them. The request handler is now wrapped in an `AsyncLocalStorage` (`requestContext`) carrying `{method, path}`, and the rejection handler reads it back, so the record carries `method`/`path` alongside the error. Honest limit, stated rather than lied about: a rejection that escapes before the request context is established (or before the router parsed anything) logs `method: null, path: null`. **Not endpoint-touching** — no request handler change, no response shape; `server.mjs` only.

- **A bind failure no longer leaves the proxy "up but not listening" (#412).** `server.listen()` had no `error` handler, so a failure to bind reached the global `uncaughtException` handler, which swallowed it — the process stayed alive and never listened. That is the worst of the three states: a process-liveness check passes and `Restart=` never fires, while every client fails. The test harness had already discovered this while building an unrelated test (a child given an unbindable address logs `EADDRNOTAVAIL` and stays alive) — the behaviour was known, written down, relied upon by a test's timing — and never fixed. Now a `server.on("error")` handler plus a `_listening` flag distinguishes the boot-time bind from a later error: if the server never began serving, it prints `FATAL: failed to bind …` and exits non-zero; a post-listen error is logged, not fatal. **Not endpoint-touching** — `server.mjs` + `test-features.mjs` only; no request handler, no response shape. The harness's EADDRINUSE collision-detection regex (`ltBootFresh`'s retry-on-collision, #219/#204) moved with it: the old regex matched the `uncaught_exception` log line, the new one matches the FATAL.

- **`npm test` no longer writes to the operator's real `~/.claude.json` (#396).** A TUI-mode boot with `HOME` left ambient resolved `resolveTuiHome` to the operator's real home and `ensureTuiCwdTrusted` **read-modify-renamed** their live Claude Code config. The harm is not only the added trust entry: a write made by the operator's own Claude session between our read and our rename was **lost**. #384's refusing `tmux` stub could not stop it — `bootTuiPane` does its filesystem work *before* the `new-session` it then refuses.
  - **`ltBoot` now pins `HOME` in its base env**, after the `...env` spread, alongside #384's `OCP_TUI_TMUX_BIN`. Re-derived against `origin/main`: of the **six** hand-rolled `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE: "true"` boot sites, **six** pinned no `HOME`; the **nine** that go through a shared fixture — eight via `ltTuiTmux`, one via `makeB2Fixture`'s `tui-pool` profile in `scripts/b2-key-snapshot.mjs` — all did. Every site that remembers is a shared fixture and every site that forgets is hand-rolled, which is `AGENTS.md`'s *constraints must be unreachable by construction* with the count attached. **This corrects a seven/seven-and-eight/eight count that shipped in the first version of this entry**: the sixth `grep` hit was inside `ltTuiTmux`'s own `env` object — the fixture, not a call site — and the ninth pinning site lives in another file the grep could not see.
  - **`HOME`, not `OCP_TUI_HOME`** — deliberately. Pinning `OCP_TUI_HOME` takes `resolveTuiHome`'s first branch and, with no env token, lands in `prepareTuiHome`'s **legacy** mode, which reaches into the real home twice: it **symlinks the real `~/.claude/.credentials.json`** *and* seeds the scratch config by **reading the real `~/.claude.json`**. That would trade a config rewrite for a credential symlink plus a copy of the operator's config. Both legs are already executed by the suite (`prepareTuiHome scratch mode: symlinks creds…` and `resolveTuiHome: explicit OCP_TUI_HOME wins…`), so this is measured, not read. Pinning `HOME` makes `realHome` itself scratch, so every branch lands inside the scratch tree — and it also redirects `TUI_CWD`, `TUI_STREAM_DIR`, `SPAWN_HOME_DIR`, the openclaw log path and every `homedir()` call. `scripts/b2-key-snapshot.mjs` already pins `HOME` for a **neighbouring** reason — its stated purpose is `/logs` snapshot determinism, which is about *reading* the operator's files rather than *writing* to their home.
  - **The containment boundary is the harness temp root, not the test's own `dir`** — the one place #384's pattern must not be copied verbatim. The nine sites that already pin `HOME` use a **sibling** of `dir`, not a child; a dir-scoped guard would have rejected all nine, measured. Containment is **strict**: unlike #384's tmux guard, `HOME` == the temp root itself is refused too, because `$TMPDIR` is shared by every concurrent test and by the b2 fixture.

### Added

- **The expiry gate on cached credentials is now pinned — `makeResolveSpawnToken` extracted to `lib/spawn-token.mjs` (#343).** `resolveSpawnToken`'s re-application of `isTokenExpiring` to CACHED creds was the one correctness wire the #343 sweep found unpinned: `server.mjs` cannot be imported by the suite (it calls `listen()` at top level), so a mutation deleting the gate left the suite green while silently resurrecting the #146 forever-stale-token shape the whole F5 design exists to prevent. The gate logic is now a pure `makeResolveSpawnToken({ isExpiring, now })` factory with an injectable `isExpiring` and a fixed `now`; `server.mjs` delegates through a module-scope instance built with NO injection, so production behaviour is byte-for-byte identical. A fixed-`now` contrast test (4 min to expiry → null, 6 min → the token) plus a missing-token case and an injection-seam test pin the gate, and are mutation-proven: deleting the gate line reddens exactly the within-buffer and default-edge tests while the 6-min / missing-token / override-seam cases stay green. **Not endpoint-touching** — `server.mjs` only re-delegates; no request handler, no response shape.

- **The teardown drain now asks the KERNEL why it is waiting, and traces the loop that could not answer (#374). Observational only — no budget was widened, and no verdict can change.** #374 recorded drains that gave up after **75 514 ms** and **111 203 ms** against a 5 000 ms budget, and everything concluded from them was **elimination**: hypotheses 2 and 3 are refuted by measurement in that thread, leaving parent-side starvation as the last candidate standing rather than as something anybody watched happen.

  **The instrument the issue was filed with cannot confirm its own hypothesis**, which two independent cross-model reviews found separately. Recording `buf.exit` alongside the timeout reads the `'exit'` **event**, and `'exit'` is itself a parent-loop callback: under exactly the condition being tested it is queued behind the same stall, so `buf.exit` reads `undefined` at record time even when the child died a minute earlier — *"child still alive"*, the opposite of the truth. The instrument is subject to the phenomenon it measures. Ground truth is therefore `spawnSync("ps", ["-o", "state=,etime=,comm=", "-p", pid])`, which needs no event loop, over a registry of children that have not yet emitted `'close'` (`ltDrain`'s condition is a *count*, so today it cannot name what it is waiting on). `buf.exit` is still recorded, labelled loop-dependent, so the divergence is visible rather than inferred — and a control test **asserts both halves at once**, `ZOMBIE-UNREAPED` from the kernel against `exit === undefined` from the loop, in the same record.

  **Two things measured here rather than assumed.** `process.kill(pid, 0)` **succeeds on a zombie**, so it cannot answer "is the child gone" — the one case that has to be nameable. And `ps` output is **column-padded**: a real zombie renders as `"Z    00:01"`, so reading the column as fixed width and comparing `=== "Z"` is *false on a real zombie*, while being true on unpadded Linux `procps` — right on CI, wrong on the workstation. The probe splits on whitespace and prefix-tests, because the state is also decorated (`Z+`, `SN`, `S+`).

  **A stall tracer** — a self-rescheduling 500 ms tick appending `{wall, cpu}` to a 256-slot ring — attaches the largest inter-tick gap and the CPU consumed across it. That separates three causes "starvation" conflates and which have different fixes: a gap with ~no CPU (the process was not executing), a gap carrying CPU the size of itself (it was executing something synchronous — a suite bug, and findable), and no gap (the loop kept ticking through the window). Seven controls calibrate it against staged faults before any field capture is trusted, and the calibration immediately found THREE defects in the instrument itself: the tracer is a **macrotask**, so two staged blocks separated only by microtasks measured as *one* 4 238 ms gap; and the CPU band, set from a standalone measurement where a busy loop costs 0.999 of its wall time, was wrong under load, where the identical block measured **1 545 ms of CPU across a 2 220 ms gap (0.696)** because the host descheduled it for ~450 ms; and a comment asserting a classification the constants no longer produced, caught by the band table on its first run after the bound moved.

  **The phenomenon was then reproduced deliberately rather than waited for**, with two concurrent full suites. At the exact site #374 names, elapsed went from **41 ms solo** (re-measured with host conditions recorded; an earlier uncontrolled solo run read 893 ms) to **120 173 / 119 960 / 120 808 ms** concurrent, while every other drain in the same runs measured between **0 ms and 43 ms** — one outlier and eighty-five neighbours, not a tail. Staggering the two suites by 180 s moved it back to **41 / 42 ms** at *double* the load average. **What that contrast does not establish is the mechanism**: independent review reproduced the same phenomenon with **no second suite at all** (83 836 ms, one contiguous 82 666 ms gap), so phase alignment between two suites cannot be the variable, and this entry claims only the contrast it measured. The tracer's answer at the captured event: **one contiguous gap of 120 808 ms carrying 635 ms of CPU**, a 0.5 % duty cycle. That **confirms the falsifiable prediction** the reviews put on the record — the arithmetic says such an elapsed time requires a *single* contiguous stall rather than accumulated lateness, and the loop turned out to have completed exactly one iteration: one condition check, one 40 ms `await` that took 120.8 seconds.

  **It also exposed a blind spot in the plan, which is why a second ledger exists.** All three reproductions **succeeded** (`ok=true`) — by the time the loop came back the child had closed — so a drain that overran its budget by **24×** recorded nothing, moved no assertion, and would have been watched in silence by an instrument that only fires on a timeout. #374's two field reports are the `ok=false` face of the same event; on the host where this reproduced, the `ok=true` face is the more common one. Late-but-successful drains are recorded in `_ltLateDrains`, deliberately **not** folded into `_ltDrainTimeouts`: that ledger is what the #358 summary reads to decide whether the peak is attributable, and a drain that succeeded leaves it attributable. Conflating them would change a verdict.

  **One reading in this instrument was wrong and is corrected here rather than quietly reworded.** Its first revision claimed a `REAPED-GONE` probe result could be a stall rather than a pipe-holder, reasoning that libuv delivers a child's exit in the poll phase while the wait loop resumes in timers, so a stall ending by rescheduling reaps the child first. The premise is measured and true; the conclusion does not follow, and independent review measured why: in that case `'close'` fires in the **same turn** as `'exit'`, because the child's death delivers both stdio EOFs in that same poll phase — **turns observed with the exit delivered but `closed=false`: zero**, over 3 + 2 staged runs. `'close'` is what removes the child from the registry, so that world leaves an **empty registry**, not a `REAPED-GONE` row, and #374's original reading was right all along. The author's own field capture had agreed with the reviewer and was not noticed. The consequence is that the emptied-registry outcome — the one the capture actually produced, which printed as *"nothing to probe"* and read as an absence of information when it is the most specific of the outcomes — **is now named**, and split by whether anything was open when the drain started: `CLOSED-BEFORE-PROBE` positively excludes both the zombie and the pipe-holder worlds, while `NO-CHILD-WAS-OPEN` is the one case that really is an absence of information.

  **Not endpoint-touching**, verified rather than asserted: `git diff origin/main -- server.mjs lib/ keys.mjs` is **0 lines**. Test harness only, no user-visible change.

- **`OCP_TUI_TOOLS` — narrow the TUI pane's built-in tool set via `claude --tools` (opt-in, #190).** Design, code and the rationale comment are **@sumlin**'s, contributed on their `feat/tui-configurable-tools` branch (`fc71749`) and re-created here under `Co-Authored-By`. **Correction (2026-08-11): the stated reason for re-creating rather than merging was wrong, and this entry is corrected rather than quietly reworded — see the ADR 0017 entry below for the mechanism.** It said this repository's public history had been recreated as a squashed root on 2026-07-27, so `git merge-base origin/main fc71749` was **empty** and no merge could be computed. Measured in a full clone after `git fetch --unshallow`: `git merge-base origin/main fc71749` returns **`bdb6662`** — v3.24.0, exactly where the branch says it was opened — and the repository has **one** root, `593d0dc`. **There is one history and no recreation.** The true statement is the boring one: three weeks of movement produced ordinary **content conflicts**, which a rebase would have resolved. The re-creation was not necessary. This was the project maintainer's error, not a tool's. @sumlin's design, code and rationale comment are theirs; corrections have been posted on #190, and reopening remains available if that record is preferred.

  Set it to a comma-separated list of built-in tool names (e.g. `Read,Glob,Grep,WebSearch,WebFetch`) and the value reaches the interactive pane's `claude --tools`. **Unset or whitespace-only leaves the argv byte-for-byte as it is today** — every built-in tool available, MCP walled off — so the default path does not move. (The original wording said "comma- **or space**-separated"; `claude --help`, verified on 2.1.220, documents only the comma form — `"Bash,Edit,Read"` — so the docs were narrowed to what it actually says. No behaviour change.)

  **Why `--tools` and not `--disallowedTools`.** `--tools` is the tool-*availability* registry (which built-ins exist for the session), not a permission layer. A tool that is never offered can never raise an interactive permission prompt — and a headless tmux pane has nobody to answer one, so such a prompt hangs the turn to the wallclock cap and bricks the pane. That is the same failure that already rules out `--dangerously-skip-permissions` for TUI, which is what makes the availability registry the right lever rather than merely a convenient one.

  Scoped to the default MCP-walled surface: it is **not** read under `OCP_TUI_FULL_TOOLS=1`, whose tool surface is `CLAUDE_ALLOWED_TOOLS` (or, when that is unset, its hardcoded default set). It narrows what the pane's model is **offered** and is explicitly **not a trust boundary** — everything the pane runs still runs as the OCP host user; ADR 0007 lists `--tools ""` as only *one of three* requirements for the deferred multi-tenant B-path. Whitespace-only is treated as unset deliberately — `--tools ""` means *disable every tool* to `claude`, and a stray-empty env var must not silently do that.

  **Not endpoint-touching.** `lib/tui/session.mjs` only assembles the argv for the locally spawned interactive `claude`; no request handler, no response shape, no `server.mjs`. `docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json` is unchanged and the in-suite key-set diff stayed green.

  **Pinned behaviourally, not textually.** The four new tests point `claudeBin` at a script that prints its own argv, run the whole pane command through a real `/bin/sh`, and assert on the argv the shell actually delivered — the only way to tell `--tools 'a b'` from `--tools a b`, which a substring check on the command string cannot. The two `shq` claims ("one argv element", "cannot break the shell string") live in **separate** tests on purpose: one mutation breaks both, and the metacharacter value makes `sh` fail to parse, so had they shared a test whichever ran first would have aborted the other and only one would ever have been shown able to fail.

### Removed

- **`stats.sessionHits` and `stats.sessionMisses` — the last two elements of the dead session surface — authorized by [ADR 0016](docs/adr/0016-removing-dead-session-surface.md) **Amendment 1**, NOT by ADR 0012 (#355 follow-up).** ADR 0012 authorizes *additions* only and has never covered a removal, which is the whole reason ADR 0016 had to establish a removal route in the first place. Amendment 1 supplies the authorization the entry below correctly refused to assume, on the same terms as everything ADR 0016 already enumerates. **Regenerating the snapshot is not authorization; the amendment is.**

  **Why an amendment and not a new ADR.** These two meet ADR 0016's inertness bar and its consumer bar identically to everything already listed; what they lacked was a line in the enumeration. The omission has a reusable mechanism behind it, so it is recorded rather than quietly patched: ADR 0016's Context **quotes the B.2 snapshot's `notCovered` entry for `sessions[]` and cuts it mid-sentence**, at *"…with 0 occurrences of `sessions.set(`"* — truncating the very enumeration it was quoting (which continues *"and 0 of `sessions.get(`, only `.delete` and `.clear`"*). Measured from that stopping point: **50 characters** to *"The array is unreachable, not under-probed."*, and **94 characters and two sentences** to the one naming `/status.activeSessions` **and** `stats.sessionHits`/`sessionMisses`. `/status.activeSessions` reached the Context table and the other two did not, and the Decision list, the consumer sweep and the snapshot expectations all inherited the truncation — each verifying as internally consistent, because they were consistent with *each other*. A governance document is not a different kind of artifact from a code comment: it asserts things about the code, and it can assert more than it checked.

  **Inertness (ADR 0016 clause (a)), re-derived rather than inherited.** Established by enumerating **every** `stats` reference in `server.mjs`, not by grepping for the two names — a name grep cannot rule out a computed key. Every write targets a named property (`stats.totalRequests++`, `stats.oneOffRequests++`, `stats.activeRequests++`/`--`, `stats.errors++`, `stats.timeouts++`, `stats.queueRejections++`, `stats.queued = …`); the file contains **no** `stats[…]` bracket access, **no** `...stats` spread target, **no** `Object.assign` onto it and **no** alias binding. **And their write sites were deleted directly, not orphaned indirectly**: `git show 885f62a` (PR #103) removes `stats.sessionHits++` and `stats.sessionMisses++` *in the same hunk* as the only `sessions.set(...)`.

  **Consumers (clause (b)), from the wire and the repo.** On the wire they were recorded under the **`GET /health`** record of **both profile blocks** (`probes` and `probesTuiPool`) — that is *one endpoint recorded twice, not two endpoints*: `/health` is the only response carrying them, because `stats` reaches the wire through a single bare `stats,` shorthand, while `/status` and `/usage` name four fields each and never included these. In the repo, **no consumer *names* either field under any spelling** — `ocp`, `ocp-connect`, `ocp-plugin/index.js`, `dashboard.html`, `README.md`, `scripts/doctor.mjs`, `scripts/upgrade.mjs`, `test-features.mjs` — and every *named* `/health` consumer reads a fixed field list. **Two consumers took them generically, without ever mentioning them**, which is the half a name grep cannot reach and the reason clause (b) says "from the wire and the repo rather than *from imports*": `ocp health` (`ocp:369-377`) pipes the whole `/health` body through `python3 -m json.tool` and so **printed both fields to the operator** (format-agnostic; no update needed), and `scripts/b2-key-snapshot.mjs` walks whatever key paths a response carries (updated here, per clause (c)). `ocp health` was missed by this entry's first pass and found by independent review — the amendment's own thesis recurring inside its own evidence.

  **B.2 key-set snapshot — 2 key paths removed per block, in both blocks; nothing added, no other record moved.** `GET /health` −`stats.sessionHits`, −`stats.sessionMisses`. Four deleted lines total. **No `ALIGNMENT.md` inventory row and no probe-plan entry is added or removed** — these are response keys on an endpoint that remains, not endpoints — but the `/health` row's *Authorizing ADR* cell now records `session-surface removals per ADR 0016 (+ Amendment 1)`, following the convention `/api/keys` and `/cache/stats` already use. Without it the Annual Alignment Audit (`ALIGNMENT.md:168`) asks whether a grandfathered endpoint still matches its v3.16.4 snapshot, and `/health` no longer does with nothing pointing at why. The snapshot's `notCovered` entry for `sessions[]` is **appended to, not rewritten**, because its existing text records what #395 measured.

  **Pinned behaviourally, with a measured liveness premise.** Two new tests read the two key paths off a **live** `/health` — one per profile the snapshot records, in separate `test()` bodies because one mutation breaks the same claim in both. Each proves the probe saw a real `stats` block (present, object, ≥5 keys, `totalRequests` among them) *before* claiming these two are absent. That premise is load-bearing by measurement, not by argument. Control **1200 passed / 0 failed**, measured on the PR tree (branch base `2593eb1` *plus* this branch). The base was **run, not subtracted** — a detached worktree at `2593eb1` gives **1198 / 0**, so the `+2` is these two registrations and a reader reproducing the control from the base will correctly find 1198. Against that control: re-adding `sessionHits: 0` → **1197/3**; re-adding `sessionMisses: 0` → **1197/3** (proving the second assertion in each body actually executes); deleting `stats,` from `/health` → **1196/4**, both tests failing *at the premise*. Running that last mutation against a premise-stripped variant using `body.stats ?? {}` gives **1198/2 with neither test among the failures** — the vacuous pass the premise converts into a failure.

- **The dead session-tracking surface, in full — authorized by [ADR 0016](docs/adr/0016-removing-dead-session-surface.md), NOT by ADR 0012 (#355).** ADR 0012 authorizes *additions* only; a removed key path has never had an authorizing route, so ADR 0016 both authorizes this change and establishes how grandfathered Class B.2 surface may be removed at all: its own ADR, an inertness demonstration, every consumer enumerated from the wire and the repo, and all of them updated in the same change.

  **Inertness, verified for this PR rather than inherited.** The current tree has **zero** `sessions.set(` and **zero** `sessions.get(` — the Map is only iterated, `.delete`d, `.clear`ed and `.size`d, so no code path can put anything in it. Confirmed against GitHub rather than local history, because this checkout is a **shallow clone** and `git log -S ... -- server.mjs` returns nothing for a pre-boundary removal: PR **#103** (merge commit `885f62a`, merged 2026-05-30) deleted both the only `sessions.set(sessionKey, …)` and its paired `sessions.get(sessionKey)`. **Correction (2026-08-11), appended rather than reworded because the observation was real and only its explanation was wrong:** the pickaxe returning nothing was a **shallow-clone artifact of that working tree**, not a property of this repository — the same false premise the `OCP_TUI_TOOLS` entry above corrects, per the mechanism ADR 0017 records. Measured after `git fetch --unshallow`: the repository has **one** root (`593d0dc`), `git merge-base v3.16.4 origin/main` returns **`9e25160`** (exit 0), and `git log -S'sessions.set(' -- server.mjs` names **`885f62a`** directly. The conclusion above is unchanged and is now reproducible locally; only the reason for going to the GitHub API was wrong. Then measured on a live server — six requests carrying every spelling `server.mjs` accepts (`session_id`, `conversation_id`, `x-session-id`, `x-conversation-id`, streaming and non-streaming, one id repeated across two turns) all returned 200 and moved `stats.totalRequests` to 6, while `/sessions`, `/health.sessions`, `/status.activeSessions` and `DELETE /sessions` all stayed empty/zero. Twelve releases shipped it.

  Removed: `GET /sessions` and `DELETE /sessions`; `/health`'s `sessions` and `config.sessionTTL`; `/status`'s `proxy.activeSessions`; `/settings`' `sessionTTL` (**readable *and* writable**); the `CLAUDE_SESSION_TTL` env var; the `sessions` Map, its 60s TTL reaper and `_sessionKey()`; the `Sessions: TTL=` boot banner line; `ocp sessions` / `ocp clear`; the plugin's `/ocp sessions` / `/ocp clear`; and the dashboard's "Sessions" card.

  **`sessionTTL` was an accepted REQUEST field, so its removal is rejected explicitly rather than ignored** — ADR 0016 asks that a caller learn rather than guess. Removing it from `SETTINGS_SCHEMA` *is* the rejection: `PATCH /settings {"sessionTTL": N}` now takes the generic unknown-key path and answers **400** with `unknown setting: sessionTTL`, exactly as any other unknown key always has (**207** when mixed with a valid key, which still applies). Deliberately no bespoke branch — a second way of saying no is a second thing to keep true.

  **`CLAUDE_SESSION_TTL` is now inert, not fatal.** An operator's env file or launchd plist may still export it after upgrading; the server boots normally and ignores it. Pinned by a test that boots with it set.

  **B.2 key-set snapshot — 2 endpoint records and 18 key paths removed per block, in both blocks; nothing added.** Regenerating is not authorization; ADR 0016 is. `GET /sessions` and `DELETE /sessions` records removed; `GET /health` −`sessions`, −`sessions[]`, −`config.sessionTTL`; `GET /status` −`proxy.activeSessions`; `GET /settings` −`sessionTTL{,.desc,.max,.min,.type,.unit,.value}`; `PATCH /settings` −`current.sessionTTL{,.desc,.max,.min,.type,.unit,.value}`. Note this is **wider than ADR 0016's Consequences bullet predicted** (it named `sessions`, `sessions[]`, `proxy.activeSessions` and the two records): the 14 `sessionTTL`-derived paths on `/health.config`, `/settings` and `PATCH /settings` are entailed by the same ADR's Decision to remove "the `/settings` field, the env var", since nothing is left to report. `ALIGNMENT.md`'s inventory loses the `/sessions` row and the probe plan loses both probes, in this change, so the suite's inventory cross-check stays honest.

  **Deliberately NOT removed: `stats.sessionHits` and `stats.sessionMisses`.** Both are permanently `0` and have no writer, and issue #355's option 2 lists them — but **ADR 0016 does not enumerate them anywhere**: not in its measured Context table, not in its Decision, not in its Consequences. ADR 0016's own rule is that a B.2 element may be removed only by an ADR that demonstrates *that element* inert and enumerates *its* consumers, and its "the precedent is deliberately narrow" clause forbids reading it as a general licence. Removing them here would be an unauthorized removal of grandfathered surface — the exact failure this governance exists to prevent, run in reverse. They need a one-line ADR 0016 amendment or a follow-up ADR; flagged for the maintainer. **Resolved in this same unreleased cycle, and this paragraph is kept rather than deleted because it records why the restraint was correct** — ADR 0016 Amendment 1 supplied the authorization and the two fields are now removed; see the entry above. The narrowness clause blocked its own author's intent, which is exactly what it is for.

  Also left alone, because it is live rather than dead: `conversationId` itself. It still gates response caching (`server.mjs`: `CACHE_TTL > 0 && !conversationId`) and still appears in per-request logs. Only the Map it used to index is gone. `plan.currentSession` (Anthropic's 5-hour plan window) is unrelated to all of this and untouched.

### Fixed

- **A bare, credential-free `GET /api/usage?hours=-2400000000` or `?limit=-100000000000000000000` held a connection open forever — the FINITE class #398's guard structurally cannot see (#400).** Both are **pre-existing**: they hang identically at v3.16.4, on `main`, and on #398's branch. Measured before the fix, loopback, default `CLAUDE_AUTH_MODE=none`: **0 bytes received, socket still open at the deadline**, one `{"event":"unhandled_rejection"}` per request. `Math.min(x, cap)` bounds only the **high** side, so on both parameters it is the **negative** direction that survives the clamp and is refused by the sink itself — `new Date(...)` leaving `Date`'s ±8.64e15 ms range for `hours`, an out-of-int64 SQLite bind for `limit`. Class **B.2**, `/api/usage`, `ALIGNMENT.md:132`, authorized by **ADR 0006 (grandfathered as of v3.16.4)** — **route (a)**, on the reasoning ADR 0017 records at `:46`: a request that receives no response at all is not a behaviour anyone can be relying on. Re-checked rather than inherited: `git show $(git rev-list -n1 v3.16.4):keys.mjs` gives both sinks byte-identical to the pre-fix code, so the hang is *inside* what ADR 0006 froze. **No B.2 response key-set change** — `docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json` is unchanged and ADR 0012 is not engaged.

  **The guard is at the SINK, and is deliberately not duplicated as a handler pre-check.** That is the completeness argument, not a preference: for `hours` the boundary **moves with the clock**, so a pre-check evaluates `Date.now()` at a different instant than the sink does and an input can pass it and still throw. Guarding on the authoritative operation makes the changed-input set equal the throw set **by construction, at every instant** — strictly stronger than #398's enumeration. Two independent cross-vendor reviews of #400 derived this placement separately and then disagreed about the disposition; the maintainer's decision on #400 is what this implements.

  **`hours` is a CHECK and is exact; `limit` is a CATCH and is only narrow — stated rather than blurred.** `Date.prototype.toISOString` throws `RangeError` if and only if the time value is NaN (ECMA-262), so testing `Number.isNaN(d.getTime())` **on the same `Date` object that is then formatted** is that condition exactly, with no error matching and nothing that can be swallowed. `limit`'s failure surfaces from the SQLite bind, so it is a `catch` that rethrows unless **two structured properties** match — `err.code === "ERR_SQLITE_ERROR"` **and** `err.errcode === 20` (`SQLITE_MISMATCH`) — never the message text. [measured, node v26.5.0, 22 values through a real `LIMIT ?` bind] out-of-domain binds give `errcode 20` while a store fault on the same statement gives `errcode 1`; `2^53`, `-2^53` and `±(2^53+2)` are **accepted**, so `Number.isSafeInteger` is too strict by a hair and would change requests that are answered today. [**corrected in review, and the correction is the point**] The first version of this justification said `SQLITE_MISMATCH`'s other sources are rowid/type-affinity conflicts on *writes*. **That is false on the read path** — it is also emitted by `OP_MustBeInt`, which SQLite uses for `LIMIT`/`OFFSET`, and review constructed `errcode 20` **on this exact statement with a valid caller `limit = 50`** by making `usage_log` a view with a non-integer inner `LIMIT` (three variants reproduced). **The structure is what bounds the damage:** in every variant the absorbed fault re-raised from the retry *outside* the `try` and reached the caller, so the residual harm is exactly **one misleading log line**, never default data in place of a fault; and it is unreachable as shipped because `usage_log` is a real `TABLE` with no inner `LIMIT` — a property of the **SQL**, which is where the "re-derive this predicate if the query gains another `MustBeInt` site" warning now lives. The claim was findable **because** it was tagged `[reasoned, NOT measured]` and named the weakest link. Two structural properties bound it and both are tested: the `try` wraps **only** `stmt.all(limit)`, so `getDb()`/`d.prepare()` failures cannot be absorbed, and the substituted retry is **also outside** the `try`, so a genuinely broken store throws instead of returning 50 rows of default data.

  **The disposition is the documented default (24 h / 50 rows), and it is LOGGED.** Not a 400 — #403 measured that **no** 400 in `server.mjs` is URL-triggered and deliberately declined to invent that rejection kind. Not a clamp of `limit` to `-1` — that makes SQLite's negative-`LIMIT` rule load-bearing, and the guard has to keep working whatever that rule is. The stated cost is that an operator sending `?limit=-1e20` **silently gets 50 rows** instead of being told the parameter was unusable; the mitigation is one `{"event":"usage_param_substituted","param":…,"supplied":…,"substituted":…}` line per substitution, in the same envelope `logEvent` emits so `/logs` parses it identically. The value is unusable and the operator cannot see that from the response either way, so the record is where it belongs.

  **Deliberately NOT fixed, because it is route (b):** `getUsageTimeline` builds `since` with `.toISOString()` while `created_at` is stored SQLite-style, and `' ' (0x20) < 'T' (0x54)`, so a **same-day** `since` excludes today's rows. `keys.mjs` already defines `sqliteDatetime` for exactly this mismatch and that function still does not use it. Fixing it changes **which rows an answered request returns** — a semantics change on a grandfathered endpoint (`ALIGNMENT.md:114`, ADR 0006:39) needing its own ADR, with ADR 0010 as the worked precedent; it should also cover `getUsageByKey({since, until})`, which feeds raw client strings into the same comparison. Issue #400 carries the measurement.
