  **The `server.mjs` hunk is comment-only** — `usageQueryInt`'s "WHAT THIS DOES NOT FIX" block asserted both of these inputs still hang, which is no longer true. `git diff -U0 server.mjs` contains no non-`//` line.

  **Pinned behaviourally, on the clock, and the B.2 snapshot is NOT the evidence.** `lt400Get` asserts a response **arrived within a 15 s deadline** *before* asserting its status, so reverting a guard fails on the deadline arm rather than as a status mismatch — the defect is silence, and a status assertion alone would misreport it. The substitution is additionally asserted **on the log**, because that is the only thing on the wire that distinguishes "the default was substituted" from "the request happened to be answered". Control **1208 passed / 0 failed / 2 skipped**, measured on the PR tree; the diff adds **3** test registrations and removes none (`git diff -U0 test-features.mjs | grep -cE '^\+(ltTest|test)\("'`), so a reader reproducing the control from `origin/main` will find **1205**. **Route (a) does not depend on any of that**, which review established more sharply than this PR first did: the `limit` guard fires only where `stmt.all(limit)` **threw**, and anything that threw was **unanswered** — so the authorization holds whether or not the discrimination is exact, and refining the predicate later is an engineering change rather than a new authorization request. Twelve mutations, each reddening a **named** test at a **named** assertion, all taken under a filesystem mutex shared with a second suite running concurrently on the host and all scanned for `UNPROVEN` / `[#358]` / drain-timeout markers (none carried any): reverting either guard fails on the **deadline** arm rather than as a status mismatch (1207/1, 1206/2); substituting the cap instead of the default fails on the **value** arm (1207/1, 1206/2); deleting the log fails on the log arm (1206/2); widening either guard by one plausible step — `hours < 0`, `Number.isSafeInteger` — fails **preservation** (1206/2, 1207/1); nesting the retry inside the catch, blanketing the catch, swallowing a `prepare` failure or wrapping the timeline query each fail the store-fault test (1207/1 each); and dropping the discarded value from the log line fails the payload arm (1207/1). Which assertions the table proves and which ride on another row is enumerated in the PR body rather than glossed — an earlier draft claimed "no claim rides on another's mutation", which asserted more than the table showed.

  **One arm was measured UNABLE TO FAIL, and replaced — recorded because the mechanism generalises.** The store-fault test first asserted only that the fault *propagates* (`threw`, `errcode`), and claimed that tested the `errcode === 20` discrimination. It did not: blanket the catch and the suite scored **1208 / 0**. The induced fault is deterministic, so a blanket catch absorbs it and the substituted retry — deliberately outside the `try` — raises the **identical** error; every field that arm read is byte-identical under both implementations. The **log** is not: a blanket catch records a substitution for a `limit` the sink accepts, blaming a valid input for a database fault in the operator's log. The test now captures the sink's `console.error` in the child and asserts that count is **0**; measured at the sink, pristine → 0 and the blanket-catch mutation → 1. So the log line is not only the mitigation for the silent-substitution cost — **it is the only channel on which the predicate is observable at all.** The snapshot's greenness is necessary but **not** evidence here: it records only the localhost **success** path of each endpoint+method pair and its own `notCovered` block excludes validation and error responses, so it says the response *keys* did not move and nothing about whether a request is answered.

- **The `#366 review C` harness self-check can no longer pass without exercising its own subject, and `testSkipped()` misuse is now caught on every runner path (#402).** Two holes in the same mechanism, both in the harness's accounting. **Not endpoint-touching**: `git diff origin/main -- server.mjs lib/` is **0 lines**; `test-features.mjs` and this file only. No request handler, no response shape, no B.2 key-set change, so no ADR citation is owed.
  - **Hole 1 — a green self-check that proved nothing.** `#366 review C`'s async fixture skips via `skipRemainingTest()`, so it must settle through `test()`'s **rejection** handler; that path is the whole subject. Had the fixture called the top-level `testSkipped()` instead, the body would have returned normally, settled through the **resolve** handler, incremented `passed` and printed `✓` — and all four assertions would still have held, because not one of them could see a pass. **Fixed with `passedNames`, the exact symmetric counterpart of #401's `failedNames`**: every `passed++` routes through `recordPass(name)`, the self-check asserts `passedNames.length === passed` before asking the negative, and then asks whether *its own* name is absent. Order is load-bearing and unchanged from #401's: registration, then ran-and-skipped, then the `await`, then the completeness premise, then the negative.
  - **There were FOUR `passed++` sites, not three.** The fourth belonged to `asyncTest` — a verbatim copy of `testAsync()` minus its `TEST_SKIPPED` arm, sitting mid-file for the singleflight block. An incomplete ledger turns the negative into a negative over an incomplete list, which is the failure `failedNames` already documents. Wiring a duplicate runner up to the ledger would have created a fourth place for them to drift apart, so it is now `const asyncTest = testAsync` — behaviour unchanged for all **33** call sites, none of which throws the sentinel or calls `testSkipped()`.
  - **Hole 2 — the twin: `testSkipped()` misuse inside an async body was undetected.** `test()`'s **sync** path caught it by comparing the skip counter across the body; the async resolve handler had no counterpart and neither did `testAsync()`. **Measured before anything was written: 2 of 2 async forms (before-await and after-await) were counted as skipped AND as passed**, the exact pair the facility exists to prevent, while the sync control was caught.
  - **The sync guard was NOT copied to the async path, and that was re-measured rather than inherited from the comment warning against it.** A resolve handler comparing `skipped` against a pre-body snapshot **false-accuses an innocent async test** when any unrelated skip lands during its `await` — reproduced in isolation. The fix is per-invocation attribution via `AsyncLocalStorage` (`node:async_hooks`, builtin, stable since Node 16; this repo's `engines.node` is `>=22.5`), which caught **exactly** the three misuse cases and flagged none of the four legitimate ones, including an unrelated skip landing mid-flight. `skipRemainingTest()`'s exemption is **structural** — both skip helpers funnel through an internal `_recordSkip()` and only the public `testSkipped()` consults the context — rather than a flag set and cleared around a call that throws.
  - **The sync guard was REPLACED, not joined.** Keeping both would leave the context check permanently unprovable on the sync path: the narrow guard fires first, so every mutation aimed at the broad one dies before reaching it and the table records a kill belonging to the other assertion. The sync path therefore gets its own named regression test rather than riding on the guard that used to cover it.
  - **The runner is now a factory (`createHarness`), which is what makes any of this testable.** One of the verdicts under test *is* a failure, so driving it against the live suite would make `npm test` exit 1 on a healthy tree; a miniature re-implementation inside the test is the shape `AGENTS.md` forbids, because it passes while the real runner is broken. A second **instance** of the same code is the real thing with its own ledger. `log` is injectable for the same reason — `.github/workflows/flake-hunt.yml:179` histograms stdout `✗` lines with `grep -h '✗' logs/*.log`, so a deliberately-failing fixture would read there as a flake on a clean run. The self-checks collect the lines and assert on them, which is how "the message stays accurate on all three paths" is proven rather than claimed.
  - **The swallowed abort — found by independent review of this PR, and closed rather than documented (#415 F1).** A body that calls `skipRemainingTest()` and **catches** its abort returns normally, reaching the runner's normal-completion path with a skip already counted. As first pushed this branch counted that as **passed** — skipped *and* passed, the exact pair the facility exists to prevent — on **all three** runners, and `origin/main`'s counter comparison had caught it. Measured on the real sliced harness: `origin/main` → `failed=["swallower"]`; this branch as first pushed → `passed=["swallower"]`; with the fix → `failed=["swallower"]`. So "the broader check implies the narrower one" was **false in this corner**: the context check only ever looked for *misuse*, and reaching normal completion with a skip recorded was the missing half. `_recordSkip` — the single funnel both helpers reach — now also records the skip against the running body, and all three runners report a body that recorded a skip and continued. Verified against every legitimate path first: a propagating `skipRemainingTest()` stays *skipped*, a top-level `testSkipped()` outside any body is untouched, and the unrelated-skip-in-flight case is unaffected.
  - **This also gives the structural bypass a killing mutation row at last.** Reverting it makes the swallower fail as *misuse* instead, with the other message — so the three swallower tests assert the swallow-specific **message**, which is what discriminates the two constructions. The earlier claim that reverting the bypass was "inert" was wrong, and it was wrong because the swallowed case had not been probed.

  - **12 new registrations, one claim each.** Nine for #402 — sync misuse, async misuse before `await`, async misuse after `await`, `testAsync()` misuse, the innocent-async-in-flight regression, top-level `testSkipped()`, `skipRemainingTest()` sync and async, and the ledger-completeness invariant across all four outcomes — plus three for the #415 F1 swallowed abort (sync body, async body, `testAsync()`). Separate bodies deliberately: one mutation reddening a shared body leaves the second claim in it unproven however green the suite is. Base `origin/main` at `60e0b4b` measured, not assumed — **1205 passed / 0 failed, 2 skipped**; with this branch, **1217 / 0 / 2**, and `1217 − 1205 = 12` is exactly these registrations. The `#366 review C` async test's **name changed** (it now claims "never as failed, never as passed"), so a passing-name-set differential against the base shows one rename alongside the twelve additions.

- **The `#366 review C` harness self-check no longer reddens because some *other* test failed (#394).** Its `failed` assertion subtracted the suite's **global** failure counter across an `await`, so every failure that landed in that window was charged to it. The comment conceded the premise in the same breath it relied on it — *"in a green run nothing else touches them"* — and a mutation run is not a green run. **Not endpoint-touching**: `test-features.mjs` and this file only; no request handler, no response shape, no `server.mjs`.
  - **Measured, not argued.** At `af7c416`, injecting **one** unrelated failing *synchronous* test turned a clean `1191 passed / 0 failed` into `1190 / 2` — the injected failure plus `#366 review C` as collateral. With the fix, the byte-identical injection gives `1191 / 1` and that row prints `✓`. So the single test guaranteed to fire in every red run was the one carrying no information about what broke, which trains a reader to discount a red they should be reading.
  - **The tax was POSITIONAL, which is worse than a constant offset.** The reading body resumes at the first top-level `await` past the self-check block, so a failure landing before that resume was charged and one landing after was not — whether a given mutation paid depended on where its failure happened to land, which the person writing a mutation table neither chooses nor can see. That is why #388's round-3 review hit it in both T1 and T2 while #393's two mutations and #399's eight saw nothing. **Anyone still carrying a mutation table taken before this fix should not blanket-subtract one** — check whether the row is actually in the red set.
  - **Scoped by NAME, via a failure ledger, not by a smaller counter window.** `failedNames` records the name of every test at the moment it increments `failed` (all five `failed++` sites now go through `recordFailure(name)`), and the self-check asks whether *its own* name is in that ledger. A smaller window was not available: the second read is necessarily after an `await`, and the microtask checkpoint it resumes at is shared with the rest of the module.
  - **It cannot pass on zero observations, and the assertion ORDER is what makes that true.** The scoped check is a negative, so three things must hold before it runs: the `asyncOwn.length` premise proves the inner body registered a promise, the `skipped` delta proves it ran and skipped, and the `await` proves its settle handler finished — the counting decision has been made by the time anything is asserted about it. A fourth assertion pins `failedNames.length === failed`, so a future `failed++` written without a matching `recordFailure()` fails loudly instead of quietly turning the check into a negative over an incomplete list. All four are mutation-proven in the PR body, including that fourth one.
  - **The `skipped` half is deliberately left as a counter delta, and the asymmetry is now stated in the comment.** Re-derived rather than inherited from #378's review: both of its reads are plain statements of the same synchronous module evaluation, with only a `.length`, the `test()` call and a `.slice()` between them, so nothing can run in that window that the block did not run. A control mutation confirms it is live rather than merely sound — deleting the inner body's `skipRemainingTest()` reddens that assertion, *before* the await, which is also what stops a mutation aimed lower from reaching a vacuous negative.
- **A malformed percent-escape in a `/api/keys/…` path left the socket open forever; it now answers 404 (#379 cases 1 and 2, plus the sibling the issue does not list).** `decodeURIComponent` throws `URIError: URI malformed` on any input that is not a valid percent-encoding of UTF-8, and three routes called it **unguarded, in the router, on a client-controlled path segment**. The throw escaped the `async` request callback — Node never observes the promise a request handler returns — so `unhandledRejection` logged it and **nothing wrote a response and nothing closed the socket**. Measured on `main` before this fix, loopback, default `CLAUDE_AUTH_MODE=none`, 4 s deadline: `DELETE /api/keys/%E0%A4%A`, `PATCH /api/keys/%/quota` and `GET /api/keys/%/quota` each returned **0 bytes with the socket still open** and left exactly one `unhandled_rejection` line behind. **Neither needs a request body** — for the PATCH, the decode is the line *above* `req.setEncoding("utf8")`. After: all three answer `404 {"error":"Key not found"}` in ~2 ms, and the `unhandled_rejection` count for these inputs goes **6 → 0**.
  - **The `GET /api/keys/:id/quota` sibling is fixed here too, and it is not in #379's table.** It is the same unguarded call on the adjacent route of the same handler block, found by probing rather than reported, and measured to hang identically. Folded in under Iron Rule 11 §11.1 — cheaper than its own PR, found by this PR's stated job, and a reviewer can still follow the diff; none of the "never fold" conditions apply (same severity, same citation class, no hidden dependency). Fixing two of three would have left an identical credential-free hang on the very next route of the same handler block (on `main` the three calls sit at `server.mjs:3979`, `:3988` and `:4037`).
  - **404, not 400, and the choice is evidence-led** — #379 flags that it may itself move a rule. [measured] of the **18** probed paths that carry a malformed escape and do *not* reach one of these three calls, **16 already answer `404`** (`/%`, `/api/%`, `GET /api/keys/%`, `POST /api/keys/%`, `DELETE /cache/%`, `PATCH /settings/%`, …) — so 404 applies a rule this surface already has. The other two are stated rather than dropped, because an enumeration that omits its exceptions is not evidence: `GET /api/usage/%` and `GET /logs/%` answer `200` because both routes match by **prefix** and therefore *resolve* the URL rather than failing to. And **every** `jsonResponse(res, 400, …)` site in `server.mjs` is triggered by the request **body** or a body-derived field, never by the URL, so a URL-triggered 400 would invent a rejection *kind* that exists nowhere here — stated by trigger rather than by count, because that grep now also matches the comment asserting it. The body `{"error":"Key not found"}` is byte-identical to the 404 the two quota routes already return for a segment naming no key, so those two routes gain **no new response shape at all**.
  - **The DELETE is the one site where 404 is a status the route does not emit today**, stated rather than glossed. Its authorization is route (a): the only inputs whose behaviour changes are inputs that receive **no response at all**. Reusing its `200 {"revoked":false}` was considered and rejected — it needs an invented "use the raw undecoded segment" rule, and it would report a lookup that never happened (`no-such-key` answering `revoked:false` means "I looked and it was not there"; `%E0%A4%A` is not a key name at all).
  - **Authorized by ADR 0006 (grandfathered as of v3.16.4); contract unchanged — behaviour-preserving, route (a).** All three call sites are byte-identical at v3.16.4 (`git rev-list -n1 v3.16.4` → `9e25160`; read with `git show` because v3.16.4 and `main` share **no ancestor** — roots `593d0dc` vs `c180987` — so `git log -S` on `main` is not usable evidence). **Correction (2026-08-11, #409), appended rather than reworded:** the parenthetical's *reason* is false. `git show` was the right method for the wrong reason. v3.16.4 **is** an ancestor of `main` — `git merge-base v3.16.4 origin/main` returns `9e25160` at exit 0 — the repository has **one** root (`593d0dc`), `c180987` is an ordinary commit with parent `f2f9058`, and `git log -S` on `main` *is* usable evidence. The "two roots" reading was a **shallow-clone** artifact of the checkout it was read from. The byte-identical claim in `server.mjs` was corrected by `2593eb1` (#408) and the mechanism recorded in ADR 0017; **this site was counted as corrected but never marked**, which is why #409 re-swept. The second layer is stronger than byte-identity: [measured] booting **v3.16.4 itself**, each of these three requests sent the client **zero bytes and then killed the daemon** (exit 1, stack naming `server.mjs:1633`, `:1642`, `:1668` — v3.16.4 has no `unhandledRejection` handler, so Node's default terminated the process). The grandfathered snapshot's behaviour for these inputs is "no response, and the proxy dies". Contrast **#383**, where `POST /api/keys`' name regex entered *after* v3.16.4 and is not grandfathered at all.
  - **Route (a) is sound by exhaustion, and here it needs no enumeration of the input domain.** The `catch` **is** the throw set: it is unfiltered, so the inputs whose behaviour changes are precisely those for which the call throws — and every one of those reached the line, threw, and (there is no enclosing `try` anywhere in the request callback) was never answered. Changed-input set == previously-unanswered set, exactly.
  - **`decodeKeySegment` returns `null`, and every call site tests `=== null`.** The empty string is a **legitimate** decode result: `DELETE /api/keys/` answers `200 {"revoked":false,"idOrName":""}` today, so a `if (!idOrName)` guard would capture an answered request and change it — `?limit=0` from case 3, one route over. Pinned by its own assertion, and mutation **M4** (weakening `=== null` to a falsy test) is red on it.
  - **No B.2 response key-set change** — `docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json` is unchanged and the #346 suite check passes untouched, so **ADR 0012 is not engaged**. Case 4 of #379 (`POST /v1/chat/completions` with `{"messages":[null]}`) is **Class B.1** and is fixed in its own entry below: different authority, different PR.

- **`ocp doctor`'s multi-unit check can finally tell a declared second instance from a leftover duplicate (#327 part 3).** The check (#220, after incident #215) predates any way for an instance to say who it is, so multiplicity and duplication were the same observation and a host running two instances *correctly* was warned at on every `ocp update`. `OCP_INSTANCE_NAME` (`server.mjs`'s `INSTANCE_NAME`, `/health.instanceName`, shipped v3.29.0) is the discriminator that was missing; this teaches the check to read it. **Not endpoint-touching** — `scripts/doctor.mjs` and `test-features.mjs` (plus this file and `README.md`); no request handler, no `/health` field, no response shape.
  - **The acceptance condition was never "the warning stops".** It was "the warning still fires on undeclared multiplicity, and now only on that" — the same host also carried a genuine leftover duplicate unit next to the intended one, the exact defect this check exists to surface, and the permanent false alarm is what buried it. A warning that fires every run on a healthy configuration is worse than no warning, because it converts a real signal into background. The warn set is therefore a strict **superset** of the old one, by construction: port grouping is computed first and is untouched by any declaration, so nothing that warned before stops warning, and a same-port collision between two *differently* named units still warns exactly as loudly.
  - **The declaration is read from the unit file, never from `/health`,** and the rejected source is the interesting half. `/health` is the runtime truth but doctor runs before and around a restart, the check's own stated question is static ("which units WOULD start at boot"), and — decisively — a leftover duplicate is typically *enabled but not running*, so the live endpoint cannot see the one thing the check exists to find. There is deliberately no precedence rule and no fallback: "read the unit, else ask `/health`" would make the verdict depend on whether the host happened to be mid-upgrade. It also costs nothing, because `systemctl show -p Environment` was already in the check's single batched call.
  - **A new finding, for the shape that was previously invisible.** Two or more enabled units claiming the same identity on *different* ports now WARN. They do not contend for a port — both start, both bind — which is exactly why the failure is quiet, and why "different ports are never a boot race" was true and complete only while there was nothing else to say about them. The message is deliberately not worded as a boot race, nominates no unit to disable (MED-7's principle from #230), and offers the declaration as the first remedy since it loses nothing.
  - **An absent `OCP_INSTANCE_NAME` and an explicitly empty one are the same CLAIM and not the same OBSERVATION,** and the asymmetry is deliberate. They are folded together for the distinctness test, because README defines an empty value as the primary and requires only the *second* instance to declare itself — so both are a claim to be the primary, and two units making it is unresolved whichever spelling each used. Treating an absence as "nothing to compare" would be a predicate satisfied by a missing operand, which is the defect shape recorded in `AGENTS.md` from #371. They stay distinct in the parsed model (`string | null`), in `describeDeclaration`'s three renderings and in both WARN messages, because the operator's next move differs and because rendering an absence as `OCP_INSTANCE_NAME=""` would invent a directive nobody wrote. **Two** places fold them, and the code comment now enumerates both rather than claiming uniqueness: the distinctness test, and the INFO inventory's `(primary)` label — the second is reachable only in the `declared` state, where at most one unit can claim the primary, so there is never a pair to tell apart. An earlier revision claimed the fold happened in exactly one place; independent review on #399 found the second, and a universal negative is exactly the shape `AGENTS.md`'s `4022be4` says must name the mutation that proves it.
  - **A correctly-declared multi-instance host now says so out loud,** at INFO: `2 enabled OCP units, all declared and distinct … "ocp.service" :3456 (primary), "ocp-wifibot.service" :3457 ("wifibot")`. Silence cannot carry that, and this file already learned once (MED-3.5 on #230) that "verified clear" and "nothing looked" being the same silence is its own defect. It is pushed only on hosts that actually have more than one enabled unit, so it is not a line every host carries forever.
  - **The value is `.trim()`ed to mirror `server.mjs`'s `INSTANCE_NAME` exactly**, so the check models what the server will do with the string rather than what the file literally says; a quoted whole-assignment (`Environment="OCP_INSTANCE_NAME=wifi bot"`, systemd's rendering when the value contains whitespace) is read whole rather than truncated at the space; a longer key ending in the same letters is not mistaken for it; and on launchd a self-closing `<string/>` counts as a declaration with an empty value, not as an absent one.
  - **A hostile instance name is withheld from the message but never from the analysis.** Unlike the unit `Id` / launchd `Label` validators, which reject the whole candidate, a bad instance name may only be withheld from the *rendering* — rejecting the candidate would let a duplicate unit hide behind a weird string in a file. The raw value is still what the distinctness test compares.
  - `scripts/upgrade.mjs` is untouched: `groups` still means exactly "the port-collision groups", which is what `describeSecondUnit` (#253) reads. Established behaviourally rather than by grepping for callers (ADR 0015 correction 3): #253's own end-to-end test drives the real gather layer through `runUpgrade` and asserts the rollback phase names the second enabled unit.
  - **#327 stays open, and two things it asks for are deliberately still missing.** This is the first of the three consequences the issue opened with. `ocp update` still updates only the instance it was invoked from, and the fleet inventory is still not derivable. Separately, the `could not verify: … returned too many candidates` line the issue quotes is **not** what this changes: that line is the `unknown` state, produced by the *gathering* layer when a scope could not be enumerated at all, and no declaration can help when nothing could be read. The multiplicity **verdict** is what learned to read the declaration.

- **`ocp update`'s neutral post-restart verdict recommended a rollback for seven different measurements, including ones a rollback cannot fix (#386).** When no cell claimed the outcome, `runFullUpgrade` threw a bare `new Error("post-flight failed")` and its `catch` attached the generic **tree-state** hint — *"Working tree may be at new version. Run `ocp update --rollback` to restore from snapshot."* That sentence is written for a failure **before the service was touched** (a failed `git checkout`, a failed `npm install`), where the tree really is the only thing in doubt. Inheriting it after the post-flight makes it exactly wrong: by then the tree is known-good and the service is the unknown.
  - **Measured end to end through `runUpgrade`, and it is seven kinds rather than the one that was reported.** With the restart commands all exiting 0, `unreachable`, `timeout`, `http-error`, `unparseable`, `body-not-ocp`, `version-mismatch` **and** `probe-failed-unclassified` all fell through, and all seven printed the identical two lines: `✗ post-flight failed` / `hint: Working tree may be at new version…`. The CLI prints `e.message`, `snapshot`, `target` and `hint` — never a phase's `message` — so the classification the post-flight phase carries never reached the operator at all.
  - **What did *not* cause it.** An earlier draft of this entry blamed #372/#381's re-keying of DOWN onto positive evidence. That is a non sequitur, caught in review: with the restart commands exiting 0 the restart-failure arms cannot fire *whatever* they are keyed on, so the re-keying is irrelevant to this lane — these seven have always fallen through for want of a cell of their own. Measured, forward lane, rows carrying the generic tree hint: **9** at `8a2c884` (pre-#381, all restart-OK), **8** at `40bd94b` (#381), **8** at `11f49f2` (#385). #381 *removed* two by giving `probe-could-not-run` and `not-ok-status` their own cells and *added* one — the `probe-failed-unclassified` + restart-FAILED row #386 reported, which before #381 printed `THE PROXY IS DOWN`. #385 changed **zero** rows, exactly as it said: it moved the classification and left every call site's wording untouched.
  - **Fixed at the destination, not at the kind.** #386 offered both remedies and preferred fixing the neutral verdict, because patching the one kind that reached it leaves the arm wrong for whatever kind is added next — which had by then happened twice (`not-ok-status` in #372, `probe-failed-unclassified` in #381, the second one added by the fix for the first). The arm now carries its own message and hint: it names the measurement with the classifier's raw `detail`, says **`ocp doctor` first**, names the restart failure when there was one, offers the `--post-flight-only` re-check, and **demotes** the rollback to a trailing sentence — the same demotion the DOWN cell already performs. The rollback is offered, not deleted; the tree may well be at the new version.
  - **The measurement is named with the raw `detail`, deliberately not with `postFlightFailureSuffix`,** whose `default:` arm narrates an unrecognised kind as *"(unreachable — …)"*. Rendering that here would pre-narrate the next unnamed kind as a dead proxy inside the one arm whose job is to claim nothing — #386's own shape, re-imported.
  - The generic tree-state hint keeps the domain it was written for: every arm of the post-restart ladder now sets its own hint, so it is reached only by a failure before the service was disturbed. That invariant and its control both have tests.
- **A bare, credential-free `GET /api/usage?limit=%` held a connection open forever — `parseInt` produced `NaN` and handed it to a sink that throws (#379 case 3).** No request body, no `Authorization` header, and unanswered in the **default** `CLAUDE_AUTH_MODE=none`. `parseInt("%", 10)` is `NaN`, `Math.min(NaN, 500)` is `NaN`, and `getRecentUsage(NaN)` throws `datatype mismatch` at the SQLite bind; the throw escapes the `async` request callback, which Node does not observe, so `unhandledRejection` logs it and **nothing answers or closes the socket**. Measured before the fix on a real `server.mjs`: 0 bytes received, socket still open at a 4 s deadline, one `unhandled_rejection` line per request. That is a connection-exhaustion primitive, not merely a correctness bug. The percent-escape is incidental — `?limit=abc` behaves identically, because `NaN` is the defect.

  **`?hours` is fixed in the same change, deliberately.** It is the same cause on the **adjacent line of the same handler** (`new Date(NaN).toISOString()` → `RangeError: Invalid time value`), at the same severity and the same citation class, and measured to hang identically. Fixing only `limit` would have left an identical credential-free hang one character away. Iron Rule 11 §11.1 fold-in test: cheaper than its own PR, found by this PR's stated job, and the diff stays readable. The issue's other three cases are **not** touched here — cases 1 and 2 are `decodeURIComponent` in the router and case 4 is Class B.1, all different mechanisms or classes.

  **Authorized by ADR 0006 (grandfathered as of v3.16.4) — behaviour-preserving, route (a).** At v3.16.4 (`git rev-list -n1 v3.16.4` → `9e25160`; note the tag and `main` share **no** ancestor, so `git log -S` on `main` cannot see this) both call sites **and both sinks in `keys.mjs`** are **byte-identical** to the pre-fix code — so the grandfathered snapshot *is* the defective behaviour, and the hang is inside what ADR 0006 froze rather than something added after it (contrast **#383**, whose key-name regex entered between v3.17.1 and v3.18.0 and is therefore unauthorized). And `limit`'s documented meaning — "how many recent rows, default 50, capped at 500" — is unchanged. Same reasoning as #360's `parsed === null` guard: a request that currently receives **no response at all** is not a behaviour anyone can be relying on. `lib/env.mjs`'s `parsePositiveInt` was **not** reused because it would have changed four inputs that are answered today (`?limit=5abc` → 5 via `parseInt` prefix, `?limit=-1` → SQLite `LIMIT -1` = unbounded, `?limit=0` → 0 rows, `?hours=-1` → a window in the future) — a contract change needing its own ADR (`ALIGNMENT.md:114`), and the #383 precedent warns against adding a rejection where v3.16.4 accepted.

  **The finiteness check runs AFTER the `Math.min` clamp, and that ordering is the correctness argument.** `parseInt` yields NaN, ±Infinity or a finite integer, and `Math.min(+Infinity, 500)` is `500` — an ordinary finite value that is *answered* at v3.16.4, so a `?limit` of 309+ digits returns the 500-row cap today. Checking finiteness *before* the clamp would substitute the default instead: a contract change on an answered request. The first revision of this fix did exactly that and it was caught by independent review, not by the original 14-input probe, whose largest input was 16 digits (the defect needs 309). **Route (a) is now sound by case exhaustion rather than by sampling** — which matters, because sampling is what let `+Infinity` through. `parseInt` returns exactly NaN, `±Infinity` or a finite integer, so the only non-finite values `Math.min(parseInt(…), cap)` can yield are **NaN** and **-Infinity** (`+Infinity` becomes the cap, a finite value, so the guard cannot fire for it) — and both were measured to throw at both sinks, i.e. both were unanswered. The set of inputs whose value changes is therefore *exactly* the set that received no response. Corroborated over 47 raw inputs × both parameters against the real sinks: **zero** inputs the old code answered change value, and 20 that it could not answer now do.

  **What this does not fix.** A *finite* value can still reach both sinks and throw, so those requests still hang: `?limit=-100000000000000000000` (SQLite bind → `datatype mismatch`) and `?hours=-2400000000` (`Date` range overflow → `Invalid time value`). Both are **pre-existing** — identical at v3.16.4 and on `main` — and both are a different input class from the `parseInt`-yields-NaN defect #379 case 3 describes, with their own contract questions. Tracked as **issue #400**, which carries both sinks' measured acceptance sets, rather than folded in; this guard closes the NaN class only, and the code comment says so at the call site.

  **Correction to the issue: `/logs` is not the precedent it was cited as, checked on the wire.** #379 names `GET /logs?limit=%E0%A4%A` → `200` as an existing clamp to copy. It is not one. `/logs` **has no `limit` parameter** (it reads `n` and `level`), so that `200` is an unread query param. And `?n=%` also answers `200` not because anything clamps but because `NaN` flows into `Array.prototype.slice`, which coerces it to `0` — measured, `?n=%` returns **173517 bytes** (the whole log file) against **776** for `?n=5`. `/logs` survives by *sink tolerance*, not by a guard, so copying it would mean letting `NaN` through and hoping the next sink forgives — exactly what SQLite's bind and `Date.prototype.toISOString` do not.

  **No B.2 response key-set change** — no field is added, removed or renamed, so ADR 0012 is not engaged and the checked-in snapshot is unchanged (the `#346` suite check passes untouched).

- **`POST /v1/chat/completions` with a non-object `messages` ELEMENT hung the connection — or bought a billed empty spawn (#379 case 4, the last of the four).** `{"messages":[null]}` passed every guard #360 added — the body is an object, `messages` is truthy, the array is non-empty — and then threw deep in the spawn path (`extractSystemPrompt` / `messagesToPrompt` dereference `m.role` / `m.content` on null). The throw escaped the `async` router: measured before the fix, 0 bytes, socket still open at a 4 s deadline, one `unhandled_rejection`. The **same** array with a primitive element (`42`, `"str"`, `true`) or an object with no string `role` did **not** hang — property access on a primitive boxes rather than throws — so it flowed through as an empty prompt and paid for a real, billed upstream spawn, the #360 "empty-handed" shape one level down. Both halves are the same defect: the element was never validated.
  - **Class B.1, and the spec compels the 400.** OpenAI's message schema (https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference/chat/create) makes each message an object with a REQUIRED string `role`; ADR 0006's grandfather provision (4th bullet) does not extend to B.1. The guard is `!isJsonObject(m) || typeof m.role !== "string"` per element, returning `400 {"error":{"message":"messages[N] must be an object with a string 'role'","type":"invalid_request_error"}}` — the shared `isJsonObject` predicate plus the schema's one load-bearing field. `content` is deliberately NOT validated: OpenAI permits null content on an assistant message carrying `tool_calls`/refusal, and `contentToText` already handles it defensively.
  - **No B.2 response key-set change** — `server.mjs` only, B.1 surface; `docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json` untouched.

- **A graceful stop landing mid-turn left the live, authenticated `claude` pane running — and reported success (#362).** A pane serving a turn was owned by nothing `gracefulShutdown` could reach: a warm pane **leaves** the pool the instant it is acquired (correctly — POOL/REAPER INVARIANT property 2 says an acquired pane is the caller's), a cold-booted pane was never in the pool, and neither is in `activeProcesses`, because a pane's `claude` is a child of the tmux **server**, not of node. So `drain()` missed it, `activeProcesses.size === 0` held, and `process.exit(0)` ran **in the same tick** — which is exactly why `runTuiTurn`'s `finally` never got to kill it. `ocp stop` (or any `SIGTERM`) during a turn therefore exited 0 while leaving an interactive session holding the operator's credential; only a later `ocp start` reaped it, so a stop *without* a restart left it running indefinitely. **This is the same bug one state over from the one already fixed**: the pool's own step-2b comment describes the general shape accurately, and sits directly above the code that still missed the acquired case. Fixed with the small seam ADR 0015's correction 5 names, **not** the universal active-work registry the external audit prescribed and that ADR 0015 rejects: `lib/tui/session.mjs` — the module that already owns panes and already kills them — now also names the ones a turn is holding, and `gracefulShutdown` gains a step 2c that kills them **synchronously**, beside the step 2b doing the same for the pool. `activeProcesses` keeps meaning exactly what it meant. Synchronous is measured, not asserted: a mutation deferring the same kill to a `Promise.resolve().then(…)` leaves the pane orphaned just as completely as deleting the call, because `process.exit(0)` wins the tick. With TUI mode off the registry is empty and the new step issues no tmux command at all.

- **A TUI-mode host that served every request could report `auth.ok: null` indefinitely — ADR 0014's premise silently did not hold on that lane (#361).** `noteAuthVerifiedByRequest()` had exactly two call sites, both in the default `-p`/stream-json lane (the `claude_ok` branches of `callClaude` and `callClaudeStreaming`), and neither is reachable from `callClaudeTui` — which is what `server.mjs` selects **wholesale** under `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE=true`. ADR 0014 § C states the rule for *"a request that reaches the model and succeeds"*, unqualified by lane, so this is an implementation omission and not a contract change; ADR 0015's field table adjudicates `auth.ok` as ADR 0006 route (a) for exactly that reason. **The failure direction is the safe one**, which is why it survived: #308 existed because `/health` claimed `true` on hosts that could not serve a single request, and in TUI mode its fix simply never applied — producing `null` rather than a false `true`, so nothing alarmed. Measured end to end against a live `server.mjs` driving one complete turn through a stub tmux: with the call absent, a TUI turn that returned **HTTP 200 with the model's text** left `auth.ok:false, okSource:"probe"` completely untouched; with it present the same turn yields `ok:true, okSource:"request"` — while `lastOutcome` stays the probe's own `"rejected"` (ADR 0014 § C's provenance separation) and the `consecutiveFailures` tally clears, exactly as this same function has always done on the default lane. One call site covers **both** TUI lanes, because `callClaudeTuiStreaming` awaits `callClaudeTui` rather than spawning for itself. Placement is after the honesty gates and that is load-bearing: with `CLAUDE_TUI_ERROR_PATTERNS` unset the gate immediately above is literally an auth-failure-banner detector, so raising the verdict ahead of it would set `auth.ok:true` on precisely the turn whose text says the credential was refused. **The generic counters named in the same issue — `activeRequests`, `avgElapsed`/`maxElapsed`, `stats.errors`, `totalRequests`, `oneOffRequests`, `timeouts` — are deliberately NOT changed here**: ADR 0015 records each as a change to the field's *rule* (ADR 0007 introduced `tui.inflight` precisely so TUI would not be folded into `activeRequests`, and the TUI elapsed `0` is an explicit sentinel), so they need their own ADR rather than a fix.

  **Two prose corrections shipped alongside, both found by independent review, and both of the same shape as the defect itself — a statement about the code that the code contradicts.** (1) `noteAuthVerifiedByRequest`'s *header* asserted *"deliberately does NOT touch `consecutiveFailures`"* directly above the line that clears it, while its *body* already quoted that very sentence as a defect a previous reviewer had caught — the correction had been applied to the body only, so the note recording the fix sat **below** the sentence that was still wrong. The header also said *"called from both `claude_ok` sites"*, which this change made false by adding a third caller that logs no `claude_ok`. (2) `README` § "What `auth.ok` means" stated, in bold, *"`status` is unaffected by any of this"*. `auth.ok` indeed never moves `status` — but the `consecutiveFailures` tally that decides `status` **is** cleared by any successful request (ADR 0014 § Consequences: *"a deliberate restoration of ADR 0010's self-heal"*), so a `degraded` host can return to `ok` on the first request that succeeds with no probe in between. That mattered because `ocp update`'s post-flight and the dashboard's status card both read `status`, and the dashboard has no other auth signal. Both statements now agree, each names the other, and both cite ADR 0014 § Consequences as the authority they restate.

- **`ocp update` told operators "THE PROXY IS DOWN … /health is not answering" about a proxy that had just answered (#372).** The forward path's DOWN cell keyed on `restartFailure && !postFlight.ok` — the `else`. `runPostFlightCheck` reports `ok: false` for seven distinct states and that sentence is true in two of them, so the cell fired on the **absence of a successful probe** rather than on evidence of non-reach. This is the fleet-rollout path, run unattended, and it is the same defect #352/#371 fixed on the rollback path a day earlier. Measured at v3.29.2, each with a restart command failing:
  - **answering with the wrong version** → "THE PROXY IS DOWN … not answering", inside the same thrown error whose own `post-flight` phase recorded `last saw version=3.10.0`. One error, two contradictory statements about one measurement — and the CLI's phase printer surfaces the hint, so the false half is the half the operator reads.
  - **answering with the RIGHT version but `status: "degraded"`** → the same sentence, about a proxy serving exactly the version that upgrade had just installed.
  - **answering with a non-JSON body** → the same sentence, while the phase message said "Something is answering on the port, but it is not this proxy."
  - `ocp`'s `cmd_restart`, which these cells cite as their authority, has never had this defect: its DOWN cell keys on the curl exit code (`probe_rc -ne 0`), not on a version predicate. So splitting the cell is the **faithful port** and `!postFlight.ok` was the embellishment. The forward path now carries the same five cells in the same order as the rollback path, with its own wording (its hints may end by pointing at `ocp update --rollback`, which the rollback path cannot say).

- **A kind name that made a claim the code could not back: `version-mismatch` covered three unrelated states (#372).** `runPostFlightCheck` stamped it on **every** post-body rejection, in one unconditional line — but the acceptance predicate `postFlightOk` rejects on `status` as well as version, and `/health`'s status domain is `{"ok","degraded"}`, always over HTTP 200. A right-version/degraded proxy, a wrong-version proxy and a JSON body carrying no `version` field all arrived under one name. #371 reconstructed the missing distinctions inside `runRollback`'s own cells; that left the misnomer in place for `runFullUpgrade` and for `postFlightFailureSuffix` — **the renderer bash's `ocp update` delegates its final verdict to** (`_cmd_update_light` / `_cmd_update_restart` both report whatever `--post-flight-only` says). Split into `version-mismatch`, `not-ok-status` and `body-not-ocp`, so a cell can key on a kind and get what the kind says. A status-only rejection now claims nothing about versions on either path.

- **A predicate satisfied by a missing operand: `SERVING THE WRONG VERSION (3.10.0, expected )` (#372).** `servedWrongVersion` was `lastSeen !== postFlight.target`, and `readSnapshot` returns `null` for `fromVersion` when `from-version.txt` is unreadable (`scripts/lib/snapshot.mjs`'s `catch { return null; }`), so `target` is `""` and the `!==` is true for any version at all. Fixed **in the classifier rather than in each cell**: `version-mismatch` is now only stamped when two present versions were compared and differed, so every caller inherits the property instead of having to re-establish it. Worth stating because it is the counter-example to the obvious structural fix — splitting the classifier by *kind* would not have caught this, since the defect was in the other operand.

- **A valid JSON `null` body was reported as the proxy being DOWN, with a JS `TypeError` as the operator-facing detail (#372).** `lastSeen = body.version` had no optional chaining, so `null` threw; the throw was caught by `classifyPostFlightProbeFailure` two lines down, fell to its default arm, and produced `{kind: "unreachable", detail: "Cannot read properties of null (reading 'version')"}` — DOWN, for something that answered. (Same JSON-scalar shape as #360, on a different surface.)

- **`postFlightFailureSuffix` narrated answers as `unreachable`, on the lane bash reports verbatim (#372).** Its `if (result?.lastSeen)` ran ahead of the switch — the same negation shape one layer out, firing on the presence of a value `runPostFlightCheck` never resets — and its `default:` arm caught everything else. A body with no `version` field rendered `" (unreachable — serving unknown)"`. It is now keyed on the classification; the `version-mismatch` text is preserved **byte-for-byte** (it was already correct, and a test pins it), as are the no-classification fallbacks.

- **Two hints named a `--post-flight-only` invocation that cannot check anything (#372).** With an absent target they rendered `--post-flight-only null` and `--post-flight-only v`, and nothing downstream catches either: the CLI's guard fails closed only on a *missing* or `--`-prefixed value, while `"null"` can never match a real version and `"v"` v-strips to `""`, which `postFlightOk` deliberately degrades to the serving check alone — so the advice given at the operator's least-informed moment would report success against **any** version. The clause is now omitted when there is no version to name. Reachable in production on the rollback path (unreadable `from-version.txt`); on the forward path reachable through `mockDoctor` but **not** demonstrated reachable in production, and the code says which is which.

- **`nothing will start it on its own` was still asserted for both service shapes on the forward path (#372).** #352's LOW-1 fixed this on the rollback path only. `scripts/lib/install-autostart.mjs` writes `Restart=always`/`RestartSec=5`, and `recoveryPlanCommands`' own start-limit arithmetic *depends* on systemd restarting the unit by itself — so the claim contradicted a premise this file already relies on. Stated per shape: true on launchd, where a succeeded `bootout` unloads the job.

- **`unreachable` was the probe classifier's DEFAULT arm, so `THE PROXY IS DOWN` still fired on an `else` (#372, found by #381's independent review).** The DOWN cell was re-keyed onto the kind — and the kind itself was a claim nothing established: `classifyPostFlightProbeFailure` returned `unreachable` for curl exit 7 **and for everything it did not recognise**. Measured: a spawn `EAGAIN` or `ENOMEM` (the probe process could not be started at all — a purely local fault, and the fleet includes Pis), curl 52 (empty reply) and curl 56 (connection reset) — the last two meaning the peer *accepted* the connection — all reported the proxy as down. `unreachable` now means only "curl said it could not connect"; spawn failures are recognised as local by the absence of an exit status plus an errno; everything else gets `probe-failed-unclassified`, which no cell routes to DOWN. This is the same defect the same PR fixed for `version-mismatch`, one branch over in the same function — the rule was applied to the kind being looked at and not to the one beside it.

- **A degraded proxy already serving the new version was told to roll back (#372/#381).** With no cell of its own, `not-ok-status` fell to the forward path's neutral arm, whose hint is *"Run `ocp update --rollback`"*. The upgrade's code **is** what is running, and a rollback cannot clear a degraded status — that comes from the `claude` binary being unusable or from repeated auth rejections, neither of which the tree version decides. The state now has its own cell naming what is serving and pointing at `ocp doctor`. (The rollback path needed no equivalent: its neutral verdict already says `ocp doctor`.)

- **Non-string `version` values were coerced into the version comparison (#372/#381).** `postFlightOk` compares `body?.version === want`; the classifier compared `String(body.version) !== want`. Measured against target 3.29.2: `version: ["3.29.2"]` coerced *equal* and fell through to a status rejection rendering *"reported status=ok, not status=ok"*; `version: []` produced `SERVING THE WRONG VERSION (, expected 3.29.2)` — the same empty-operand rendering this issue was filed to fix, on the other operand; `version: {}` produced `[object Object]`. `/health` answers with a version **string**, so anything else is now `body-not-ocp`.

- **The "will anything start it on its own?" note was a two-way answer over a three-way domain (#372/#381).** `planRestart` returns `launchd`, `user-unit` or `system-unit`, and the non-launchd branch asserted `Restart=always` for both. OCP installs only the **user** unit; a `system-unit` is whatever `resolveOwningUnit` found holding the port, and OCP never reads its `Restart=` directive — so on a host with a hand-written unit carrying `Restart=on-failure` the hint claimed systemd might recover it. Now answered per shape from one shared helper, with the discovered-unit case stated as unknown and the launchd case hedged, because the resolved `bootout`'s exit status is discarded (`|| true`) and this note only renders when a restart command has already failed.

- **`ocp update`'s `--post-flight-only v` reported success against any version (#372/#381).** The CLI's fail-closed guard rejected a missing or `--`-prefixed target, but `"v"` is neither; it v-strips to `""`, which `postFlightOk` degrades to the serving check alone. Measured against a live proxy: `✓ service now serving v`. The guard now also rejects a target that v-strips to nothing.

- **Not endpoint-touching.** `scripts/upgrade.mjs` is the `ocp update` CLI dispatcher; `server.mjs` is untouched by this change. No `ALIGNMENT.md` rule is invoked and no ADR is cited, because none governs this surface.

### Changed

- **The aggregate request counters now count the TUI lane too — `/health`, `/status` and `/usage` (#361). Authorized by [ADR 0018](docs/adr/0018-aggregate-counters-count-every-lane.md), added in the same PR.** `stats.totalRequests`, `stats.activeRequests`, `stats.errors` and `stats.timeouts` were written only by `spawnClaudeProcess` and `trackError`, so a TUI-mode host served every request with all four frozen at `0`. The same four are republished as `/status`'s `requests.{total,active,errors,timeouts}` and `/usage`'s `proxy.{totalRequests,activeRequests,errors,timeouts}`, so all three endpoints under-reported together.
  - **The defect was half-participation, not separation.** Measured by symbol at `dd90be3`: `recordModelRequest`, `recordModelSuccess` and `recordModelError` are all called from `callClaudeTui` already, so the TUI lane wrote **every per-model counter and no aggregate one**. A failed turn moved the per-model error count while `stats.errors` stayed `0` — one response reporting the same event two ways. All-in or all-out would both be defensible; this was neither, and it was reached by omission rather than decision.
  - **Class B.2 contract change — ADR 0006 route (b), NOT the grandfather clause.** Every field keeps its name and type; the *rule that determines its value* changes from "on the `-p` lane" to "on any lane", which is `CLAUDE.md`'s dividing question and `ALIGNMENT.md:114`'s new-authorization trigger. Cited as `Authorized by ADR 0006 (Class B.2 inventory) and ADR 0018`. **ADR 0012 deliberately does not apply** — nothing is added or removed, so this is not an additive change.
  - **`docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json` is byte-identical and the in-suite key-set diff stayed green.** That is the correct signal: existing fields, existing types, new values. A diff in that file would have meant the change exceeded its authorization.
  - **Supersedes one clause of [ADR 0007](docs/adr/0007-tui-interactive-mode.md).** Its § "ALIGNMENT authorization for the `/health` change" says *"No existing `/health` field is changed, renamed, removed, or re-typed, and no existing semantics change"*; the final clause is superseded for these four fields only. ADR 0007's claim was **true when made** — it authorized adding `tui.*` without touching `stats.*` and did exactly that. It never argued that `activeRequests` *should* exclude TUI, which is how it has since been read. `tui.inflight` is unchanged and still reports the TUI-only count; `stats.activeRequests` is now the total across both lanes, so the two are in a containment relationship rather than a contradiction.
  - **Not folded, each for a stated reason.** `stats.oneOffRequests` means "served by a one-off `-p` **spawn**", not "a request" — a TUI turn is the opposite of a one-off spawn, so folding it would make the field's name false; it is now the `-p` subset of `totalRequests` rather than its duplicate, which is the first time the two have differed. `stats.queued` is a **gauge assigned from the `-p` semaphore**, not a counter, and `tui.queued` already publishes the TUI queue; summing them would make `stats.queued` disagree with `concurrency.queued`, which is fed from the same variable. `stats.queueRejections` is mirrored at `concurrency.queueRejections` for the same reason — a TUI queue-full rejection is genuinely unreported, and reporting it needs a **new field**, which would move the key-set snapshot this authorization does not cover.
  - **The per-model `0` elapsed sentinel is unchanged, and verified still deliberate.** `callClaudeTui` calls `recordModelSuccess(cliModel, 0)` under *"elapsed not measurable here"*. **There is no `stats.avgElapsed` or `stats.maxElapsed`** — those exist only per-model, where the TUI lane already participates — so there was no aggregate counter to fold, and replacing "unmeasured" with a duration is a separate rule change with an open question (which clock).
  - **`stats.timeouts` covers both wall-clock outcomes, and per-model `timeouts` is corrected with it.** `readTuiTranscript` polls to one cap and then either returns `truncated: true` or throws `tui_transcript_timeout`; the difference is how much text arrived, not whether the bound was exceeded. `callClaudeTui` also called `recordModelError(cliModel, false)` unconditionally, so the TUI lane's per-model `timeouts` was permanently `0` for the same reason — folding the aggregate field alone would have replaced one asymmetry with a fresher one inside the same response.
  - **Repairs a runbook that gated on the broken field.** `docs/runbooks/615-canary.md` § "Step 1 — Quiesce the host" tells the operator to wait until `activeRequests` is `0`, and that runbook's whole purpose is a **TUI-mode** billing canary with `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE=true` as a precondition — so it has been reading `0` while a TUI turn was mid-flight, the exact state it exists to exclude. No runbook edit was needed: the text was always right and the field was not.
  - **Consumers were swept from the wire and the repo, not from imports.** Named: `dashboard.html` (`refreshStatus`), `ocp` (`cmd_usage`), `ocp-plugin/index.js` (`cmdHealth`, `cmdStatus`, `cmdUsage`). Generic, which a field-name grep cannot see: `ocp health` / `ocp status` pipe the whole body through `python3 -m json.tool` and will print the new values with no code change, and `scripts/b2-key-snapshot.mjs` records key paths only and is value-blind by construction. None needs a code change; all were already reading these fields and under-reporting.
  - **Still not covered, stated so a green suite is not read as coverage.** TUI queue-full rejections are counted nowhere; cache hits and singleflight followers still move no aggregate counter on **either** lane (ADR 0015 correction 1's two-token question, untouched here); `avgElapsed`/`maxElapsed` still report `0` for every TUI turn; a TUI turn **waiting** for a semaphore slot still reads `activeRequests: 0` (symmetric with `-p`, and it bounds the runbook repair above to *running* turns); and per-model `requests` can now exceed `stats.totalRequests` by one on a queued disconnect, because `recordModelRequest` runs before the semaphore and the aggregate counter after it.
  - **Found by independent review, and recorded because the defects were all in the PROSE rather than the behaviour.** Three claims about this change were wrong while the code was right: a `server.mjs` comment said the `tui_transcript_timeout` token was "pinned by a behavioural test" when **no assertion on it existed anywhere** — so a rename would have silently under-counted while the suite stayed green; the per-model `timeouts` correction had no test and is invisible to the B.2 key-set snapshot (which probes a fresh server whose model map is empty); and ADR 0018's own table pointed a reviewer at the wrong call site. All three are fixed, and the two coverage gaps now have tests and mutations (including a **rename** mutation, which is the one the false comment claimed was already covered). A fourth review finding was itself mistaken in a useful way: per-model stats are served by **`/usage` only**, not `/health`, and an earlier draft of the new test asserted against `/health` on that premise.

- **`POST /api/keys` now requires a JSON object — a scalar or array body is `400` and mints nothing (#383). Authorized by [ADR 0017](docs/adr/0017-api-keys-request-shape.md), added in the same PR.** Until this change `42`, `"str"`, `true` and `[]` each returned **201 with a real, usable API key**: property access on a primitive boxes rather than throwing, so `parsed.name` was `undefined`, the `||` fell through to the `key-<epoch-ms>` auto-name, and `createKey()` ran — indistinguishable on the wire from the documented `{}` request. Measured against a running server with the **key store** read after each request, not inferred from the code. `{}` still mints an auto-named key, `{"name":"…"}` still mints a named one, and `null` keeps the exact status and message #360 gave it.
  - **This closes the deferral the #360 entry below records.** That entry says of this same defect: *"Filed as #383 and deliberately not fixed here"*, because tightening it needed its own ADR. This is that ADR and that fix, in the same release. Its neighbouring sentence — *"`POST /api/keys` deliberately uses neither \[predicate]: it tests `parsed === null`"* — described the code accurately when it was written and is superseded here; both entries are left standing so the sequence stays legible.
  - **Class B.2 contract change — ADR 0006 route (b), NOT the grandfather clause.** `/api/keys*` is grandfathered at v3.16.4 and this changes *which requests are accepted*, which `ALIGNMENT.md:114` and ADR 0006:39 make a new authorization request. Cited as `Authorized by ADR 0006 (Class B.2 inventory) and ADR 0017`. The grandfather clause is deliberately not cited as if this were behaviour-preserving: no field's value is being made truthful under an unchanged rule — the rule for accepting a request is what changes.
  - **The changed-input set is closed by the JSON grammar, not sampled.** `JSON.parse` with no reviver returns exactly one of RFC 8259's six value productions — object, array, string, number, boolean, null. A body that does not parse is already answered by the handler's own `catch`; `null` was already `400` and is unchanged; `object` is the admitted case. That leaves array | string | number | boolean, and the regression test probes one per production — the complete changed set rather than a selection from it.
  - **The key-name regex `/^[A-Za-z0-9 ._-]{1,64}$/` is retroactively authorized as of v3.18.0 — no code change.** It is not v3.16.4 behaviour and never had an authorization: it entered in `879b40f` (*"escape dashboard DB-sourced values + validate key names (#114)"*, 2026-05-31) and has shipped in **every** tagged release from v3.18.0 through v3.29.2 — re-derived tag by tag across all 51 tags before merge. `"my key ✓"` and any name over 64 characters were accepted at v3.16.4 and are a 400 today. It is correct on its merits (#114 was a dashboard-injection fix), so ADR 0017 § Decision 2 authorizes it with its real provenance recorded rather than quietly re-baselining it. Widening or narrowing the charset still needs its own ADR.
  - **The handler comment no longer argues against fixing this.** It argued the endpoint sat at its v3.16.4 snapshot — twelve lines above the unauthorized narrowing that had already moved it off that snapshot. It is now a record of what the endpoint accepts and under which authorization (ADR 0017 § Decision 3).
  - **A false claim about this repository's git history was live in four places, and is corrected in all of them.**

    **CORRECTION (2026-08-11, #409) — that sentence is itself wrong, and it is the more dangerous kind of wrong: a claim that a sweep is finished tells the next reader not to look.** Re-swept across `docs/`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `*.mjs`, `ocp`, `ocp-connect`, `ocp-plugin/`, `README.md`, `ALIGNMENT.md`, `AGENTS.md` and `CLAUDE.md` for every form of the claim — `no ancestor`, `shares no ancestor`, `common ancestor`, `ancestorless`, `unrelated histories`, `squashed root`, `recreated`/`recreation`, `c180987`, `git log --all`, `merge-base … empty/nothing` — plus its *consequence* phrasings (`GitHub API`, `pickaxe`, `not usable evidence`, `read with git show because`, `because this checkout`). **Two instances were still live and uncorrected in place at `2593eb1`**, and this entry asserted otherwise:

    - **`docs/adr/0008-tui-warm-pane-pool.md:73`** — *"shares no ancestor with `main`"*, in the amendment #404 landed. Never corrected anywhere. `git merge-base origin/main db8cc6a` → **`bdb6662`**, exit 0.
    - **This CHANGELOG's own #379-cases-1+2 entry** — *"v3.16.4 and `main` share **no ancestor** — roots `593d0dc` vs `c180987` — so `git log -S` on `main` is not usable evidence"*. This entry **quoted that exact sentence** (*"is not usable evidence"*) while counting it among the corrected three; the correction existed only here, ~80 lines below, with **no marker at the site**. A reader landing on the claim saw no signal at all.

    Both are now corrected in place, by appending rather than rewording.

    **The two have DIFFERENT causes, and neither is a grep that missed a paraphrase.** An earlier draft of this entry said both were hidden because the sweep matched `no common ancestor` while ADR 0008 says `shares no ancestor`. That was itself an unverified claim, and the second reviewer refuted it from the repo: the two sites #408 **did** correct (`server.mjs:2592`, `:2679` at `2593eb1^`) say `share NO ancestor`, and the two it **missed** say `share **no ancestor**` and `shares no ancestor`. A grep for `no common ancestor` matches **none of the four**, so it cannot have been the discriminator; a grep for `no ancestor` matches **all four**. The phrase does not separate found from missed. What the evidence actually supports:

    - **ADR 0008 — a SCOPE gap, not a pattern gap.** `git show 2593eb1 --name-only` lists seven files and `docs/adr/0008-tui-warm-pane-pool.md` is not among them. No pattern was ever run against it, so no pattern could have found it. The claim reached the file through #404's amendment, which landed the same day.
    - **This CHANGELOG's `#379` entry — not hidden at all; KNOWN and left unmarked.** #408's own entry quotes that site's distinctive phrase (*"is not usable evidence"*) while counting it among the corrected three. A site you quote is a site you found.

    **The honest lesson is therefore narrower and less flattering than a grep story**: correcting a claim in the narrative of a *later* entry, without a marker at the site, is indistinguishable to a reader from not having corrected it — and indistinguishable to the *author* from having finished. An earlier draft also called this "the identical shape as this cycle's `ocp health` finding". It is not: `ocp health` genuinely was invisible to a field-name grep, because a format-agnostic passthrough contains no field name. That parallel is withdrawn.

    **The honest count is two found, and a third cannot be ruled out.** Any statement of the claim's *consequence* rather than the claim itself carries none of the swept tokens, and that is exactly the form of the first one. Treat this as a floor, not a total.

    The original entry's substance follows, unchanged and still correct:

    Three `server.mjs`/CHANGELOG statements asserted that `v3.16.4` and `main` "share NO ancestor" (one naming "roots 593d0dc vs c180987"), that the public history had been "recreated as a squashed root" on 2026-07-27, and that `git merge-base` was therefore "empty" — concluding that `git log -S` on `main` "is not usable evidence" and that two external contributors' branches could not be merged at all. **Measured in a full clone:** the repository has **one** root, `593d0dc`; `git merge-base $(git rev-list -n1 v3.16.4) origin/main` returns `9e25160`, so v3.16.4 **is** an ancestor of `main`; `c180987` is an ordinary 2-file, 28-insertion docs commit with parent `f2f9058`; `git log -S` on `main` names `879b40f` correctly; and `git merge-base origin/main` against both fork heads returns `bdb6662` (v3.24.0). **There is one history and no recreation.**

    **The mechanism, because it is what makes the artifact convincing rather than obvious.** The checkout it was read from was a **shallow clone**, with one boundary commit in `.git/shallow`. Reproduced from a fresh `git clone --depth=103` — the maintainer's checkout has since been `git fetch --unshallow`ed and no longer shows any of this:

    - **The boundary commit's object still knows its parent; only the graph does not.** `git log -1 --format=%p <boundary>` prints an *empty* parent list, while `git cat-file -p <boundary>` on the very same object shows **one** `parent` line.
    - **`git merge-base` across the boundary exits `1` with empty stdout *and empty stderr*** — byte-identical to how git reports a genuine absence of any common ancestor. Nothing distinguishes "there is none" from "I cannot walk far enough to answer", and the empty output was read as the first.
    - **`git log --all` is not the cure.** It returns the byte-identical wrong line that `git log -S origin/main` does, because both walk the same truncated graph — and it never errors, which is why it reads as confirmation. The cure is `git fetch --unshallow`.

    The two `server.mjs` comments' *conclusions* were independently sound (both rest on `git show` against a resolved tag, which is correct regardless) and are preserved verbatim; only the stated reason changed. The two CHANGELOG entries above are corrected in scope: the false factual clause only, leaving how the contributors' work was handled as written. Folded in under Iron Rule 11 §11.1 rather than deferred, because a comment that instructs the next contributor in the wrong method is the defect ADR 0017 exists to record — and because this one had already cost two contributors a merge that would have worked.
  - **No Class B.2 response key path moves; ADR 0012 is not invoked.** This changes which **requests** are accepted, not what any response contains. `docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json` is byte-identical and `npm test`'s key-set check passes unchanged — the snapshot's probe for this endpoint sends `{ name: "b2-key-snapshot" }`, an object, so it is admitted before and after. No field is added, so there is nothing for ADR 0012 to authorize.

- **`OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE`'s hard cap `POOL_MAX_SIZE` raised `4 → 32` — authored by [@sumlin](https://github.com/sumlin) in [#189](https://github.com/dtzp555-max/ocp/pull/189), re-created here with `Co-Authored-By` attribution.** The original cap of 4 aimed to protect a small host from too many resident idle `claude` processes, but the operator already controls the pool size directly via `OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE`, so the low hard cap protected nobody who couldn't already protect themselves — it only stopped an operator on capable hardware from using more of their own RAM. The cap's remaining purpose is unchanged: bound a fat-fingered value (`OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE=99999999`) into a bounded number rather than a fork bomb. The pool stays **default-off**, and the "keep it small on a small host" guidance stays in the docs. **Not endpoint-touching**: the only `server.mjs` change is one word in the env-var header comment (`max 4` → `max 32`); no request handler, no response shape, no wire call.
  - **Amends [ADR 0008](docs/adr/0008-tui-warm-pane-pool.md)**, which stated the cap in its Decision *and* again in its Consequences. Both were updated, plus a dated amendment block under Decision carrying the contributor's own argument verbatim. The cap is asserted in five files; each was found by grep (`POOL_MAX_SIZE`, `max 4`, `= 4`) rather than from memory.
  - **Why it was re-created rather than merged — CORRECTED 2026-08-11.** #189 branched from `bdb6662` (v3.24.0) on 2026-07-21, and this entry said the public history had been recreated as a squashed root on 2026-07-27, leaving `git merge-base origin/main db8cc6a` **empty**. That is false. Measured in a full clone after `git fetch --unshallow`: `git merge-base origin/main db8cc6a` returns **`bdb6662`**, the same base the branch names, and the repository has a single root `593d0dc`. **There is one history and no recreation**; the branch is an ordinary descendant of v3.24.0. What three weeks of movement actually produced were **content conflicts**, which a rebase would have resolved — the re-creation was not necessary. The mistake was the project maintainer's, and the mechanism is recorded in the ADR 0017 entry below. Corrections have been posted on #189; reopening remains available if that record is preferred.
  - **The cap's VALUE was not pinned by any test, on `main` or in #189** — the existing assertion is `resolvePoolSize("99") === POOL_MAX_SIZE`, which compares against the constant and therefore stays green at 4, at 32 or at 7. A behavioural pin (`resolvePoolSize("99999999") === 32`) is added in its own `test()` so neither it nor the symbolic assertion can absorb a mutation aimed at the other; mutation-proven both ways in the PR body.
  - **On upgrade, a host that ALREADY sets `OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE` above 4 will hold that many warm panes instead of 4** — e.g. `=8` goes from 4 resident idle `claude` processes to 8, with no config change of the operator's own. This is the one genuine behavioural consequence of the change and it is observable either way (the boot banner prints `TUI warm pool: ON size=N`, and `/health`'s `tui.pool.size` reports the effective value). "Default-off" is true for anyone who never set the variable and does not describe this population; TUI mode is single-user and opt-in, so that population is small, but it is not empty.
  - **Corrected a standing-cost statement this change multiplies by 8 (found by independent review, folded in under Iron Rule 11 §11.1).** `server.mjs`'s reap-tick comment and ADR 0008's Decision §3 both said the pre-sweep drain *"costs one pane re-boot per tick"*. Verified from source that it never did: `drain()` drops **every** warm pane and `resume()` → `refill()` re-boots serially back to `OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE`, so the cost always scaled with pool size. At the old cap the sentence was off by at most 4×; at 32 it understates an idle instance's standing background work by 8×. Both statements are now size-relative. The stale wording states the cap's *consequence* rather than the cap, which is why the `POOL_MAX_SIZE` / `max 4` / `= 4` grep could not have found it — recorded because that is the reusable lesson, not the fix.
  - **No Class B.2 response key path moves — and the snapshot's own blind-spot note now says so at the new ceiling.** Every recorded key path is byte-identical (both `probes` and `probesTuiPool` blocks unchanged; `npm test` fails on any difference). What *did* change is prose: the `notCovered` entry — and its duplicate in `scripts/b2-key-snapshot.mjs`'s `B2_PROFILES` jsdoc — asserted `pool=4`, which read as a **boundary** measurement precisely because 4 was the cap, and becomes a mid-range datum at a cap of 32. That entry is prospective (it states what a green run does *not* cover), so this change is what made it stale, and it is annotated here rather than deferred.
    - **Re-measured, with the control stated.** `OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE=32` against the same fixture (boot banner `TUI warm pool: ON size=32`) gives **200 key paths against the 200 recorded at pool=1, diff empty**. A control compared that same probe against the `probes` block, which is known to differ, and drift *was* reported — so the null is a result. This is recorded because the first attempt printed a bogus total from a **wrong field name** (`keyPaths` for `keys`), and a differ that reports no difference because it is reading the wrong field is exactly the failure mode that block exists to warn about.
    - **Tagged `[measured, except where marked]`**, per the file's own convention, because it carries claims established differently and each is marked inline. Beside the wire probe: executing `TuiPanePool.stats()` at sizes 1, 4 and 32 returns the **same 10 scalar keys** every time (`[measured]`), and the source shows why it cannot vary — a fixed object literal with no size-conditional key (`[reasoned]`). Three independent routes to the same conclusion. The `stats()` clause was **first written `[reasoned]` and corrected to `[measured]`** once it was actually executed rather than only read: under-claiming provenance is the inverse of the #354 defect this tagging exists to prevent, but it is still a wrong answer to the one question the tag is asked. Found by independent review — the jsdoc copy spells it `pool=4`, which the `POOL_MAX_SIZE` / `max 4` / `= 4` grep structurally could not match.

- **The post-restart verdict is now ONE classification shared by both `ocp update` paths (#373).** `runFullUpgrade` and `runRollback` each carried their own copy of an eight-arm verdict ladder, and the duplication had already produced one real divergence (#372: the answering-but-wrong-version arm existed on one path and not the other). `classifyRestartOutcome` now owns the classification and its precedence; each path keeps its own **wording**, which genuinely differs and must keep differing (the rollback path cannot tell an operator to run the rollback).
  - **What the ordering constraint actually is — corrected in review, because the obvious justification is out of date and the change that dated it was this one's own predecessor.** #351 recorded *"Ordering is load-bearing. The local-probe-fault arm is checked first, because it is also a non-ok probe."* That was exact while the DOWN cell was `restartFailure && !postFlight.ok` — an `else` that genuinely **overlapped** the local-fault arm. #372/#381 re-keyed it to `didNotReach`, so the restart-failure arms are now **disjoint** and their relative order is free: measured exhaustively over the classifier's complete 80-input domain, swapping any adjacent pair — or reversing all four — is a **no-op**. What still order-depends is narrower: `UNMEASURED` first, the two bare catch-alls last, and `PROBE_LOCAL_FAULT` after its restart-failure sibling, which is the one overlapping pair left and the only surviving descendant of #351's incident.
  - **Callers do still state one ordering.** Each keeps a catch-all written as `verdict !== OK && verdict !== WARNED_SUCCESS`, and because that is a bare test rather than an equality test its position is load-bearing — moving `runRollback`'s above its own `PROBE_LOCAL_FAULT` cell changes the rollback lane only, which is the #372 drift shape recreated. The classification's ordering is shared; the anti-drift test holds the two callers' catch-alls consistent.
  - **Behaviour-preserving, proved from the wire rather than from the diff** (ADR 0015's method, which landed the same day and rejected a refactor for counting text instead of tracing behaviour): both paths were driven over the cross-product of {2 platforms} × {restart failed or not} × {13 probe outcomes} × {2 lanes} = 104 cases, plus 4 rows exercising the no-probe lane — **108 in total** — and every message, hint and phase they emit was captured before and after. The dumps are byte-identical. (The arithmetic is spelled out because an earlier draft wrote the product as three factors multiplying to 52 while quoting the correct total; a number is a claim.)
