  - **That sweep caught a real regression the refactor itself introduced, and no unit test would have.** `if (!postFlight.ok)` was an `else` and caught every non-ok probe by construction; equality tests on a verdict are exhaustive **by enumeration**, so a verdict no arm names falls past every cell into the function's `return` — a *successful* rollback reported for a host whose probe failed. It happened for `not-ok-status`, which the rollback path deliberately gives no cell. Both neutral arms are now written as "not a success verdict", refusing a future verdict by default, and the property has its own sweep test. The defect lived in which states are **unreachable**, not in what any reachable state does — which is why a case-per-cell test could not see it.

- **`execRestartRetry` turned a missing runner into "THE PROXY IS DOWN" (#356 F6).** `attempts`, `backoffMs`, `log` and `sleep` all had defaults; `run` did not — so calling without one threw `TypeError: run is not a function` on every attempt, and the `catch` treated that exactly like a command failure: three attempts, rising backoff, then a failure verdict carrying the TypeError's text as the operator-facing detail. It now throws immediately. Deliberately **not** defaulted to a real shell: a test that forgot to inject would then run real `launchctl`/`systemctl`, which is precisely how #217's review took production down.

- **The restoration pass no longer abandons the command that starts the service (#356 F4).** Both restore loops stopped at the first failure. That is the right direction for the *restart* loop — continuing past a failed tear-down is meaningless — and the wrong one for a *restore*, whose whole job is getting the service back up. Latent rather than live: on every current plan shape the `break` could not skip anything (launchd's tear-down carries `|| true`; every systemd shape is one command), and it stops being latent the moment a plan gains a third command. Both loops now run every command and report the aggregate; `restoreOutcome.ok` keeps its meaning.

- **Two judgement calls resolved by writing down why, which is what #356 exists for (F3, F7).**
  - **The restoration pass now RETRIES on launchd, and still gets one attempt on systemd — decided per plan shape, and the decision was reversed in review on measurement.** The obvious justification is systemd-only: on a **user unit**, retrying would put six of our own invocations inside systemd's documented 5-starts-per-10s default, and a tripped limit latches the unit `failed` so that a plain `systemctl restart` keeps failing — the printed recovery command would itself stop working. On a **discovered system unit** OCP never reads the unit's `Restart=` or `StartLimit*`, so one attempt is the conservative choice. Neither applies to **launchd**, which has no start limit at all — and launchd is not a corner case here, it is the shape of the production incident this whole feature exists for. There, a succeeded `bootout` unloads the job and nothing brings it back, so the restoration pass is the **last automated chance**, and the command its retries repeat is `bootstrap` — the one that failed transiently in that incident and succeeded by hand moments later. A second proposed reason ("retrying would re-run the tear-down against a service that may have come up in between") was **measured false**: `execRestartRetry` retries one command, the restore's `bootout` succeeds on its first attempt, and raising the budget leaves the bootout count unchanged at 2 while `bootstrap` goes 4 → 6. The retries inherit the rising backoff too, since three instantaneous attempts would be the low-yield immediate retry this decision rejects.
  - **That rising backoff was asserted to be load-bearing and was not actually pinned anywhere in the wire path (found by independent review, round 3).** The mutation offered as proof discriminated at the **helper** — `restoreRetryBudget`'s returned `backoffMs`, caught by its own `deepEqual`. The same property mutated one layer out, at the call site (`{ ...restoreBudget, backoffMs: 0, run: runShell }`), left the whole suite green. The cause was structural rather than a missed case: `restartBackoffMs` appeared in the entire suite exactly twice, in the two shared fixtures, and both pinned it to `0` so the suite does not sleep — so a **non-zero restore backoff was unobservable by construction**, and a refactor could have dropped the delay while keeping the retry count. Now pinned behaviourally through `execRestartRetry`'s own operator-facing message, which is formatted from the delay it is about to sleep for precisely so it cannot misreport it: a launchd run at `restartBackoffMs: 50` asserts the restore's retry lines read `0.05s` then `0.1s`. The two mutations now cover distinct layers — the call-site one reddens only the new test, the helper one reddens the new test **and** the existing `deepEqual`.
  - **The asymmetry argument's conclusion held, but it was cited to an authority that says the opposite (same review).** The conclusion — a spurious retry in the restore cannot produce a wrong verdict — is correct, and the stated reason was not: *"the verdict comes from the post-flight probe and never from a command's exit status (#325's rule)"*. `restartFailure` **is** a command exit status and it gates five of `classifyRestartOutcome`'s ten arms; and #325 bans the exit status from **substituting** for the probe, then forty lines later keeps it as an explicit **co-input** (`if [[ $probe_rc -ne 0 && $restart_failed -ne 0 ]]`, under *"the probe and the command outcome are two independent facts"*). The cited authority contained the counterexample to the sentence citing it. Replaced with the true and narrower premise: *the restore's* exit statuses are not classifier inputs at all, and `restartFailure` is already fixed before the restore runs. Also fixed in the same pass: `restoreRetryBudget` and its rationale had been inserted **between** `recoveryPlanCommands`' doc comment and `recoveryPlanCommands` itself, so in a file where the comments are the deliverable each function sat under the other's rationale. Comments and tests only — no behaviour change.
  - **"Success with no measurement" stays expressible for the all-mock bookkeeping lane only.** It is unreachable in production (the post-flight gate is `opts.mockProbe || !opts.mockExec`, and `mockExec` is test-only), the dangerous half already throws, the absence of a measurement is *visible* in the result as `post-flight: skipped-mock` rather than implied, and ~40 existing tests depend on that lane for phase/target bookkeeping rather than for a service verdict. **Found while deciding it: `runFullUpgrade`'s "restart failed and nothing measured" arm had no test at all** — #352's LOW-4 covers the rollback twin only. An arm with *no* test is a different and more serious thing than an arm with a weak one, and it is exactly the shape F7 warns about: an untested throw is one refactor from returning success on a host nobody looked at. It now has one.

- **Not endpoint-touching.** `scripts/upgrade.mjs` is the `ocp update` CLI dispatcher; `server.mjs` is untouched by this change. No `ALIGNMENT.md` rule is invoked and no ADR is cited, because none governs this surface.
- **The Class B.2 key-set snapshot now records TWO configuration profiles, closing the 12 unguarded key paths #357 named — 10 of them inside `/health`'s `tui.pool` (#357).** The mechanism booted `server.mjs` against one fixture, so any response shape reachable only under a different configuration was invisible to the primary ADR 0012 audit. `tui.pool`'s contents come straight from `TuiPanePool.stats()` — a counter bag that grows whenever someone instruments the warm-pane path — so it is precisely where the next additive field is likely to land, and a field added there would have shipped with a green `npm test`.
  - **NEW COVERAGE, NOT NEW SURFACE — and the diff proves it rather than asserting it.** The `probes` block (the default profile) is **byte-identical** to its previous contents; the snapshot diff is **331 added lines and 2 removed**, and **every removed line is `notCovered` prose, not a key path** (`git diff --numstat`; earlier drafts of this bullet said 318 and then 328 — the figure moves whenever the prose block is edited, so it is re-measured at each push rather than carried forward, because numbers are claims too). No B.2 response gained, lost or renamed a field in this PR. ADR 0012 is not engaged: nothing was added to any endpoint. `server.mjs` is not modified — not endpoint-touching.
  - **Two profiles, not one and not eight, and the sufficiency was measured.** `probes` pins TUI mode, the warm pool and skip-permissions all OFF; `probesTuiPool` turns all three ON (`CLAUDE_TUI_MODE=true`, `OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE=1`, `CLAUDE_SKIP_PERMISSIONS=true`). The three settings are independent — `TUI_MODE` decides `spawn.reason`, `SKIP_PERMISSIONS` decides `config.allowedTools[]`, and `tui.pool` needs `TUI_MODE && OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE >= 1` — so the two corners *bracket* every combination instead of sampling it. Verified by booting the five reachable variants (TUI alone; TUI + pool=1; skip-permissions alone; all three at pool=1; all three at pool=4) and probing all 16 pairs under each: **no key path appeared that is absent from both blocks.** `pool=4` records the same key set as `pool=1`, because the size is a value.
  - **`/health` is the only endpoint whose key set differs by CONFIGURATION, and that was measured across all 16 pairs rather than assumed from reading `/status`.** The variant's other deltas are caused by the absent warm-up traffic, not by the config — the same deltas appear under the skip-permissions-only variant, which is not in TUI mode at all.
  - **Both blocks get the full treatment: all 16 pairs, two fresh boots for stability, and their own `ALIGNMENT.md` inventory coverage check.** A second profile must not become a place a B.2 endpoint can hide, so the constitution check is applied per profile rather than once. The suite also asserts the two profiles genuinely *differ*, keyed on the 12 named paths — a variant whose environment silently stopped being applied would otherwise pass every other assertion by recording the default shape twice.
  - **Neither profile runs a real `claude` pane, and the suite proves it from the wire rather than claiming it.** `OCP_TUI_TMUX_BIN` now points at a stub `tmux` that appends its argv to a log and exits 1; the test asserts the only invocation in either profile is `list-sessions`. Two measurements forced this. First, with warm-up traffic under `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE=true` the two `POST /v1/chat/completions` calls issued **four `tmux new-session` commands**, each launching the configured `claude` binary in a pane — so the variant profile runs no warm-up. Second, with the **real** `tmux` a TUI-mode boot reaches `reapStaleTuiSessions({ includeLegacy: true })`, which **kill-sessions every legacy-named `ocp-tui-<8hex>` session even when ordinary sessions are present** — that is someone's running pane — and additionally reaches **`tmux kill-server`** only in the narrower case where no *foreign* session remains. An earlier draft of this bullet said `npm test` "would have destroyed the operator's whole tmux server"; **that was wrong**, and the measured scenario table is two bullets down.
  - **The release-audit instruction is updated in the same PR that would otherwise falsify it.** `CLAUDE.md`'s `how:` clause told a releaser that *"added lines are new B.2 surface"*. After this PR that diff is **331 added lines carrying 221 key paths, and not one is new surface** — so followed literally at the next release it would produce **221 phantom ADR 0012 condition-5 failures**. `report:`'s whole value rests on *"silence is a result"*, and a 221-item false alarm at the first release after this change is precisely what teaches a releaser to discount the mechanism — the failure mode ADR 0012 names by hand. `how:` now distinguishes a key path added **inside an existing block** (surface) from **a whole new profile block** (coverage), `report:` says a new block is reported as one line naming the block and its PR rather than as N additions, and `baseline:` records this one-time addition as **0 new surface, cumulative ADR 0012 count unchanged at 2**.
  - **The `tmux kill-server` claim was overstated, and is retagged.** An earlier draft said, under a `[measured]` tag, that with the real `tmux` a TUI-mode boot *"would destroy the operator's whole tmux server on every `npm test`"*. Measured properly — by feeding a fake `tmux` to the **real** `reapStaleTuiSessions`, which is the only way to establish it, since the stub means a real one never runs — `lib/tui/session.mjs`'s `!othersRemain && sparedLive === 0` gate means **any ordinary session present stops `kill-server`**: normal sessions → no `kill-server`; **only** a legacy `ocp-tui-<8hex>` session → it fires; legacy **plus** a normal session → `kill-session` fires and `kill-server` does not; another OCP on a different port → neither; no sessions at all → `kill-server` fires with nothing to lose; **this instance's own port-scoped `ocp-tui-<port>-*` sessions → both fire** — the row that shows "ours" is a *superset* of "legacy-shaped" (`isOwn || isLegacyOwn`), which is what makes the `othersRemain` gate legible. That last row is unreachable under `npm test`, which draws a random port, so it is documentation rather than a live hazard. **The mitigation stands on the narrower fact**: `kill-session` on a live legacy-named session is reachable *with* foreign sessions present, and that is someone's running pane. The entry now carries how each half was established, which is what the `[measured]`/`[reasoned]` tagging exists for.
  - **A profile cannot choose the tmux binary, by construction.** The pin was applied *before* the `...profile.env` spread, under a comment advertising that a profile may override "any pin above" — and since `expectTmuxCalls` is author-chosen per profile too, a future profile could have pointed at the real `tmux` and set a matching count with every guard still passing. The pin is now applied **after** the spread, and `makeB2Fixture` **throws** if a profile carries that key: last-write-wins makes it unreachable, the throw makes the attempt loud rather than silently ignored. **Only the throw is enforced by test** — measured: restoring the old ordering with the throw in place *survives* the suite, because no profile can then reach the spread carrying the key. The ordering is kept as the by-construction half, and would still hold if the throw were later deleted as "unreachable", but this entry does not claim more than that.
  - **Scope, narrowed after review.** This pins the tmux binary for the **snapshot mechanism**. It does not make `npm test` as a whole unable to touch a real `tmux` — two other live-server tests boot `server.mjs` in TUI mode with no such pin. Pre-existing, invisible on CI, filed as **#384** rather than folded in, because stubbing at one of those sites can change what its TUI turn does and that is its own unit of work.
  - **The cost of that bound is stated, not hidden.** Without warm-up traffic the variant block records no element keys for `/api/usage`'s `byKey[]`/`recent[]`/`timeline[]` or for `recentErrors[]` on `/health` and `/status` — 26 paths that the default block does record, so a field added to any of them is still caught. Both this and the remaining blind spot (a *future* shape-deciding setting that both profiles happen to pin identically) are new `[measured]`-tagged entries in the snapshot's `notCovered` block, and the superseded entry is replaced rather than left describing a gap that is now closed.
- **An unvalidated JSON body spent real quota and minted a real API key; a `null` body left the request unanswered forever (#360).** One root cause, three consequences of decreasing severity and increasing visibility. `JSON.parse` verifies **syntax**, not that the result is a non-null object — `JSON.parse("null")` succeeds — and the four body-reading handlers then indexed the result without checking its shape.
  - **Quota burn: a body carrying no messages became a real, billed upstream call.** `POST /v1/chat/completions` with `42`, `"a string"`, `true`, `[]` or `[1,2]` did **not** throw — property access on a primitive boxes rather than throwing, so `parsed.messages`, `parsed.input` and `parsed.prompt` were all `undefined`, the `||` chain manufactured `[{ role: "user", content: "" }]`, and the request proceeded to a metered `claude` spawn returning **HTTP 200**. Measured: **one spawn per request.** No 4xx, no warning field, `finish_reason: "stop"` — every observable said the request succeeded. This is the worst of the three: a client can send it by accident and the operator pays for it, and nothing in the logs distinguishes it from a legitimate turn.
  - **Widened in review: the scalar guard closed the scalar door, not the door.** `{}`, `{"messages":null}`, `{"messages":0}` and `{"model":"haiku"}` are all **objects**, all pass a shape guard, and every one still reached the `||` chain and bought a spawn (measured: `200`, `spawns=1` each). `{"model":"haiku"}` is a far more plausible accidental client body than `42`, so fixing only the scalars would have removed six bodies and left an unbounded family. The guard now turns on the condition the `||` chain itself turns on — none of `messages`/`input`/`prompt` truthy — so the only bodies refused are the ones whose sole remaining content is an empty prompt. `{"messages":[]}` and `{"messages":42}` are untouched and keep the existing `'messages' must be a non-empty array` answer, and `{"prompt":"…"}` / `{"input":[…]}` still reach the model, each asserted to still spawn.
  - **Credential surface: a body that was never validated created a real key.** `POST /api/keys` with any non-null scalar returned **201 and minted a key**, because `parsed.name` was `undefined` and the `||` fell through to the `key-${Date.now()}` auto-name. The generated name is server-side and the supplied-name path is still regex-validated, so this is an API-contract and hygiene defect rather than an injection one — but a credential created from a body the server never agreed to parse should not be a 201. **Filed as #383 and deliberately not fixed here**: those scalars are *answered* today, so tightening them changes which requests a grandfathered B.2 endpoint accepts. Contrast the chat route, where the same underlying bug **is** fixed because ADR 0006's grandfather provision explicitly does not extend to B.1 and OpenAI's spec compels the 400 — same bug, two authorities, two answers.
  - **The hang: `null` threw, and nothing answered.** `parsed.messages`, `Object.entries(updates)`, `parsed.name` and `k in quotaBody` each throw on `null`, and **the throw is never answered** — Node does not observe the promise an `async` request callback returns, so the process-level `unhandledRejection` listener logs it and nothing writes a response or closes the connection. Measured: zero bytes received, socket still open, until the client gave up. **`PATCH /api/keys/:id/quota` hung on all four primitives, not just `null`** (the `in` operator requires an object on its right), where the issue reported one.
  - **`PATCH /settings` is the sharpest site: the guard EXISTED and `null` defeated it.** `typeof updates !== "object" || Array.isArray(updates)` reads as complete, and `typeof null === "object"` walks straight past it into `Object.entries(null)`. Every other non-object already landed on that guard's 400, so this was not "validation was forgotten" — it was a validation that reads as complete and is not.
  - **The guards are now stated POSITIVELY, because the negation is what failed.** `typeof x !== "object"` tests for the *absence* of a type tag, and `null` is not absent from that tag. Two named predicates replace the hand-rolled negations and say what the next line actually needs: `isJsonObject` (safe to `Object.entries`; excludes arrays) and the weaker `isLegalInOperand` (named for exactly what it certifies: `k in v` requires an object on its right, and arrays satisfy that). Two rather than one because the four handlers require different things and collapsing them would silently widen two of them. `POST /api/keys` deliberately uses neither: it tests `parsed === null`, an equality against the exact value observed to break, which is the strongest form available where every wider predicate would capture inputs this change must not touch. The null case is now decided **once** instead of re-remembered per site, which is how a fifth reader would otherwise arrive carrying this bug again.
  - **Class B.1 for the chat route.** OpenAI's `chat/completions` defines the request body as an object with a **required** `messages` array (https://platform.openai.com/docs/api-reference/chat/create), authorized by ADR 0006. A scalar or array body carries no `messages`, so 400 is the specified answer and the 200 was not. OCP already returns this exact error shape to the semantically identical `{"messages": 42}`.
  - **Class B.2 for the three admin routes: ADR 0006 (grandfathered as of v3.16.4), route (a).** **No field is added, removed, renamed, retyped or re-ruled**, and no field's documented meaning moves. The rule that decides each response ("the body must be a JSON object") always meant this and was simply not enforced for one value — the same shape as the `stats.activeRequests` worked example in `CLAUDE.md`. **No route gains a new error *shape***: `/settings` reuses its existing 400 and message verbatim, and the two key routes reuse the `{ error: "<string>" }` form they already emit for `Invalid JSON`. **ADR 0012 is not engaged — no response field is added** — and `docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json` is unchanged.
  - **Scope is deliberately narrower than "reject every non-object", and the narrowness carries its own assertions.** The rule applied was: *only inputs that today receive **no response** change behaviour.* So the credential-minting 201 is **named but not fixed here** — tightening it changes *which requests a grandfathered B.2 endpoint accepts*, i.e. a request-shape change that `ALIGNMENT.md:114` makes a new authorization request needing its own ADR. Likewise the quota guard deliberately does not capture arrays, so their existing "Provide at least one of…" answer is untouched. Both unchanged arms are pinned by tests, so the decision cannot be mistaken for an oversight or silently widened later. The one deviation is B.1, where the authority is OpenAI's spec rather than a v3.16.4 snapshot and the spec makes the 400 mandatory.
  - **The tests assert the CLOCK before the status, because the defect is silence.** A test checking only for 4xx would pass against a handler that answers by luck and would misreport an unanswered socket as a status mismatch. Every probe uses the existing `ltRawSend` raw-socket client with a bounded deadline and asserts `!timedOut` first, with its own message; the mutation table records each guard's removal reddening the **timeout** arm. A spawn counter fed by the fake `claude` binary makes the quota-burn half non-vacuous — a guard that returned 400 but still spawned would satisfy every status assertion and still bill the operator, which the missing-`return` mutation proves can fail on its own.
  - **Four more unanswered requests exist and are reported, not fixed here.** Probing found **four more currently reachable** ones that no JSON-shape guard can reach, from three unrelated causes — `URIError` from `decodeURIComponent` in the **router itself**, a SQLite `datatype mismatch` from `parseInt("%") → NaN`, and a null **element** in `{"messages":[null]}`. One of them, `GET /api/usage?limit=%`, is a **bare GET with no body and no credentials**, because `/api/usage` has no `isAdmin` gate at all. So the issue's "leaves the mechanism intact for the fifth" understates it: the fifth through eighth already exist. They are filed as their own issue: three causes across two citation classes is at least two PRs, not one.
  - **The obvious general fix is the one ADR 0015 already rejected, and this PR does not re-propose it.** Those four make "one request-lifecycle wrapper — every request answered exactly once" look inevitable, and ADR 0015 names that re-derivation as its reason for existing: *"Anyone reading `server.mjs` with fresh eyes will reach 'these six lanes should share one lifecycle wrapper' within an hour. Reaching it without the six corrections below repeats a rejected design."* The three-boundary refactor is **not approved as drafted**; narrow, individually-reviewable fixes are the approved path (ADR 0015 § Decision), and correction 5 covers exactly this shape — *"Several defects the program was meant to fix have local seams that do not require the program."* The follow-up issue lists the local seam and class evidence per route instead.

- **The install-marker check was type-blind, and on a standard install `$HOME` was ONE stray file away from `rm -rf $HOME` (#366).** `classifyInstallDir` scored a candidate directory with `entries.includes(marker)` and no `stat`, so the name match said "this file exists" while meaning only "this name exists". A marker must now `statSync` to a regular file.
  - **Read this part before deciding whether to take the release.** README documents the install at `~/ocp`, so on a standard host **`$HOME` already contains a *directory* named `ocp`** — which the old name-only check scored as a free marker. Half the two-marker threshold was therefore already spent before anything went wrong, and a **single** stray `models.json`, `server.mjs` or `setup.mjs` in `$HOME` completed it. `OCP_DIR=$HOME` plus `--fresh-install --yes` would then have run `rm -rf $HOME`. Measured against the documented layout, pre-fix vs post-fix:

    ```
    PRE-FIX  (4022be4): markers=["ocp","models.json"]  isInstall=true   safeToReplace=true
    POST-FIX (this PR): markers=["models.json"]        isInstall=false  safeToReplace=false
    ```

    Identical for a stray `server.mjs` and a stray `setup.mjs`. **That is one typo, not the two contrived directories the issue described** — #366 judged the trigger "not a realistic accident" and this entry's first draft repeated that framing; both were wrong, and an independent review corrected them. The two-marker-directories case in the issue title is the *demonstration*; `$HOME` is the exposure.
  - **Not endpoint-touching.** `server.mjs` is not modified; the change is `scripts/lib/install-dir.mjs` plus its tests. No endpoint class, no ADR, no `cli.js` citation is engaged — the PR template's fourth option.
  - **Why a `/opt`-shaped directory was already refused, and why that matters.** Before this change it scored exactly **one** marker (`ocp/`, a directory), so `OCP_DIR=/opt` — on the very host #350 exists to serve — was caught by the **threshold of 2, not by any type check**. That makes the threshold load-bearing in a way #350 never claimed. The type check does not replace it: the two guard *different* cases, and mutation **M2** (`>= 2` → `>= 1`) pins the threshold's own case, which is a directory holding exactly one **real marker file** — the stray-`server.mjs`-in-`$HOME` shape from #348. `/opt` is now refused by both, scoring 0 where it scored 1.
  - **Two shapes beyond the reported one were also scoring, both found while measuring.** A **dangling symlink** named `server.mjs` counted as a marker (`existsSync` would have said false, but nothing was calling it), and so did a **symlink to a directory**. Both now score zero, because `statSync` throws `ENOENT` on the first and reports `isDirectory()` on the second.
  - **Symlinks to regular files still count — a deliberate choice, on one argument rather than two.** `statSync` follows, which asks the same question the `package.json` arm two lines above already asks with `readFileSync`, so the two arms of one function agree. A first draft added that a symlinked `ocp` is "a real shape that must keep working"; **review found nothing supporting that and it is withdrawn** — `git ls-files -s` has zero mode-`120000` entries, all four markers are `100644`/`100755`, and installs come from `git clone`, which cannot produce a symlinked marker. (The one documented symlink points *inward from outside*, `ln -sf ~/ocp/ocp /usr/local/bin/ocp`, which this function never inspects.) `readdirSync(…, { withFileTypes: true })` is still rejected, because measured on macOS APFS a symlink's `Dirent` reports `isSymbolicLink()` and `isFile() === false` — it would answer a different question from the `package.json` arm.
  - **The `entries.includes()` test is KEPT alongside the stat, and that is not redundancy.** Measured on macOS APFS: for an entry actually named `SERVER.MJS`, `entries.includes("server.mjs")` is `false` while `statSync(dir + "/server.mjs")` succeeds with `isFile() === true`. Dropping the entries test would therefore **widen** what scores as an OCP install on any case-insensitive filesystem — the opposite of this fix's direction, on the guard whose false positive is an `rm -rf` argument. Pinned by mutation **M3 — but only on a case-insensitive filesystem. CI is `ubuntu-latest`/ext4, where the two conditions agree and M3 SURVIVES**, so this conjunct is real, load-bearing, and *unprotected by the only automated gate*. The test now reports itself as a **`⊘ SKIP`** on a case-sensitive volume rather than passing quietly, and `npm test` prints a skipped count **on its own line** — `=== Results: N passed, M failed ===` is left byte-identical, because `.github/workflows/flake-hunt.yml` greps it at three sites and two of them are anchored on the suffix `', 0 failed ==='`. A first cut appended `, N skipped` inside that line and broke both: measured on real logs, a three-run hunt in which every run was clean reported `clean (0 failed) | 0 / 3` and attached a spurious log section — the workflow that exists to measure flakiness reporting 100% flake on a clean run. Found in review.
  - **A marker that cannot be stat'd no longer counts — and it is now DIAGNOSED, not misreported.** On a directory that is readable but not executable (mode 444), `readdirSync` lists all four markers while `statSync` on each throws `EACCES`, so a genuine install moves from `safeToReplace = true` to `false`. Refusing is right. But the first cut used a boolean, which collapsed "I looked and it is not a file" into "I could not look", and the tool then told an operator with a permission-locked install that it **"is NOT an OCP install"** and advised **removing it by hand** — the one action that loses it. Found in review. `classifyMarkerEntry` now returns `file` / `not-a-file` / `unreadable`, the record carries `unreadableMarkers`, and the refusal names the permission bit and says *do NOT delete it*. **The verdict is unchanged; only the diagnosis is.**
  - **Why that state cannot be confused with a real "not an install", structurally rather than by observation.** `statSync` on a child and `readFileSync` on `package.json` both require **search (`x`)** permission on the directory, so no permission state can fail the marker stats while leaving the `package.json` arm working. Measured across modes `755`/`744`/`644`/`544`/`444`/`311`/`111`: `stat=EACCES` never co-occurs with `readFile(package.json)=ok`. Dropping `x` (`644`, `444`) fails both; dropping `r` (`311`, `111`) fails `readdirSync` instead and hits the pre-existing "could not be inspected" branch. The gap this fills is exactly **r-without-x**.
  - **Both consumers of that verdict were fixed too, not just the classifier.** Correcting `why` alone left `doctor.mjs`'s `human_required[2]` and `runFreshInstall`'s refusal still ending with *"If you genuinely want this directory replaced, remove it yourself first"* — unconditionally. The tool then contradicted itself in adjacent lines, and **the destructive instruction was the last thing the operator read**, which is worse than before the `why` was fixed. Both now branch on `unreadableMarkers`, which until this round had no production consumer at all: a permission-locked install is told *do NOT delete this directory*, is pointed at the search/execute bit, and `verify` says the same. A genuinely foreign directory keeps the ordinary advice, pinned by a control test.
  - **A skipped test no longer counts as passed as well.** `testSkipped()` called from inside a `test()` body incremented `skipped`, printed `⊘`, and then let the body return — so `passed++` also ran and the same test printed both marks, which is verbatim the pair the facility exists to prevent. Reachable on any root run (`sudo npm test`, root-by-default containers, and this fleet's Pi and Oracle hosts run system-level units); GitHub's runner is `runner`, so the merge gate never hit it. `test()` now compares the skip counter across the body, the one in-callback caller was hoisted to a top-level `if/else`, and a harness self-check asserts that a skipping body increments `skipped` and **not** `passed`.
  - **`package.json` as a *directory* was already refused** — `readFileSync` throws `EISDIR` and the catch falls through to the marker count — but nothing pinned it, so it now carries a test (mutation **M6**, catch sets `namedPackage = true`).

- **`npm test` could drive the operator's real `tmux`, including `kill-session` on a live legacy-named pane (#384).** `lib/tui/session.mjs:54` resolves `process.env.OCP_TUI_TMUX_BIN || "tmux"` at module load, and two live-server tests booted `server.mjs` with `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE: "true"` and no such variable — `test-features.mjs:2066` (boot only) and `:2521` (boots and POSTs). `ltBoot`'s base env did not pin one either, so the resolution fell through to PATH. Invisible on CI, which has no `tmux`, and on a workstation with no tmux server running.
  - **Measured on the maintainer's host before anything was changed**, by giving both sites a PATH-resolved logging `tmux` in place of the real binary. `:2521` reached it on **every** run with three invocations — the boot reap's `list-sessions`, then one `tmux new-session -d` per request, each launching `claude` in a pane rooted at the operator's real `$HOME/.ocp-tui/work`. `:2066` reached it in **12 of 15** runs (3/5 on a loaded host, 9/10 on a quiet one — the frequency tracks load, the reachability does not): it asserts on the inert-flag warning (`ignored in TUI mode`, `server.mjs:4158` as of `c0e57dc`), printed *before* the boot reap (`:4190`), so whether the reap beats the test's `SIGKILL` is a race — and an already-exec'd `tmux` outlives its parent's `SIGKILL`, so losing that race does not undo it. The `test-features.mjs:2066`/`:2521` numbers are the **pre-change** ones #384 cites, kept so the issue and this entry can be read together.
  - **Pinned in `ltBoot`'s base env, not at the two sites.** Per-site pinning is a rule the next TUI test has to remember, and these two sites are what that looks like when it isn't remembered; `AGENTS.md` asks for constraints that are unreachable by construction. It is applied unconditionally rather than gated on `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE`, because a conditional pin is the same hole one level down and `ltBoot` cannot see which caller will later turn TUI on. No-op for every non-TUI test — all **six** `tmux` call sites are gated (`server.mjs:536`, `:569`, `:570`, `:1018`, `:1949`/`:2126`, `:4190`, as of `c0e57dc`) — and the full suite was run to confirm that rather than assume it.
  - **The stub refuses every subcommand, and the permissive alternative was built and rejected on measurement.** Refusing puts `reapStaleTuiSessions` out at its first guard (`lib/tui/session.mjs:125`), so no `kill-session` and no `kill-server` are ever issued, and makes `bootTuiPane` throw `tui_spawn_failed` at `new-session`. A stub that *succeeds* does the opposite of what it is for: a `list-sessions` exiting 0 with empty output reads as "server up, zero sessions", which satisfies `!othersRemain && sparedLive === 0` (`session.mjs:154`) and **fires `kill-server`** — the hazard reproduced rather than removed. It also buys nothing: `:2521` measured 38 tmux invocations / 11723 ms under the permissive stub against 3 / 936 ms under the refusing one, with the same four assertions passing either way. Its truncation assertions were never at risk, because `messagesToPrompt` is the first statement of `callClaudeTui` (`server.mjs:1905`), ahead of the semaphore and every tmux operation.
  - **The pin is applied after the `...env` spread and a deliberate override is validated, not ignored.** A test may supply its own `tmux`, but only a file it created inside its own scratch dir, checked through `realpath` so a symlink cannot walk out. Anything else throws: silently ignoring an override the author believes took effect is worse than refusing it, and a non-existent path would otherwise masquerade as a refusing stub and hide a typo. **That lane is not speculative — it has a live consumer.** The #346 B.2 key-set integration test calls `ltBootFresh(fx.env, fx.dir)` with a fixture that already sets `OCP_TUI_TMUX_BIN = <fx.dir>/bin/tmux` (#382, `scripts/b2-key-snapshot.mjs:351`), so the containment check runs on **every suite run, once per profile**, and accepts it — measured on the real fixtures. An earlier draft asserted the opposite as a *verification*; it was false, and #393's review caught it. The coupling matters: move `makeB2Fixture`'s `bin/` outside `fx.dir` and the B.2 snapshot test starts throwing from `ltBoot`.
  - **Asserted behaviourally, from the stub's own argv log.** A decoy `tmux` first on PATH supplies the operand that makes the claim falsifiable — an empty stub log alone cannot distinguish "the pin worked" from "the pin is gone and something else ran" — and it is also what makes removing the pin safe to mutation-test, since the calls then land on the decoy instead of the operator's binary. The count is an equality (exactly one `list-sessions`), so a boot that resolved some other binary fails instead of passing on a missing operand.
  - **What this does NOT fix, described accurately because understating it is how a follow-up gets deprioritised (#396).** The refusing stub is *structurally unable* to stop `bootTuiPane`'s filesystem work: `mkdirSync(cwd)` and `prepareTuiHome` both run **before** the `new-session` that the stub then refuses. With `OCP_TUI_HOME` and `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` unset — the state at both TUI sites — `resolveTuiHome` (`lib/tui/session.mjs:284`) returns the operator's **real `$HOME`**, so `ensureTuiCwdTrusted` (`:239`) **reads and atomically rewrites the operator's live `~/.claude.json`**, adding a `projects["$HOME/.ocp-tui/work"]` trust entry. That is the Claude Code config file, not a stray scratch directory, and an earlier draft of this entry called it "filesystem side effects" — understated in the same direction, and the same defect class, as the overstated `kill-server` claim retracted above. Out of scope here (different mechanism, different severity; folding it would cross the line §11.1 says never to cross) and filed as **#396**.
  - **Not endpoint-touching**: `server.mjs` and `lib/tui/` are unmodified.

### Added

- **The TUI LAN boot gate's CALL SITE is pinned, not just the predicate it consults (#370).** `isLoopbackBind` has 8 unit blocks and is correct; what nothing asserted was that `server.mjs:829` still *consults* it. That is #339's shape — a well-tested pure function whose call site can quietly stop calling it — aimed at a security boundary rather than a health field: with the consultation dropped, TUI mode boots network-exposed and the suite stays green. `:829` is three operands, so it is three separate pins, and which arm a mutation kills is stated rather than left to be inferred — the obvious mutation kills the wrong one. Replacing `!isLoopbackBind(...)` with `false` is the "call site stopped consulting the helper" defect and reddens the new refusal test **alone** — one failing test, against a baseline of zero; replacing it with `true` *widens* the gate into refusing **every** live TUI boot in the suite instead, while both new tests correctly survive. Test identities are recorded rather than absolute suite totals, because a total drifts with every unrelated PR that adds a test and a later reader cannot then tell drift from a defect — measured, this entry's own counts went stale across four merges (#389, #390, #395, #393) before the PR landed. The widening row carries no count at all for the same reason: that number is a *census of the suite's TUI boots*, not a property of the gate, and it was measured at 3, then 6, then 7 while this PR was open. What does not drift, and what the row exists to pin, is that both #370 tests survive. The least predictable member of the census is `integration (#346)`'s B.2 key-set snapshot, whose `tui-pool` profile boots TUI mode. Full mutation table in the PR body.
  - **The issue's premise was half wrong, which is why this PR is smaller than the issue.** #370 reports **both** gates unpinned, resting on *"a test booting the server with a non-loopback `CLAUDE_BIND` — 0, `ltBoot` pins `CLAUDE_BIND: "127.0.0.1"`"*. `ltBoot` spreads `...env` **after** its own `CLAUDE_BIND: "127.0.0.1"` default (`test-features.mjs:2240-2241`), so a caller overrides that pin, and the pre-existing `boot gate REFUSES each unsafe config` test already did. (Stated as "after its own default" rather than "last": #384/#393 added one deliberate post-spread pin, `OCP_TUI_TMUX_BIN`, which a caller is *not* meant to override — `CLAUDE_BIND` is still overridable and that is what this refutation rests on.) Measured: hardcoding `loopbackBind: true` at `server.mjs:856` reddens it. **The local-tools gate was already pinned**; only the TUI LAN gate at `:829` was not, and no test is added for `:856`.
  - **`OCP_TUI_ALLOW_LAN` appeared zero times in the suite** — the issue's own headline metric — so the documented escape hatch was unpinned in the *other* direction: a call site that stopped consulting the override would refuse a deployment the operator had explicitly allowed, and nothing would go red. Its test asserts on **positive** evidence that the boot reached `listen()`, not on the absence of the gate's message, because an absence is equally satisfied by a child that died before reaching the gate at all.
  - **The gate tests no longer bind `0.0.0.0`, and the reason is the mutation run, not the passing run.** Both gates fire before `listen()`, so nothing binds while they work. But mutation-proving a gate test is the one run where the gate is deliberately broken, and measured on this tree that run then prints `listening on http://0.0.0.0:<port> (LAN mode)` — the proof of a network-exposure guard performing the exposure. The address is now `192.0.2.1` (RFC 5737 TEST-NET-1): still non-loopback under `isLoopbackBind`, but assigned to no interface, so a broken-gate run dies at `listen EADDRNOTAVAIL` with no socket opened. No predicate coverage is lost — `isLoopbackBind("0.0.0.0") === false` is its own unit test, and this file's job at this level is the call site, not the predicate.
  - **The safety guard that makes the address choice trustworthy is the FIRST rung of its ladder, not the last — found by independent review.** As first written, the `!buf.out.includes("listening on")` assertion sat below two rungs that both take `EADDRNOTAVAIL` as a *precondition*, so on the one host it exists for — one that can actually bind TEST-NET-1, via an interface holding it or Linux's `net.ipv4.ip_nonlocal_bind` — it could never execute: the wait would time out and the rung above would throw describing the opposite of what happened. Widening the wait alone does not fix it; the `EADDRNOTAVAIL` assertion blocks it too, so both had to move below the guard. This matters because the escape-hatch test is the suite's only boot that deliberately passes the LAN gate: on a permissive host the *passing* run would stand up a TUI-mode, `CLAUDE_AUTH_MODE=none` server off-loopback for the full wait window, with the guard against exactly that dead. Latent, not live — the bind fails here and on `ubuntu-latest` CI — so the fix is the hoist, not a revert. Proven against a synthetic successful-bind `buf`, with a bind-fails control showing the demonstration is not vacuously throwing.
  - **Not endpoint-touching.** `server.mjs` is byte-identical to `main`; the change is `test-features.mjs` only.

### Governance

- **`AGENTS.md`'s mutex prescription no longer names a poll interval — it was measured wrong within hours of merging.** The entry as merged said `sleep 10`, and an agent following it correctly recorded **zero acquisitions in 22 minutes** while a sibling re-acquired within its own window on every release; first acquisition came within one run at a short interval. A `mkdir` lock has **no queue**, so it offers no ordering guarantee and starvation is reachable — and the rule written for the concurrent case is the thing that breaks in it. **Both agents in that run were polling at the same interval**, so what it demonstrates is the missing queue, not that a faster poller beats a slower one; an earlier revision of this entry asserted the latter and it is not what was measured.

  Reported under the 元条款 by the author of #417, against a rule that had landed while they worked, and **the interval was removed rather than replaced**. Substituting a shorter literal would have been the **sixth** prescription in that bullet. The previous **five** — `kill -0`, `[ "$st" = "Z" ]`, the `cwd` comparison, `ps -o lstart=`, and the interval itself — were each falsified by measurement, so a replacement would be a second attempt at the prescription that just failed, and a short interval is a value measured to *work*, not one measured to be *fair*: it converts a systematic loss into a fair race without giving the lock a queue. The interval and the fairness discipline are properties of the implementation, and **#416** owns both, where each becomes something a test can redden. The falsified literal is kept in the entry as the record of what was measured, not as guidance.

  Not endpoint-touching; `AGENTS.md` and this file only.

- **`AGENTS.md` § "Testing discipline" gains two rules about the conditions a measurement is taken under, rather than about how a test is written.** Every rule in that section said how to write a test; none said the number you read back has to have been produced alone, or that a signal you send has to have been aimed. Both gaps let a real incident through while every written rule was satisfied, which is the reporting trigger the 元条款 describes.

  **A measurement requires exclusivity, and a red you cannot attribute is not a result.** The rule deliberately rests on the observation and **not** on its cause. #374 recorded, on a host running two concurrent full suites, teardown waits of **75 514 ms** and **111 203 ms** against a 5 000 ms budget; a clean run on the same host is green, so contention is *necessary*, and whether it is *sufficient* is exactly what #374 leaves open — that issue carries a framing correction saying so, and this entry does not restate its open question as a settled cause. What is not in question is the consequence for measurement: the harness reported the serialization claim **UNPROVEN** and named the offender, and the agent that hit it correctly discarded the row rather than recording it as a mutation effect. Separately, a mutation campaign's first baseline came back `exit 143` with no `=== Results:` line because another agent killed it; it was voided, re-run, and the baseline then confirmed twice. Prescription: a `mkdir`-atomic mutex around every suite run, released in a `trap` whose **last statement is `exit`** — and **the entry deliberately stops there**. It first tried to specify the whole protocol, and **four successive prescriptions in it failed measurement** across five review rounds: `kill -0` (succeeds on a zombie), `[ "$st" = "Z" ]` (macOS pads the column, so it is false on the very case it names, while being right on Linux CI), the `cwd` comparison (**measured to reject a valid owner** — a driver that `cd`s in a subshell keeps the `cwd` it was launched with, so "mismatch ⇒ break" would have destroyed a legitimately-held lock), and `ps -o lstart=` (a **local-time rendering**, so two parties in different `TZ`s false-mismatch). A fifth gap had no prescription at all: `mkdir` succeeds a moment before the owner record is written, and a claimant polling in that window can neither safely break nor safely wait — the unreachable-clearing-condition shape for the **third** time in this repo, inside the bullet that names it. **Every one was found by executing the rule and none by reading it**, including across four rounds by a reviewer hunting for exactly that, so the protocol moved to **#416** to be built in code with tests, where each line becomes something a mutation can redden instead of an adjective a reader can believe. What stays is the transferable knowledge: `kill -0` lies about zombies, `ps` output is column-padded on macOS, and a process's `cwd` is where it *is* rather than which tree it *serves* — it can veto a signal, never authorise one. The entry now also **states the base VOID/RED rule in the repo**, which until now existed only in an issue thread, before extending it with a **third category**: a run can complete, print a results line, and still carry an environmentally-caused red. And it records that two agreeing measurements are the cheapest validity check available, in the form that needs no second run — the `1205 / 0 / 2` vs `1207 / 0 / 2` gap was reconciled **from the diff**, by counting the two test registrations #413 adds, rather than by re-running anything.

  **Attribute a PID by its `cwd` before killing it — killing by PID is necessary and not sufficient.** The existing prohibition covers *pattern* kills, and it was **fully satisfied** by the incident that produced this rule: PR #413's independent reviewer sent `SIGTERM` to four individually-chosen PIDs, and one belonged to another agent's worktree, invalidating that agent's baseline. Compliance with the old rule is what makes it worth writing down. Prescription: resolve the working directory with `lsof -a -p <pid> -d cwd -Fn` and act only on what sits under your own worktree — the normal working shape here is several agents in several worktrees running the same suite, and a real OCP instance may be serving on the documented default port on the very host you are testing on, so the suite's own children and somebody's live proxy are both `node` processes owned by you. The reviewer disclosed its own error unprompted; that is the behaviour to copy.

  Not endpoint-touching; `AGENTS.md` and this file only.

- **`AGENTS.md` § "Testing discipline" records the rule that an assertion which never EXECUTED is indistinguishable from one that passed** — both are silence — and separates three ways a claim goes unproven inside a run that looks green, because they were being treated as one thing and their remedies are not interchangeable. **Asymmetric** (an earlier assertion catches the mutation aimed at a later one) is fixed by ORDERING. **Mutual** (one mutation breaks two claims in the same body, so the first throw ends it) cannot be fixed by ordering at all — both are broken, and only separate `test()` bodies report both. **Process death** is fixed by neither: `test()` invokes its body immediately in one process, so a kill takes every later `test()` call with it, and only a separate child process isolates.

  Measured on #405's M3 (drop `shq()` from the `--tools` value): **1193 passed / 2 failed against that branch's 1195/0 baseline**, reddening exactly the two tests carrying *"stays ONE argv element, not word-split"* and *"cannot break the shell string"* (quoted as the fragments invariant across the rename #405 applied after that run); co-located, one row is the most that could ever have appeared. The entry records two ways the first draft got its own subject wrong — a loud `sh` error was read as a process-level abort when it is an ordinary parent-side `AssertionError` from a `spawnSync` child, and the property that actually forces separation is that **one mutation breaks both claims**, not what the failure looked like.

  The same shape outside the suite, twice: #405's merge refusal (`Required status check … is expected` beside five green checks, with `strict_required_status_checks_policy: true` observed on ruleset `20181348`), and #404's `until ! gh pr checks | grep -q pending`, which exited immediately because the checks had not been **created** yet. Prescription: require a positive count before trusting a negative predicate, and derive it from what applies to *this* change — `alignment.yml` is path-filtered, and this PR hung its own wait loop on `≥5` when only 2 checks apply to a docs-only change. Not endpoint-touching; `AGENTS.md` and this file only.

## v3.29.2 — 2026-08-10

### Fixed

- **The COMPLETION text was silently corrupted whenever a character straddled a chunk boundary on the child's pipe (#365, the other half of #359).** `lineBuffer += d.toString()` and `stderr += d` in `callClaude` and `callClaudeStreaming` decoded each `Buffer` from the spawned `claude` **independently**, so a multi-byte character whose bytes arrive in two `'data'` events became replacement characters before the pieces were joined — and joining cannot repair it. #359 fixed the request side, which corrupts the prompt; this is the answer side, which corrupts what the model said on its way to the client. Just as silent: the NDJSON line stays syntactically valid, nothing throws, nothing logs.
  - **Two lines at the shared spawn boundary, not four at the readers.** `spawnClaudeProcess` is the sole spawn site for the `-p`/stream-json path and `callClaude` + `callClaudeStreaming` are its only two callers, so `proc.stdout.setEncoding("utf8")` + `proc.stderr.setEncoding("utf8")` there cover all four accumulators. `setEncoding` installs a `StringDecoder`, which holds an incomplete trailing sequence until the next chunk completes it.
  - **This is not "no consumer-visible change".** A `stream-json` line long enough to be split mid-character used to deliver altered text to the client — a wrong `choices[0].message.content`, or a wrong SSE `delta.content`. It now delivers what the child wrote. The *contract* is unchanged: no field added, removed, renamed, retyped, or re-ruled, and no field's documented meaning moves.
  - **What each of the four accumulators feeds was checked before changing it, because `stderr`'s type is load-bearing in ways `body`'s was not.** The two `lineBuffer`s feed `parseStreamJsonLines` → the completion text and the SSE deltas. The two `stderr`s are string-sliced at 300, logged via `logEvent`, passed to `trackError` (which re-slices at 200 into `/status`'s and `/health`'s `recentErrors[].message`), run through `sanitizeError`, and turned into an `Error` message that becomes a 500 body or an SSE error frame. None of it constrained the fix: `stderr` starts as `""`, so `+= Buffer` **already** coerced with `toString("utf8")` — it was always a string, and it still is. Only the bytes are now decoded across chunk boundaries instead of per chunk. Every one of those consumers is pinned by a new test.
  - **End-of-stream is a decision, and it is not #359's.** Node flushes a still-incomplete sequence at EOF (`onEofChunk` calls `decoder.end()`) rather than dropping it, and keeping the flush is right on both streams for different reasons. On **stdout** it is inert: a truncated final line has no terminating newline, so `parseStreamJsonLines` already leaves it in `remainder`, which is discarded — a child that died mid-write therefore still surfaces as its real cause (non-zero exit / no `result` event) and never as a spurious NDJSON parse error. #359 rejected a truncated request body precisely because a drop would let a truncated body pass as complete; here the truncated line is *already* discarded, so there is nothing to mistake for complete and nothing to gain from failing the request. On **stderr** the flush is observable, and dropping would silently shorten a diagnostic, so the flushed substitution character is kept — which also leaves the message byte-identical to today's whole-buffer decode.
  - **Tests control the child's chunk boundaries.** A fake `claude` writes its stdout/stderr from files in two `cat`s separated by a real gap, so the test picks the byte offset at which the pipe is cut, and the files are re-staged per case while the server stays booted. Both the buffered and the streaming path are swept, over a payload carrying a 2-byte, 3-byte and 4-byte character; the stderr path additionally asserts the exact `sanitizeError(stderr.slice(0,300))` output, the SSE error frame, and `/health`'s `recentErrors[].message`. A control child reproduces the pre-fix accumulation verbatim and must corrupt at exactly the offsets the two-chunk join predicts, and must report >= 2 `'data'` events — so a coalesced write or an immune payload fails loudly instead of passing vacuously.
  - **The sweep is provably sufficient rather than exhaustive, and that was a measurement, not a preference.** Each offset costs a real child spawn per path, so cutting at literally every byte offset is ~300 spawns; with that in place, 2 of 6 full runs on a host at load average ~11 produced *unrelated* failures of a class this suite already documents (`_runStartShScenario` polls for a backgrounded `node` to be scheduled, and its own comment records that poll flaking on a loaded host), while the unmodified base tree produced none in 2 runs under the same conditions. The sweep now cuts at every continuation-byte offset — the only offsets at which this defect can corrupt anything — plus the offset either side of each character and a stride sample of the remainder: 24 offsets instead of 101, and 72 child spawns instead of 303. Sufficiency is asserted rather than argued: the set of offsets the pre-fix join corrupts is computed over *all* offsets in-process and every one of them must be in the swept set, so narrowing it again cannot silently drop coverage.
  - **Class B, both sub-buckets, route (a).** The completion body is B.1 — `POST /v1/chat/completions`, OpenAI's spec + ADR 0006. Five grandfathered B.2 responses carry corrected values downstream, none of them edited by this change: `recentErrors[].message` on `/health` and `/status`, the `stderr` field of the `claude_exit` log entry on `/logs`, and the character totals behind `/api/usage` and `/cache/stats`. Every one of those rules ("the error message, truncated"; "characters in the response") is untouched while its value stops being corrupted: ADR 0006 (grandfathered as of v3.16.4), **route (a)**, the same shape as the `stats.activeRequests` worked example in `CLAUDE.md`. No field is added, so ADR 0012 is not engaged.
  - **Found by this PR's own independent review, and fixed in it:** the streaming sweep asserted neither of the two non-vacuity premises the other three tests assert, so mutation M4 — which disables the harness's split entirely — left it printing a green tick while testing nothing. The evidence was already in the mutation table (M4 reddened three tests, not four) and the conclusion had not been drawn from it. It now measures both premises per offset like its sibling. The review also corrected a comment claiming the retained `d.toString()` protects a future caller that forgets `setEncoding`; it does not — with a `Buffer` that expression *is* the defect — and the guarantee lives only at the spawn boundary.

- **The same one-shot restart loop was still in `ocp update --rollback` — the *recovery* path (#352).** #347 fixed `runFullUpgrade`. `runRollback`, in the same file, kept `for (const c of restartPlan.plan.cmds) exec(c.cmd, c.label)` byte for byte, with an `exec` that throws. That is the path `ocp update`'s own failure hint tells the operator to run: it is invoked *because* the forward path already failed, by someone who has just been told the proxy is not running. A one-shot restart there turns a recoverable outage into a longer one, and its failure message — `rollback phase restart failed` — is a statement about a command, not about the service.
  - **The throw also made #274's post-flight unreachable.** `runRollback` has had its own `runPostFlightCheck` call since #274, added precisely so a rollback could not report success without confirming what is serving. On a restart-command failure the throw jumped past it, so in the one case that measurement existed for, nothing measured. It is now reached whether or not a restart command failed (its `mockExec` gate is unchanged).
  - **Retry, restoration and the outcome cells, ported from #347** — `execRestartRetry` with `RESTART_ATTEMPTS` and rising backoff, a bounded `restart-restore` pass after a settle delay, and a verdict that comes from the probe rather than from a subcommand's exit status: probe could not **run** → **UNKNOWN**, never DOWN, checked first so a host with a broken `curl` is never told its proxy is dead; something answering with the wrong version → **UP BUT SERVING THE WRONG VERSION**; nothing answering → **THE PROXY IS DOWN**; answering correctly → warned success, not a silent `✓`. `git checkout`, `npm install` and the MED-E `daemon-reload` are deliberately untouched and still fail fast.
  - **Every verdict is keyed on the #291 classification of the last probe attempt, so no sentence asserts more than was measured.** `postFlight.ok === false` covers five states and the DOWN wording ("/health is not answering") is true in only two, so a five-cell ladder replaced the single `!ok` test: `probe-could-not-run` → UNKNOWN (checked first, so a host with a broken `curl` is never told its proxy is dead); a body rejected for its **version** → UP BUT SERVING THE WRONG VERSION; a non-JSON body, a non-2xx, or JSON with no `version` field → **SOMETHING ELSE IS ANSWERING**, whose remedy is `ocp doctor` and finding the port's owner rather than another restart, because recovery commands cannot bind a port someone else holds; `unreachable`/`timeout` → THE PROXY IS DOWN. DOWN requires positive evidence of non-reach rather than being the `else`.
  - **Two of those cells exist because the first two attempts at this were wrong, and both were caught by review rather than by test.** The DOWN cell first fired for a non-JSON body — an error whose phase message said "something is answering … but it is not this proxy" while its hint said "/health is not answering", with the CLI printing only the hint. It also broke the authority being cited: `ocp`'s `_curl_probe` returns 0 whenever `curl` exits 0, and `curl -sf` exits 0 on a 200 with a non-JSON body, so bash reaches warned-success and *cannot* reach its DOWN cell. Then the wrong-version cell fired for a correctly rolled-back but **degraded** proxy, printing "SERVING THE WRONG VERSION (3.10.0, expected 3.10.0)" — because `runPostFlightCheck` labels every post-body rejection `version-mismatch` while `postFlightOk` also fails on **status**, and `/health` reports `degraded` over HTTP 200. The shared classifier's conflation of the two is recorded on the follow-up issue rather than fixed here, since it is reached by `runFullUpgrade` too.
  - **The hints are not copied, because the forward path's hints end in advice this path cannot give.** `runFullUpgrade` closes by pointing at `ocp update --rollback`; here that is the command already running, and repeating it sends the operator round the same loop. The DOWN hint says instead that the working tree is **already** rolled back to the snapshot's version and the only thing outstanding is a running service. The probe-could-not-run cell says `THE ROLLBACK MAY HAVE SUCCEEDED — do NOT re-run it on this evidence` rather than the pre-#352 message, which asserted the restored tree might not be what is running — a claim about the tree, emitted for a fault in the local `curl`. The DOWN hint also no longer claims "nothing will start it on its own" on the systemd shape, where `install-autostart.mjs` writes `Restart=always`.
  - **A failed rollback DOES auto-restore, argued rather than inherited.** It is the last automated step there is — declining to attempt the repair hands the operator a hint they have already followed once — and the commands bring up the **old** tree, already checked out and installed, so there is no "wrong version came back up" hazard for it to weigh, which the forward path does have. It is bounded exactly as #347's is. (A first draft also argued that `allowNotListeningFallback: isRollback` proved the service must already be down here; it does not — that flag *permits* the not-listening case without establishing it, and the ordinary rollback reverts a service that is very much listening. Corrected before merge; the two reasons above carry the decision on their own.)
  - **On a user unit the executed restoration pass clears the start limit first, which #347's forward path does not do.** Deliberate divergence, forced by the arithmetic in this feature's own design note: the retries plus the restoration pass plus systemd's `Restart=always` restarts land inside one 10 s window, and once the limit trips a plain `systemctl restart` keeps failing — so a restoration pass that is the fourth such invocation is expected to be a no-op, leaving the repair only in the printed text. Running `recoveryPlanCommands`' list makes the attempted repair and the printed one the same commands. Scoped to `user-unit` and **not** `system-unit`, because there the list would emit `sudo systemctl reset-failed`, a different `sudo` command from the `sudo systemctl restart` that `planRestart` verified with `sudo -n -l` — a password prompt, i.e. a hang, in a recovery path. What actually keeps `sudo` out of this function is narrower than the first draft claimed, and an independent review measured the overstatement: the retry loop and the restore's `else` branch execute the plan's commands verbatim, and a `system-unit` plan carries `sudo`. The property rests on `resolveRestartPlan` refusing `isRollback && owner.kind === "system-unit"` before any command runs — so that refusal is what now carries a test with teeth (it must fire with **zero** commands executed). The exclusion below it buys the narrower guarantee that the step this pass *adds* never carries `sudo`.
  - **The verdict block is a copy of `runFullUpgrade`'s, not a call into a shared one**, and this is stated rather than left for a reader to discover: the issue predicted the cell logic was "already exported"; only `execRestartRetry`, `recoveryPlanCommands`, `postFlightFailureSuffix` and `postFlightOnlyCommand` are. Extracting it touches `runFullUpgrade` and is filed as its own reviewable unit rather than folded in here.
  - **The `reset-failed` rewrite is verified on the plan shape only this path can reach.** `recoveryPlanCommands` derives its command by rewriting `" restart -- "` inside the plan's own string. #347 verified that against the shapes the forward path reaches; rollback can also reach `planRestart`'s `allowNotListeningFallback` branch, which is the branch a down-service rollback actually takes. Confirmed behaviourally, end to end, rather than read off the source.
  - **`opts.execFn` added to `runRollback`**, the seam #347 added to `runFullUpgrade` and for the same reason: `opts.mockExec` makes `exec` a total no-op, so under it no command can *fail* and none of this was reachable from a test. It is a plain JavaScript function, so no `launchctl`/`systemctl` **restart** string is handed to a shell — there is no shell in that call path to intercept. That guarantee is `execFn`'s and covers only the lanes routed through it; **four other lanes are held by `mockExec`** — the argv-form `git checkout`, the `tryCopy` file restore, the MED-E `systemctl --user daemon-reload`, and `resolveRestartPlan`'s owner-gathering (`lsof`/`netstat`/`ss`/`launchctl print`). Two successive drafts attributed all of it to `execFn`; the test harness pins `mockExec: true` after the caller's spread so the distinction cannot be lost by accident.

- **The `ltTest` serialization guard reported a slow teardown as a serialization regression, permanently (#358).** The drain between queued tests is `ltWait(() => _ltActiveBoots === 0, 5000)` — and **`ltWait` returns whether the condition held, while the call discarded that boolean.** When the drain timed out the next test booted anyway, `_ltActiveBoots` reached 2, and `_ltPeakBoots` latched there via `Math.max` with no reset, so every later assertion in the run reported a violation that had already stopped being one.
  - **Two real failures were dismissed as "load" on that evidence**, in two separate PRs, before the mechanism was found. The number was true; the meaning attached to it was not.
  - **The first revision of this fix asserted something false, and independent review caught it.** It claimed that because a completed drain proves the adjacent tests did not overlap, `peak > 1` can *only* come from a timed-out drain. The first clause holds; the second assumes every boot is separated from every other boot by a drain, and **five sites boot a second child inside a single test body**, where the between-test barrier never runs. All five were `ltWait(() => buf.closed, 5000)` with the boolean discarded — the same defect one scope down — and a comment already in the file documented the counterexample verbatim. The first revision compiled that false premise into an assertion message, so a slow in-body teardown would have been reported as a real regression: a fix for a misleading message introducing a misleading message. All six teardown-then-boot gaps now record through one `ltDrain` helper.
  - **The second revision was blocked too, and for the same reason one level in: a timed-out drain only means anything when the peak is ALSO above 1.** That is the state where the peak becomes unattributable. When the peak stayed 1 the question is settled — the child closed before the next boot, or the drain was terminal in its scope and no boot follows — so serialization is *proven*, and the revision's unconditional `_ltDrainTimeouts.length === 0` assertion reported a slow teardown as a serialization result. Independent review measured it firing with `peak === 1` and the record's own `active=0` sitting inside the message contradicting it; four of the six gaps are terminal on their last iteration, so no mutation was needed to reach it. **The failure condition is now the peak alone**, and a timeout with `peak === 1` is reported as a slow-teardown lead (#374) instead. Consequence worth stating: the set of runs that fail is **unchanged** from before this PR — only the diagnosis changed. The second revision's claim that it made the suite stricter was itself wrong.
  - **All three directions now say which they are, and the middle one is the whole point.** `peak > 1` with a timed-out drain → UNPROVEN, offenders named with their elapsed wait and live boot count. `peak > 1` with every drain completed → a real serialization regression. `peak === 0` → an ordering failure, not an overlap: found while mutation-proving, because bypassing the queue makes the assertion run before anything boots, and reporting that as "children overlapped" would be a true number under a false claim.
  - **Four defects of this same family were found in this PR's own work, one of them only in the final round**, each by running something rather than re-reading it: the barrier recorded its offender under a key the summary does not render, so the *primary* #358 scenario printed `undefined(…)` and lost the test name; three drain labels were minted from pre-edit line numbers and named sites that had moved; one site carried a comment describing a different site's mechanism verbatim; and the barrier hand-copied `ltDrain`'s body instead of calling it, which is *how* the key diverged in the first place. The barrier now calls the helper, so "all six gaps go through `ltDrain`" is true rather than nearly true, and the next field added to the record cannot diverge again.
  - **Two unsourced measurements were deleted.** A comment carried "a quiet loop finishes at 1.02x nominal and a stalled one at 7.53x" — figures that appear nowhere else in the repo, in no measurement table, and in neither the commit message nor this file, while the PR's own measurements give 1.66x and 60x. Worse, they implied elapsed stays under the 10x cap, which a second comment 2,600 lines earlier explicitly forbids reading it as. Two comments in one file, added in one commit, making opposite claims about the same mechanism.
  - **`ltWait`'s `ms * 10` caps its deadline, not its elapsed time** — it re-checks the clock only at the top of its loop, so one late `await` overruns the cap by however long the event loop stalled. Measured on a verbatim copy: quiet host returns at 83 ms for `ms=50`; blocking the loop 3 s returns at 3012 ms, **6.0× past the 500 ms cap**. A quiet-host timeout on the 5000 ms budget is therefore ~5 s, and the ceiling bounds nothing. Both extremes showed up for real — one mutation run recorded 39 drains at ~84 ms and one at 78678 ms — which is why the record carries the duration rather than just a boolean.

- **`ocp update` took production down: #325's restart retry was in the bash path only (#347).** Fifteen minutes after #325 shipped, an `ocp update` on production ran `launchctl bootout` (succeeded) then `launchctl bootstrap` (returned `5: Input/output error`), reported `✗ phase restart failed`, exited 1, and left a healthy proxy **stopped for 2–3 minutes**. The identical bootstrap succeeded by hand on the first attempt — a transient fault racing the bootout that had just completed.
  - **#325 fixed the leg that was caught.** `_restart_exec_retry` (`ocp:173`) is used by `cmd_restart` at `ocp:1026`, and `ocp update` never reaches `cmd_restart` — it goes through `scripts/upgrade.mjs`'s `runFullUpgrade`, whose restart phase was `for (const c of restartPlan.plan.cmds) exec(c.cmd, c.label)`. One shot, and `exec` **throws**, so the post-flight `/health` poll that follows never ran. Nothing detected the downed service and nothing put it back. `ocp update` is the *more* dangerous of the two paths: it is the fleet-rollout command, run unattended across hosts in sequence.
  - **Bounded retry, scoped to the restart commands.** `execRestartRetry` mirrors `_restart_exec_retry`'s shape — 3 attempts (`RESTART_ATTEMPTS`, the same number `ocp:1026` passes), rising backoff. Deliberately *not* applied to `exec` generally: a failing `git checkout` or `npm install` is not transient and still fails fast on the first attempt, which is pinned by its own test.
  - **The failure path now restores instead of only reporting.** After the retry budget is spent, the whole resolved restart plan is re-run once, after a settle delay, as a visible `restart-restore` phase — the commands that would have brought the service back up. Running them again is exactly what fixed the incident by hand.
  - **The verdict comes from the probe, not from an exit status.** A restart failure no longer throws, so `/health` is always polled afterwards (now via the shared `runPostFlightCheck`, which brings #291's failure classification with it). Four outcome cells, matching `ocp`'s own (`ocp:1051-1127`): command failed + not answering → **THE PROXY IS DOWN**; command failed + answering → warned success, not a silent `✓`; probe could not run → **UNKNOWN**, never DOWN — that arm is checked first, because a machine with a broken `curl` must not be told its proxy is dead.
  - **The hint leads with service state.** It said *"Working tree may be at new version. Run `ocp update --rollback`"* — version state, which was not what mattered with the port dead. It now opens with `THE PROXY IS DOWN`, names the commands that bring it back, and demotes the tree/rollback advice to the end. A non-restart failure keeps the original tree-state hint unchanged.
  - **The restoration re-runs the whole plan, not just the command that failed.** It originally re-ran from the failure point onward, which on macOS meant `bootstrap` **alone** — while the hint told the operator to run `bootout && bootstrap`. The restore was weaker than the recovery it prescribes, and weaker than what actually ended the incident. Safe to re-run the tear-down for one specific reason, now documented where it is relied on: the resolved bootout ends in `2>/dev/null || true` (`scripts/lib/restart-unit.mjs:865`), so it cannot exit non-zero against an already-unloaded job. If that `|| true` ever goes, this becomes unsafe.
  - **On systemd the recovery hint leads with `systemctl reset-failed`.** This fix is what turns one restart into up to four in about five seconds, and `install-autostart.mjs` writes `Restart=always` / `RestartSec=5` with no `StartLimit` override — so systemd's default 5-starts-per-10s applies and its **own** auto-restarts stack with ours. Spacing the attempts cannot fix that, because systemd's contribution is not ours to schedule. What matters is the end state: a tripped limit latches the unit `failed` and a plain `systemctl restart` keeps failing, which would make the hint's own re-run command fail — worse than the bug being fixed. The recovery command list is derived by rewriting the plan's own command, so it inherits the `sudo` prefix, the `--user` scope and the unit name exactly rather than re-deriving them.
  - **A post-flight probe that could not RUN no longer headlines "run `ocp update --rollback`".** `ocp` has this cell (`ocp:1063-1067`); this path did not, so a host whose **`curl`** is broken was told to roll back a **possibly-successful** upgrade — the same defect class this issue was filed for, the headline naming the wrong thing to do. The #291 classification was already correct in the phase message; only the headline contradicted it. A probe that genuinely ran and said no still gets the original tree-state hint.
  - **Also fixed, found while writing the tests:** the post-upgrade snapshot GC was the one success-path step not gated on `mockExec`, so every all-mock test that returned successfully ran a real `rmSync` sweep over the developer's own `~/.ocp/upgrade-snapshot-*`. It had never deleted anything only because `gcSnapshots` keeps anything within `keepDays` regardless of count. Now skipped under `mockExec` like every other mutating step.

- **An OCP install outside `$HOME/ocp` could not self-update — and that is the security-hardened host (#348).** `scripts/doctor.mjs` and `scripts/upgrade.mjs` resolved the install directory as `join(homedir(), "ocp")`. On the fleet host whose install is at `/opt/ocp` behind a system unit, `sudo ./ocp update` therefore looked in `/root/ocp` (sudo's `HOME`), found nothing, set `current_version = "unknown"`, failed `from_version_supported` with `unknown < v3.4.0`, and concluded `kind = "fresh_install"` — the path that runs `rm -rf ~/ocp`. The gate held (it needs `--fresh-install --yes`), so nothing was destroyed; the host simply could not take the release.
  - **The host that could not update is the one deliberately relocated as a security measure.** `/opt/ocp` plus an unprivileged `User=` is the topology adopted to close #328's credential-escalation chain — the headline fix of the release it could not install. Generalised: **any host hardened by relocation was silently outside the self-update mechanism, and discovered it only when reaching for a security fix.**
  - **The two halves of the same tool disagreed about where OCP is installed.** The bash `ocp` has always resolved `script_dir` from `${BASH_SOURCE[0]}` through a `readlink` loop; only the `.mjs` half assumed `$HOME`.
