New `scripts/lib/install-dir.mjs` resolves from `fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)` — correct by construction and immune to sudo's `HOME` — and is used by doctor's version detection and remediation strings **and** by `runFullUpgrade`/`runRollback`, whose `ocpDir` is the directory they `git checkout`, `npm install` and `setup.mjs` into. That last one was the same bug pointed at a **mutating** command.
  - **Deliberately not applied to `resolveExpectedWorkingTree` (#254).** That function answers "which tree is this process running from", for comparison against the tree the live service runs. An operator-supplied `OCP_DIR` must never override a fact the process already knows, or a wrong value would *suppress* the mismatch warning instead of tripping it. The two questions look identical and are not.
  - **`OCP_DIR` is now a real escape hatch, and the answer is visible either way.** Previously `OCP_DIR=/opt/ocp ocp doctor` changed nothing because nothing read it. It is now consulted (absolute paths only — a relative value is refused *and said so*, since silently dropping the operator's override is the same defect class as silently assuming `$HOME/ocp`), and doctor pushes an `install_dir` check naming the directory used and how it was chosen. `--json` gains `install_dir` and `install_dir_source`.
  - **`current_version` FAILs instead of PASSing when it cannot determine a version**, naming the path it tried and pointing at `OCP_DIR`. It used to report `[PASS] current_version: current=unknown` — the check that *had* the information reported success, and a downstream check turned it into `unknown < v3.4.0`, which reads as "your version is too old" rather than "I could not find your install". `from_version_supported` no longer makes that claim about a version it never read.
  - **The PASS→FAIL flip cannot refuse an update that used to proceed**, and this is pinned by test rather than asserted: the new FAIL's condition is exactly the first conjunct of `from_version_supported`'s, so every input that trips it was **already** failing — `ready_to_upgrade` was already `false`, `next_action.kind` is computed from `fromSupported` and is unchanged, and `runUpgrade()`'s pre-flight guard tolerates `!ready_to_upgrade` for precisely the `fresh_install` kind this state produces. A host on a **known**-but-unsupported version (v3.2.0) still gets `current_version: PASS`.
  - **The escape hatch was also an `rm -rf` argument, and independent review blocked the first cut over it.** With `$OCP_DIR` read, a typo'd-but-absolute value (`/`, `/etc`, a home directory) resolved fine, had no `package.json`, failed `current_version`, selected `kind="fresh_install"`, and became the argument to `rm -rf`. **Before this change there was no reachable way to point that command anywhere but `$HOME/ocp`** — `opts.ocpDir` is dead in production and no `--ocp-dir` flag exists — so this was a *new* capability, not the same risk with a better target. `classifyInstallDir()` now permits the destructive step only against a directory that is **absent, empty, or verifiably an OCP install** (`package.json` named `open-claude-proxy`, or at least two of `server.mjs` / `setup.mjs` / `ocp` / `models.json`; one alone is not enough — a stray `server.mjs` in a home directory must not license `rm -rf $HOME`). It fails closed: a directory that cannot be inspected, or a path that is a file, is refused. The guard is applied twice — doctor withholds the steps, and `runFreshInstall` re-derives the verdict itself rather than trusting the JSON handed to it, because the component that runs `rm -rf` should not depend on the one that wrote the string. **This also closes the same hole on the default path**, which never had the guard either: a host whose `$HOME/ocp` was somebody's unrelated directory was always one `--fresh-install --yes` away from losing it.
  - **The consent message named the wrong directory to delete.** `runFreshInstall`'s refusal — the last thing an operator reads before typing `--fresh-install --yes` — hardcoded `` `rm -rf ~/ocp` ``. That was true while doctor's step was hardcoded too; once doctor started interpolating the resolved directory, the gate described deleting one directory while the command deleted another, on exactly the `/opt/ocp` host this change exists for. Now interpolated from the same resolution doctor uses, and the same correction is applied to `docs/troubleshooting.md`.
  - **A refused `OCP_DIR` was invisible on the command that matters.** The `install_dir` check carried it at PASS, and `ocp`'s `cmd_update` prints WARN and INFO lines only — so the refusal reached an operator running `ocp update` exactly never, contradicting the module's own stated principle. It is WARN now.
  - New env var `OCP_DIR` documented in README § Environment Variables; the failure and its manual workaround in [docs/troubleshooting.md](docs/troubleshooting.md#update-fresh-install). No server-side change — `server.mjs` is untouched.

## v3.29.1 — 2026-08-06

### Fixed

- **UTF-8 was silently corrupted whenever a character straddled a chunk boundary (#359, P0).** All four request-body readers accumulated with `body += chunk`, which coerces each `Buffer` to a string **independently**. A multi-byte character whose bytes arrive in two chunks is decoded as replacement characters *before* the pieces are joined, so the join can never repair it: `你好世界` became `\uFFFD\uFFFD\uFFFD好世界`. Each of the four readers now calls `req.setEncoding("utf8")`, which installs a `StringDecoder` on the stream so an incomplete trailing sequence is held until the next chunk completes it. Sites: `PATCH /settings`, `POST /v1/chat/completions`, `POST /api/keys`, `PATCH /api/keys/:id/quota`.
  - **This is not "no consumer-visible change".** A body split mid-character used to corrupt, and depending on where the split landed either altered the prompt handed to the CLI or failed `JSON.parse` with a 400; it now parses. The *contract* is unchanged — no field added, removed, renamed, retyped, or re-ruled — but the values stop being wrong, which is observable.
  - **The caps deliberately still count characters.** All four compare `body.length` — UTF-16 code units, not bytes — and #310 exists because mislabelling that quantity cost two review rounds. Accumulating `Buffer`s and calling `Buffer.concat` at the end would have changed the unit (a ~3x tightening for CJK) **and** deferred the limit check past end-of-stream, removing the early abort. `setEncoding` delivers already-decoded strings instead, so `body += chunk` and `body.length` keep their exact previous meaning. Regression-tested on all four routes: exactly-cap multi-byte bodies stay accepted, cap+1 stays rejected.
  - **No body accepted today can newly receive a 413.** Verified over 200,028 split points (all offsets of fixed payloads plus 200,000 random byte strings): the pre-fix accumulation never produced *fewer* characters than a correct decode, only more. The change can therefore only admit bodies that were previously rejected in error.
  - **Decided behaviour for malformed and truncated input, because neither is obvious.** Bytes that are not valid UTF-8 decode to U+FFFD exactly as a whole-buffer decode already did, and the result is now identical no matter where the stream was cut. A body that *ends* mid-character is **rejected**, not silently accepted: Node flushes the buffered partial sequence at end-of-stream (`readable.js` `onEofChunk` calls `decoder.end()`), the substitution character breaks `JSON.parse`, and the request gets a 400 — a truncated body must not be mistaken for a complete one.
  - **The child `stdout`/`stderr` accumulators have the same defect and are deliberately not touched here** — the `proc.stdout.on("data", …)` and `proc.stderr.on("data", …)` handlers inside `callClaude` and `callClaudeStreaming` in `server.mjs`. Cited by function rather than by line on purpose: `ALIGNMENT.md`'s note on line-number rot applies with force to a CHANGELOG, which outlives every line number in it, and #359's own line references (`:1761`, `:1787`, `:2234`, `:2303`) were already 16 lines stale against this PR's base. Setting the child streams' encoding once at the shared spawn boundary is a separate, smaller change, and both `stderr` accumulators are string-sliced and turned into `Error` messages, so their type is load-bearing in ways the body readers' is not. #359 stays open to cover them.
  - **Tests control the chunk boundaries, because the pre-existing CJK coverage structurally cannot.** It sends whole bodies through `fetch()`, and a body that arrives whole cannot exhibit the defect. The new tests drive a raw socket, split at **every internal byte offset** of 2-byte, 3-byte and 4-byte characters plus invalid and truncated byte sequences, and assert against a control server in the test process that reproduces `body += chunk` verbatim — the control must corrupt at exactly the offsets the pre-fix operation corrupts, and must report >= 2 `data` events, so a coalesced write or an immune payload fails loudly instead of passing vacuously.
  - **Class B, both sub-buckets.** `/v1/chat/completions` is B.1 (OpenAI spec + ADR 0006); `/settings`, `POST /api/keys` and `PATCH /api/keys/:id/quota` are B.2, authorized by ADR 0006 (grandfathered as of v3.16.4), **route (a)** — behaviour-preserving in the contract sense, the same shape as the `stats.activeRequests` worked example in `CLAUDE.md`. No new fields, so ADR 0012 is not engaged.

### Changed

- **`listKeys` redacts in SQL, so raw key material never enters process memory (#295).** External contribution by @anupamme. The preview is now computed by the query (`substr(key,1,8)||'...'||substr(key,-4)`) and the full `key` column is no longer SELECTed, so `GET /api/keys`, the dashboard and `ocp keys` get identical output while the secret never reaches Node. Behaviour-preserving on a grandfathered B.2 endpoint: JSON property *order* changes, and all three consumers read by name — verified at `server.mjs:3679`, `dashboard.html:224`, `ocp:594`.
  - **Review found the three-layer test design is load-bearing, not redundant.** A mutation the author did not write — a full-key leak hidden *inside* `substr(key,1,36)` — is invisible to the SQL-shape test by construction (its regex strips exactly the region the leak hides in) and invisible to the `db.prepare` spy. Only the byte-equality test against a real key catches it.
  - After this change **no query in `keys.mjs` selects the raw `key` column at all** — `validateKey` and `findKey` already selected only `id, name`, so `listKeys` was the last read-path door.

- **The ADR 0012 additive-field audit now reads the wire instead of the CHANGELOG (#346).** The sweep in `CLAUDE.md`'s `release_kit.governance_audits` used to grep the CHANGELOG for ADR 0012's condition-5 marker. It missed a **compliant** spelling three times running — sentence-initial capital (#338), markdown link (#344 first pass), bold wrapped *around* the reference (#344 second pass) — every one caught by a human reviewer and none by the mechanism. Its own written stopping rule ("if a THIRD compliant spelling is missed, stop widening") had fired, so this replaces it rather than widening it a fourth time.
  - **The spelling was the symptom; reading prose to learn what shipped was the disease.** Every version of that grep, including a hypothetically perfect one, could only see additions whose author *wrote the marker*. A field added with no marker returned "none this cycle" — a positive-looking result for the case the audit was least able to see, and the exact shape of the four pre-#288 additions that motivated ADR 0012 in the first place.
  - **New mechanism: a per-release record of each grandfathered Class B.2 endpoint's real response key set.** `scripts/b2-key-snapshot.mjs` boots a real `server.mjs`, probes **all 16 endpoint+method pairs** across the 12 Class B.2 rows in `ALIGNMENT.md` § "Current Class B inventory", and records each response's recursive **key paths** plus its status and content-type into `docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json`. `npm test` fails on any difference, naming every added and removed key path and stating which authorization each kind needs. The snapshot's git history is the per-release record: `git log -p docs/governance/b2-response-keys.json`.
  - **Values are never recorded, and that is proven rather than asserted.** The live test probes **two fresh boots** and requires byte-identical key sets, so uptime, timestamps and counters cannot make the snapshot flap. A snapshot that flapped would be worse than none: it would train every releaser to regenerate without reading the diff.
  - **Nested keys are recorded, because a flat top-level set would have missed both fields in the v3.29.0 baseline.** `instanceName` is top-level but `auth.consecutiveInconclusive` is not, and every ADR 0012 field in that baseline lives in `/health`'s `auth` block. Present-but-`null` is *in* the key set and absent is not, preserving the distinction `/health`'s `instanceName` ships `""` rather than omitted to protect (#327).
  - **The anchored baseline was reconciled against the wire, and they agree.** Both v3.29.0 fields — `instanceName` and `auth.consecutiveInconclusive` — appear in the snapshot's `GET /health` record, as do `auth.okAt` and `auth.okSource`, exactly as the previous clause predicted a reader would find (they landed under ADR 0014 as a contract change, not as additive fields, and are correctly not counted).
  - **The probe plan is checked against `ALIGNMENT.md` itself**, so a new Class B.2 endpoint added to the constitution with no probe added to the plan fails the suite instead of silently going unreported. The parse is guarded against anchor drift: a table shape the parser does not recognise is refused rather than allowed to "cover" everything vacuously.
  - **What it still cannot see is enumerated in two places, and each entry says how it was established.** 13 entries in the snapshot's own `notCovered` block, 8 tagged `[measured]` and 5 `[reasoned]`; 7 headline items under `blind_spots:` in `CLAUDE.md`. The script header keeps **no third copy** — it names those two and defers, because a third copy is the one that goes stale. Headline items: type changes under an unchanged key path; renames (they fail, but as one removal plus one addition, not as a rename); shapes that exist only under a non-default configuration, **measured at 12 key paths**; request-shaped responses whose recorded keys come from the probe's own body; error and non-localhost responses; `/usage`, which is Hybrid rather than B.2; and **authorization itself** — this detects that surface moved, never whether the move was allowed. ADR 0012 condition 5 remains a human obligation; what changed is that forgetting it is now detectable from the other side.
  - **Three prose defects in those notes were found by this PR's own review and corrected in it (#354).** The blind-spot note claimed `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE=true` "gives `tui.pool` an object instead of null"; **measured, TUI mode alone leaves `tui.pool` null** and the only delta is `− spawn.reason` — the object needs `OCP_TUI_POOL_SIZE>=1`, which adds 10 paths and which no copy of the note mentioned. A **thirteenth** blind spot was missing entirely: `PATCH /settings`'s `results.*` is keyed by the request body (measured: `{timeout}` and `{cacheTTL}` produce different key sets), a rule the probe plan already applied three lines earlier to the quota probe and had not carried across. And "written down in three places" was itself untrue. All three are prose, in the artifact whose entire job is accuracy — a blind-spot note that authoritatively points the wrong way spends the credibility of "we measured this" on a claim that was not measured, which is why every entry now carries its provenance. A second fixture profile for the 12-path config-variant gap is filed as **#357** so it has an owner rather than only a description.
  - **The marker grep is kept as a secondary cross-check**, unchanged and still deliberately missing a known compliant spelling so the gap stays visible. Its incompleteness is no longer load-bearing: a marker it misses now surfaces from the key-set diff instead.
  - **Found while building it, recorded rather than fixed here, now tracked as #355:** nothing in `server.mjs` ever writes the `sessions` Map (declared at `server.mjs:965`; **0 occurrences of `sessions.set(` and 0 of `sessions.get(`** — only `.delete` and `.clear`), so `/health`'s and `/sessions`'s `sessions[]` are empty on every request. The blast radius is wider than one empty array: `/status`'s `activeSessions` and `stats.sessionHits` / `sessionMisses` are permanently constant too, and `CLAUDE_SESSION_TTL` is a documented env var whose only effect is timing a GC sweep whose loop body cannot execute. The snapshot recording `sessions[]` as an empty key set is the first mechanism that would catch a writer being added.
  - **No `server.mjs` change**, so no endpoint class is declared: this PR adds test and governance surface only.

## v3.29.0 — 2026-08-05

### Changed

- **ADR 0006 and ADR 0008 signed off; both move `Proposed` → `Accepted`. No ADR in the repo is `Proposed` any more.** Neither decision changes — no code, no endpoint, no field, and neither ADR's body is edited beyond its status line.
  - **ADR 0006 is the one that mattered.** It defines the Class A/B taxonomy that every PR in this repo cites as its authority, and it is referenced on **21 lines of `ALIGNMENT.md`** (the supreme document), 7 of `CLAUDE.md`, 1 of `AGENTS.md`, and by **six other ADRs** — 0007, 0008, 0010, 0011, 0012, 0013. The entire citation discipline was resting on a document marked "Proposed — owner reviewing".
  - **This closes an inconsistency the previous sign-off created.** #326 accepted ADR 0012 and ADR 0013 while both derive their authority from 0006 — 0012 declares "Extends: ADR 0006", 0013's authority line names it — leaving two `Accepted` → `Proposed` edges in the dependency graph. That PR named the edges rather than hiding them and deferred 0006 because its status line said "owner reviewing", which is a statement of maintainer state and not a contributor's to clear. It has now been cleared by the maintainer.
  - **ADR 0008** carries no governance weight — zero references in `ALIGNMENT.md`, `CLAUDE.md` or `AGENTS.md`, one from another ADR — and the feature it authorizes (the TUI warm-pane pool, and `/health`'s `tui.pool` sub-object) shipped in **v3.22.1**. It is signed in the same batch for the same reason: an unaccepted record of a shipped decision makes `Proposed` mean nothing.
  - **What this buys, concretely:** no ADR's `Status` is `Proposed` any more. The next one that carries it genuinely means "undecided", which is exactly what it could not mean while four shipped-and-cited decisions sat under the same label. Stated as *status lines*, not as "the word does not appear": ADR 0010's body describes its own status history in prose, and a check that flagged that would be measuring the wrong thing.
  - **`docs/adr/README.md` is corrected in the same PR.** It still annotated 0012 and 0013 as "**Proposed** — needs maintainer sign-off", left behind when #326 changed the ADRs but not the index. A PR whose claim is "nothing is Proposed" cannot ship with the repo's own ADR index saying otherwise.
### Added

- **A non-primary OCP instance can declare itself (#327).** New env var `OCP_INSTANCE_NAME`, reported on `/health` as **`instanceName`** — **additive under ADR 0012** (additive only, read-only, no new endpoint or method, reports on what `/health` already reports on: `version`, `architecture`, `authMode`, `claudeBinary` are all instance-scalar facts, B.2 only).
  - **The issue said this needed its own ADR. Overriding that was right; the reason first given for it was false.** The PR originally dismissed the issue's reservation as "written before ADR 0012 was signed off". Measured: ADR 0012 landed on main at 09:48Z and issue #327 was opened at 10:35Z — **47 minutes later**, never edited, and its text **cites ADR 0012 by name** while carving out "a new *env-driven* field with new semantics deserves the check". It was a considered reservation made with 0012 in hand, not a stale opinion. Engaged on its merits instead: ADR 0012's conditions recognise no "env-driven" carve-out, and condition 4's "new subject in an additive costume" targets surfacing a **new subsystem** — while `/health` already reports `authMode`, `claudeBinary` and `config.*`, all operator-configured env-derived instance-scalar facts. `instanceName` is the same species: a static reflection of unit configuration with nothing behind it and nothing branching on it. The future `doctor`/sweep consumers are separate changes needing their own authorization when they arrive.
  - **The reason a second instance exists is Unix identity, not load.** OCP spawns a `claude` child per request and that child inherits the *service's* identity, so an agent answering untrusted users needs its own instance under its own user. That cannot be solved inside one process — the spawn identity comes from the unit's `User=`, one identity per unit.
  - **Two things this fixes, both of which happened.** A version sweep probing only `:3456` reported the fleet clean while a second instance sat a full release behind. And `ocp doctor`'s multi-unit check has no vocabulary for "two instances, both intended", so it warns on a correct configuration every run — and a warning that always fires is one people learn to skip, which is how the *genuine* leftover duplicate on that same host stayed hidden.
  - **Empty rather than omitted** when unset, so a consumer can distinguish "this is the primary" from "this build predates the field" — the same distinction #324's backward-compatibility test turns on. A mutation to the tempting `...(x ? {x} : {})` shape turns a test red.
  - **Scope, stated:** this makes an instance *discoverable*. Teaching `doctor` to tell declared multiplicity from a leftover duplicate needs a unit→instance mapping it does not have, and is deliberately left as follow-up on #327 rather than half-built here.
  - README gains a "Running more than one instance on a host" section documenting the `DEFAULT_PORT + n` convention, why the identity boundary is the reason, and both failure modes above.


### Fixed

- **The `auth` state table was keyed on the wrong field, and promised a value the server can never emit (#342).** `README.md`'s § "What `auth.ok` means" was keyed on `lastOutcome`, but the field the verdict's meaning turns on is `okSource`. Two rows were wrong as a result, not one.
  - **`lastOutcome: "verified-by-request"` does not exist.** `grep -c` in `server.mjs`: **0**. It was promised by the README table, by ADR 0014 § C bullet 1, by this CHANGELOG's own #308 entry, and by a comment in `lib/spawn-auth.mjs` — which sat **three lines above** another comment saying not to key on `lastOutcome`. The implementation is the correct one here: `noteAuthVerifiedByRequest` leaves `lastOutcome` to the probe, because overwriting it would make `/health` claim a probe ran when none did. Writing the value in code would have reintroduced exactly the lie #308 was about.
  - **The table had no row for the state most instances are actually in.** Once a completed request raises the verdict on a host that supplies the token through the environment, the real state is `ok: true` + `okSource: "request"` + `lastOutcome: "token-present"` — a pairing the old table's `token-present` row explicitly contradicted by asserting `ok` is `null`. Three of the four instances in the reference fleet spend most of their time there. A monitor written from that table could not match reality, and the state it would see most often was the one missing.
  - Re-keyed on `okSource` with all six reachable states enumerated, `lastOutcome` demoted to a secondary column, and inconclusive probes explained as changing only that column rather than being a row. ADR 0014, the CHANGELOG entry, the `spawn-auth.mjs` comment and three test fixtures carrying the phantom name are corrected in the same PR. No behaviour changes — `applyRequestVerdictTtl` already keyed on `okSource`; suite green before and after at 1041.

- **The ADR 0012 additive-field sweep had two defects, found on its first real run, pushing the count in opposite directions (#338).** The sweep was introduced last release to make B.2 surface growth visible, and its first use miscounted.
  - **Case-sensitive grep ⇒ under-count.** The prescribed literal missed `**Additive under ADR 0012.**` — capital `A`, because the marker opened a sentence. A real shipped field (`auth.consecutiveInconclusive`, #324) vanished from the audit. This is the dangerous one, and it fires on the **compliant** path: the author wrote the marker exactly as ADR 0012 condition 5 requires. It was caught only because a reviewer re-ran the grep with different flags — which is not a control.
  - **Counting its own prose ⇒ over-count.** A CHANGELOG entry describing the sweep necessarily quotes the marker it greps for, which would have put a permanent +1 into the cumulative total.
  - Both corrupt the one number ADR 0012 names as load-bearing: *"a monotonically rising integer is what makes the accumulation visible at all."* The `how:` clause now specifies case-insensitive matching, counting **entries** rather than occurrences, reading every hit rather than reporting the raw number, and stating the raw-vs-reported difference when they diverge. **A known-good baseline is anchored in the clause** — 2 as of v3.29.0, `instanceName` and `auth.consecutiveInconclusive` — so future releasers increment a fixed number instead of recomputing it with whichever flags they happen to use, since that recomputation is what produced both defects.

- **A duplicate `auth:` key in `/health` had silently disconnected the fix immediately below it (#339).** Caught by adversarial review of this batch **before the tag was pushed**, and confirmed by mutation on the live wire rather than by reading the diff: with `effectiveAuthStatus()` stubbed to return a marker, the marker did not appear in the response — the raw `authStatus` latch was being served instead. JS object literals keep the **last** duplicate key, with no error and no warning, so `auth: effectiveAuthStatus()` was computed and thrown away.
  - **Provenance:** #334 changed `auth: authStatus` → `auth: effectiveAuthStatus()`. #336 was written against pre-#334 code and its hunk re-added the old line alongside `instanceName` — a rebase-conflict artifact in a hand-resolved merge.
  - **Consequence had it shipped:** `/health`'s auth verdict would never expire. ADR 0014 § "Why the raise must expire" exists for exactly the state #324 observed on a real host for hours — a request-verified `ok: true`, then an expired token, then *inconclusive* probes that preserve `ok` indefinitely. The read-time TTL was the **only** guard for that shape. `/health` would report `auth.ok: true` while nothing could be served, and `ocp doctor` would read that body and report `PASS`. Byte-for-byte the #308 symptom, re-opened by #308's own release batch.
  - **Why 1040 green tests missed it:** `applyRequestVerdictTtl` is unit-tested and correct. Nothing asserted that `/health` *calls* it. The TTL-applied and raw values are identical in every state a live test can reach inside test time, so no live assertion could tell them apart.
  - **The wiring is now pinned behaviourally** (#340). The test posts a request to raise the verdict, steps the child's clock past the freshness window, and asserts `/health` reports `okSource: "expired"` / `ok: null`. It fails against both shapes of the defect — the original `auth: authStatus` and the quoted `"auth": authStatus` — and takes about six seconds.
  - **The first attempt at this shipped a source-grep guard instead, on a justification that was reasoned rather than executed.** It claimed no state was reachable in test time where the TTL-applied and raw values differ. Independent review executed the claim and it was false: `ltBoot`'s third argument lands before the script, so `--import` can step the child's clock with no production change, no env knob and no new ADR. The guard is deleted; it also had a proven bypass — `"auth": authStatus` counts as one key and still serves the latch — which is exactly what a source-text check cannot see and a behavioural one cannot miss. Recorded because the failure mode being fixed here and the failure mode of the first fix are the same one: **a thing that looks verified because something adjacent to it was verified.** #343 sweeps for other helpers that are correct, tested, and unpinned at their call site.

- **`/health` reported `auth.ok: true` on hosts where no request could be served (#308, ADR 0014).** Root cause, measured rather than reasoned about: **`claude auth status` reports whether a token is PRESENT, not whether it is VALID.** In an isolated empty `HOME`, with no token it exits 1 (`loggedIn: false`); with a **fabricated** token — a string that never existed — it exits **0** and reports `loggedIn: true, authMethod: "oauth_token"`.
  - Everything in the report follows. The service carries `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` in its `EnvironmentFile`, the probe inherits it, the child sees a token and exits 0. The same binary run by hand in an interactive shell has no such variable, falls back to an empty `~/.claude/.credentials.json`, and exits 1. The token was **present and expired** — presence is what the probe measured, validity is what the request needed.
  - **Not one host: all three Linux hosts in the fleet use that mechanism.** On any of them `auth.ok: true` meant "a token is set", and would have kept saying so indefinitely after expiry.
  - **The probe now reports what it measured.** When the child's credential came from the environment, exit 0 yields `auth.ok: null` / `lastOutcome: "token-present"` instead of `authenticated`. `null` is not a new state — ADR 0010 already defines it as "no conclusive verdict" and `ocp doctor` already treats it as WARN, not FAIL, so it does not block `ocp update`.
  - **A completed request is now conclusive evidence**, and free: it was happening anyway. A successful completion sets `auth.ok: true` / `okSource: "request"`, and deliberately leaves `lastOutcome` as the probe set it — overwriting it would make `/health` claim a probe ran when none did. So on an env-token host the steady state is `okSource: "request"` + `lastOutcome: "token-present"`, and that pairing is normal.
  - **The guard that was supposed to make provenance un-droppable could not see the hardest branch.** Its pattern matched `authStatus = {…};` and so structurally could not match the token-present write, which is a *ternary* — 4 of 5 assignments matched, and a `>= 4` floor let the most complex branch pass invisibly. Now an exact count of 5 over `authStatus = …;`, plus a behavioural assertion on that branch's own fields, so the contract does not rest on a source pattern at all. A mutation dropping the fields from exactly that branch now turns the money test red.
  - **The verdict's provenance is a separate pair of fields from the probe's outcome** — `auth.okSource` (`none`/`probe`/`request`/`expired`) and `auth.okAt`, alongside the existing `lastOutcome`/`lastCheck`. This is not tidiness: keying the freshness window on `lastOutcome` made it a fiction in two ways, both caught by independent review and both proven by execution. **(a)** Every probe completion rewrites `lastOutcome`, and a probe always completes within ~610s by default — shorter than the window — so the decay branch was **unreachable**, and the docs described a semantic the system did not have. **(b)** Worse, a single inconclusive probe disarmed it *permanently*: the inconclusive branches preserve `ok` (correctly) while rewriting `lastOutcome`, so a request-established verdict stopped matching and stayed `true` forever — replayed, `T+100h` still read `auth.ok: true`. That is the unbounded false `true` this whole design exists to prevent, reintroduced by its own guard. Keyed on `okSource`/`okAt`, the same replay now expires at `T+16min`.
  - **An exit-0 probe resets the rejection tally on both branches.** The first implementation preserved it on the token-present branch, silently removing ADR 0010's self-healing on exactly the hosts that use the env-token mechanism — once the tally reached the degrade threshold nothing could lower it, because a successful probe was the only thing that did. A successful request clears it too.
  - **A probe never overwrites a fresher request verdict.** The token-present branch measured less than a recent completed request; letting it clobber that would contradict this change's own evidence hierarchy.
  - **That verdict expires after 15 minutes, and the expiry is the load-bearing part.** Without it the design creates a latch strictly worse than the bug it fixes: a proxy serves, the verdict is raised, the token later expires, requests start failing — and *nothing can lower it*, because the probe still sees a token present and cannot contradict a stale `true`. That state is byte-identical to what #308 reported, reached by a different path. This is #324's shape in the opposite direction, and its criterion applies: do not ask what the clearing condition is, ask whether it is **reachable**.
  - The expiry is a **pure injectable function**, not an env var. The window has to be testable at arbitrary values without becoming an operator knob — a knob on a safety decision is a knob for turning the safety off, the same argument ADR 0010 makes for `AUTH_DEGRADE_AFTER`.
  - **`/health`'s `status` cannot be moved by any of this.** `proxyHealthStatus` reads `consecutiveFailures` and never `ok`, so no verdict change here can flip a host to `degraded`. It does **not** follow that the tally is untouched — an exit-0 probe resets it on both branches and a successful request clears it, which is a deliberate restoration of ADR 0010's self-heal. An earlier revision of this line said "nothing here writes that tally"; that was false, and it survived one round after the commit message that introduced the fix had already named it as concealment.
  - Request *failures* deliberately do **not** lower the verdict. `server.mjs` does not classify a child's failure — it rejects with raw `stderr` — so doing that would mean matching text in another program's error output, and two reproduction attempts (fabricated token, no credentials) both **hung** rather than failing in any classifiable way. The asymmetry is safe in the right direction: the change can only raise confidence on conclusive evidence, never lower it on evidence nobody understands. ADR 0014 records this as an open question rather than guessing.


- **`ocp restart` no longer leaves the service DOWN when the start half fails (#325).** A restart is stop+start; when the start half failed, `cmd_restart` printed `✗ Restart command failed.` and returned 1 — with a healthy proxy already unloaded and the port dead. That wording reads like the restart was *declined*. It wasn't: the service was stopped, by this command, and stayed stopped. This happened in production during the v3.28.0 rollout — `launchctl bootout` succeeded, `launchctl bootstrap` returned `5: Input/output error`, and the proxy was down until someone noticed.
  - **The failure was transient, and one retry was enough.** The identical `bootstrap` succeeded by hand seconds later — unchanged plist, unchanged domain — and `ocp update` ran the same two commands successfully minutes afterwards. The plausible cause is a race against the `bootout` that just completed, which is what a short backoff fixes. Each resolved command now gets up to 3 attempts with a rising delay.
  - **The verdict now comes from the health probe, not from a subcommand's exit status.** A failing command does not prove the proxy is down — the service manager may have started it anyway — and a succeeding one does not prove it is up. So the failure branch no longer returns early; it falls through and measures.
  - **Four outcomes, four messages, where there were two.** The missing cell was the incident itself: a command failed *and* the proxy is not answering. That now says `✗ THE PROXY IS DOWN`, states that this command stopped it and could not start it again, and prints the exact start command the resolver produced for this host. The inverse cell — command failed but the proxy is answering — reports the service as UP with a warning rather than a silent `✓`, because silently swallowing it would train operators to ignore the retry warnings that are the early signal for the DOWN case.
  - **The local-`curl`-fault cell is reachable in two states and no longer describes them alike.** With a failed command it said "Restart command ran" — false, and contradicting the warning printed immediately above it — and offered no way back. It now says the command failed, that the proxy *may* be down because the stop half already ran, and prints the start command. The all-succeeded wording is byte-for-byte unchanged, pinned by a control test.
  - **What the DOWN message does not claim.** It reports what the probe measured just now, not that the service "will stay stopped": a service binding slowly after a *partially* failed plan may still be coming up. Polling `/health` to a deadline (as the update path's post-flight does) is **not** implemented here and is left as follow-up; re-running the printed start command is harmless if it turns out to be up.
  - **Exit-code contract, now in `ocp restart --help`:** 0 means the proxy answers `/health` afterwards, **not** that every command succeeded. A script keying on exit 0 as "the commands ran cleanly" needs to read the output.
  - **A local `curl` fault still yields `UNKNOWN`, never `DOWN`.** The new branch keys on "probe non-zero", and #299's local-fault arm is also non-zero; ordering keeps a machine that merely cannot *run* curl from being told its proxy is dead. A dedicated test pins the precedence, and a mutation confirms it can fail.
- **OCP's own inbound credentials no longer reach a spawned `claude`, on every spawn path (#328).** `spawnClaudeProcess` built the child environment as `{...process.env}` and stripped only four `ANTHROPIC_*` variables, so `PROXY_API_KEY` — the credential that authenticates callers **to** OCP — was handed to every child. The child is the one component that reads attacker-controlled text, and it has no use for that key: it authenticates to Anthropic with `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` and never talks to OCP. `OCP_ADMIN_KEY` and `PROXY_ANONYMOUS_KEY` leaked the same way.
  - **Demonstrated, not theorised.** On a host running two OCP instances split by Unix identity — an unprivileged one backing an agent that answers untrusted users, a sudo-capable one for the operator — both configured with the *same* `PROXY_API_KEY`, an independent reviewer sent one benign-framed prompt: the child read the key from its own environment, called the privileged instance with it, and the resulting child confirmed passwordless sudo. The identity boundary was bypassed without ever being attacked. Sharing one key across instances is an operator error and was fixed there; the key reaching the child is OCP's.
  - **There were FOUR sites, not two, and two of them were found only by independent review.** The third was the one that mattered most. An independent review found `lib/tui/session.mjs` carrying its own copy of the same four-name denylist, so every secret reached the tmux pane. That is the **only** request path when `CLAUDE_TUI_MODE=true`: `server.mjs` selects `callClaudeTui` over `callClaude` wholesale, so a fix confined to `spawnClaudeProcess` would have left every TUI request unprotected while the changelog claimed otherwise. Both the pane's `env -u` prefix and the tmux **server's** own environment are scrubbed now — and the second is not merely belt-and-braces: with only the pane prefix in place, `tmux show-environment -g` **still returned all three secrets from inside the pane**, a cross-platform retrieval path, not just the Linux `/proc` one. **Scoped honestly:** that only holds when OCP starts the tmux server. If one is already running — which OCP's reap deliberately does not kill when foreign sessions exist, and OCP uses the default socket — the operator's own shell environment remains readable from the pane, and `ocp-connect` writes an OCP credential into that shell. A dedicated socket would close it; tracked as follow-up — a server started by OCP inherits OCP's environment and its panes inherit from the server, and OCP's boot reap calls `tmux kill-server`, which guarantees the next `new-session` rebuilds it under that environment. So the leak was the default path, not an edge case.
  - **The fourth site is `setup.mjs`**, which carried its own copy of the same denylist on its `claude -p` auth probe (and no `env` option at all on `claude --version`, so that one inherited everything). Lower severity — that child's prompt is the literal `"ping"`, so the injection chain of this issue is not reachable through it — but it is reached by `ocp update`, and leaving it would have kept the "remember to extend it everywhere" defect alive. **Stated plainly: the `setup.mjs` fix has no test.** Reverting it leaves the suite green. Running the real `setup.mjs` in a harness writes configs and installs autostart, and this repo has an incident from a test harness doing exactly that, so building one for a P1 with no injection path was judged the wrong trade. It is a declared gap, not an oversight.
  - **A per-user key from `ocp keys add` is invisible to both of the earlier passes, and that is the multi-user case.** `scrubInboundAuthEnv` can only remove a value it can *see* — the three named credentials in the parent environment. But `ocp-connect`'s documented flow when the remote requires auth issues a key from OCP's SQLite store, which is neither of those names nor equal to any of their values. In multi mode `PROXY_API_KEY` is typically unset, so the by-value pass is **entirely inert**: measured, `removed: []` with the per-user key reaching the child untouched. A third pass matches by **format** — every OCP-issued key is `ocp_` plus exactly 32 base64url characters by construction (`keys.mjs`) — so it is caught under any variable name, while a real OpenAI key (`sk-…`) cannot collide. The pattern is asserted against a genuinely minted key rather than a literal, so changing the key format fails a test instead of silently disabling the pass.
  - **The TUI pane prefix now covers aliases too.** It is a list of names, so it could not express "whatever variable carries the credential" — which mattered precisely in the foreign-tmux-server case where the server-env scrub never applies and the prefix is the only defence.
  - **Deleting by NAME was not enough, and OCP itself is what proved it.** `ocp-connect` writes the user's OCP credential into **`OPENAI_API_KEY`** — every shell rc, `launchctl setenv`'s user domain on macOS, `~/.config/environment.d/` on Linux — and `docs/lan-mode.md` teaches the same by hand. OCP's own autostart runs in exactly those two scopes. So on any host that has run `ocp connect`, the same secret still reached the child under a name the list does not contain, and **#328 was not closed**. A reviewer demonstrated it end to end against a real server. `scrubInboundAuthEnv` now also removes any variable whose **value** is one of the inbound credentials, whatever it is called. Not by adding `OPENAI_API_KEY` to the name list: a child can legitimately need a real OpenAI key via an MCP server, and the goal is to remove OCP's credential, not that variable. Values shorter than 8 characters are matched by name only, so a one-character key cannot strip every flag that happens to share its value — a stated limit, not an oversight.
    - **The test already described this threat while the code could not implement it.** `the VALUE of <name> reached the child under some other name` has been in the assertions since the first revision; it could never fire, because the implementation deleted by name. Both now agree, and a mutation proves the assertion is live.
  - **The declared gap is closed, and the reasoning that declared it was wrong.** An earlier revision called `setup.mjs`'s two sites untestable because running the real `setup.mjs` writes configs and installs autostart. A reviewer pointed out the false dichotomy: the choice was never "run it or don't test it", it is "run it or **slice** it" — the technique `AGENTS.md` endorses for `ocp-connect`. Both sites are now covered by slice tests that spawn nothing and write nothing, and each fails independently when its own site is reverted.
  - **A frozen list, not four more `delete` lines.** `INBOUND_AUTH_ENV_VARS` + `scrubInboundAuthEnv()` in `lib/spawn-auth.mjs` are what every site calls. The ten: `server.mjs`'s two binary lookups, its auth probe, its `-p` spawn and its keychain read; `lib/tui/`'s pane prefix and tmux server env; `setup.mjs`'s three spawns. Those two sites had already drifted in purpose, and a denylist you must remember to extend in both places is precisely the failure mode that produced this. A new inbound-auth variable is added in one place.
  - Deliberately **not** stripped: `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` (an outbound credential the child genuinely needs — an integration assertion pins that it reaches the child. The isolated-spawn branch also re-injects it downstream of the scrub — but only under `decision.isolated && decision.token`, so on a real-HOME fallback (`OCP_SPAWN_REAL_HOME=1`) a scrub-site edit **would** break requests. The earlier unconditional wording was wrong) and `PROXY_ADVERTISE_ANON_KEY` (a boolean flag, not a secret).
  - **Scope, stated rather than implied:** this stops OCP from *handing* the credential to the child. It does not put it out of reach — the child runs as the same Unix user, so on Linux it can still read the parent's `/proc/<pid>/environ` or the 0600 unit file if it has `Bash`. Defence in depth is the claim; "the child cannot obtain it" is not.
  - Presence, not truthiness: an empty-string secret is still removed. `if (env[name])` would leave the *name* visible to the child, and a mutation proves the distinction is tested.
  - **Sixteen tests, all behavioural.** The fake `claude` dumps its own environment and the assertions read it; nothing greps source. **Seven of the ten scrub sites are covered separately** (six before review 5, which found the alias layer unpinned) — reverting any one of those turns exactly one test red while the others stay green, so a partial fix cannot pass there. The remaining three (`server.mjs`'s two binary lookups and the macOS keychain read) are scrubbed for consistency and are **not** test-pinned: none reads attacker-controlled text, and `server.mjs` cannot be imported by the suite. Stated rather than glossed — an earlier revision of this entry claimed all of them were covered, and a reviewer disproved it by mutation. Each test also asserts a control (the capture is non-empty and looks like an environment) so a broken capture cannot satisfy "no secret found" vacuously.
- **The auth probe could wedge at a stale `auth.ok=false` and block `ocp update` forever (#324).** Two individually-correct rules composed into a trap: a conclusive non-zero exit latches `ok:false`, and an inconclusive probe (timeout / spawn failure) deliberately *preserves* the last conclusive verdict — right on its own, since a timeout measures host load rather than credential validity. Together they mean **only a conclusive success can clear the latch, and a probe that reliably times out never produces one.** Observed in production: a host served 51 requests with zero errors and `/health` reported `ok` throughout, while `ocp update` refused for hours with `Pre-upgrade check failed: fix_oauth`. The only symptom was the upgrade path being shut, and nothing anywhere said "stuck".
  - **Additive under ADR 0012.** New field on `/health`: **`auth.consecutiveInconclusive`** — consecutive inconclusive probes since the last conclusive one, reset to 0 by *both* conclusive outcomes. It is the first change to use ADR 0012's standing authorization.
  - **`auth.ok` is deliberately unchanged.** The last conclusive verdict really was `false`; rewriting the rule that determines a grandfathered B.2 field's value would be a contract change needing its own ADR (the ADR 0010 test). Staleness lives in the new counter, and only a consumer that must *decide* something reads it.
  - **`/health`'s `status` cannot move because of this.** `proxyHealthStatus` reads `consecutiveFailures`, never `ok`, and inconclusive probes still do not touch that tally. A test asserts both.
  - **`doctor` is where the decision changed.** `classifyAuthOk` now returns WARN instead of FAIL when a latched `false` has at least `AUTH_STALE_AFTER_INCONCLUSIVE` inconclusive probes behind it **and the most recent probe did not conclude**, so `next_action.kind` is no longer `fix_oauth` and the upgrade proceeds. A genuine, current rejection still FAILs — pinned by a control test, because deleting the FAIL branch would be a far worse bug than the one being fixed.
  - **Backward compatibility is pinned too.** During an upgrade, `/health` is read from the *running* (older) server, which does not emit the new field. An absent or non-numeric counter reads as 0 — never as "stale" — so a pre-#324 server keeps reporting real rejections to the gate.
  - **The counter alone was not a sufficient condition, and only mutation revealed it.** An independent reviewer made the server *preserve* the counter on a conclusive rejection instead of resetting it — the whole suite stayed green, while the resulting state (`ok:false`, `lastOutcome:"rejected"`, counter ≥ 3) would have been read as stale and unlocked the gate **seconds after a conclusive rejection**. The decider now also requires the most recent outcome to be inconclusive. A correct server can never emit that combination, so this is strictly tightening on correct servers and a backstop against an incorrect one — and the producer's reset is now pinned from its own side too, in the order (inconclusive first, then rejection) that the original test could not reach.
  - The threshold lives in `lib/constants.mjs` (`AUTH_STALE_AFTER_INCONCLUSIVE`), not in either consumer: a threshold the producer and the decider disagree about is exactly the drift that file exists to prevent.

### Changed

- **ADR 0012 and ADR 0013 signed off; both move `Proposed` → `Accepted`.** Neither *decision* changes: no code, no endpoint, no field, and neither ADR's Decision or Consequences section is edited. What changes is that the repo stops citing unapproved authorizations as though they were settled. Both were already load-bearing in shipped artifacts while marked `Proposed` — at v3.28.0, `ALIGNMENT.md:116` states ADR 0012's standing authorization as an operative rule and `:134` cites it for `/cache/stats`'s `inflight`/`requesters`; `server.mjs:2974`, `lib/tool-support.mjs:55` and `README.md` § "Client-tools boundary" cite ADR 0013, and the 400 body OCP returns to callers ends with the literal string `See ADR 0013.` (reproduced against a running v3.28.0 instance). A supreme document quoting a pending authorization, and a production error message naming a pending ADR, is worse than either outcome: it makes `Proposed` stop meaning anything, so the next reader cannot tell which pending records are genuinely undecided.
  - **ADR 0013 is descriptive**, not a new decision — it records behaviour that shipped in v3.28.0, verified against OpenAI's published specification field by field during #322's independent review. It keeps its own exit: § "The shape a real implementation would take" records what a genuine implementation needs, and § "Consequences" states the refusal is "the natural thing to delete" if that is ever built.
  - **ADR 0012's Context table is corrected — two of its four rows carried a wrong shipped version, and one mis-stated where its field lives.** `tui` was recorded as v3.19.0; those six fields are listed in the **v3.20.0** section of this file. `pool` was recorded as a top-level `/health` field shipped in v3.20.0; it is nested at **`tui.pool`** (ADR 0008 § "Observability", confirmed against a live `/health` with no top-level `pool` key) and shipped with the warm pane pool (#158) in **v3.22.1**. That row was independently impossible as written — ADR 0008 is dated 2026-07-13 and v3.20.0 was released 2026-06-10, so a field ADR 0012 records as authorized by ADR 0008 could not have shipped a month before that ADR existed. This is not cosmetic: § "Retroactive scope" authorizes those four changes **"as of their shipped versions"**, so a wrong stamp authorizes the wrong thing, and sign-off is the one moment the repo inspects an ADR's factual claims. Rows 1 and 3 were checked and are correct.
  - **ADR 0012's failure mode is now instrumented for compliant additions — not closed.** Its § "Consequences" accepts the risk of "surface growing one 'obviously fine' field at a time" and shipped with nothing able to see it. `CLAUDE.md`'s `release_kit:` overlay gains a `governance_audits:` block that reads condition 5's audit trail once per release and requires the answer be written down, including "none this cycle" and a cumulative running total — the total is the load-bearing part, because per-release increments are exactly what normalises the accumulation. **Its blind spot is documented in the block itself:** the sweep greps for the `additive under ADR 0012` marker, so it sees only additions whose author complied. A field added *without* the marker — the exact shape of the four pre-#288 additions — yields a green "none this cycle". Detecting silent growth needs a per-release record of each B.2 endpoint's real response key set, which is genuine machinery and is deliberately not built here.
  - Recorded rather than glossed: **ADR 0006 (`Proposed — owner reviewing`) and ADR 0008 (`Proposed`) are the same defect and are deliberately NOT included.** ADR 0006 is the more serious instance by a wide margin — it defines the Class A/B taxonomy every PR in this repo cites, appearing on 21 lines of `ALIGNMENT.md`, 7 of `CLAUDE.md`, 7 of the PR template and 1 of `AGENTS.md` (counts are lines containing a reference, not total occurrences), and cited by six other ADRs. Its status line states maintainer state, not document state, and clearing that on the maintainer's behalf is not a contributor's call.
  - **A consequence of that exclusion, stated because this entry argues from consistency:** accepting these two leaves a new `Accepted` → `Proposed` edge in the ADR graph — ADR 0012 declares "Extends: ADR 0006" and ADR 0013's authority line names it. If ADR 0006 were later rejected or rewritten, ADR 0012's standing authorization would be left dangling. The practical risk is near zero (rejecting 0006 would orphan the entire Class A/B taxonomy, not just these two), but the edge is real and this PR creates it.

## v3.28.0 — 2026-08-03

### Fixed

- **The five remaining probes in `ocp` that narrated a local `curl` fault as a fact about the proxy (#296, #299, #300).** Each was a bare `curl` whose `else` branch asserted a *specific cause* for every nonzero exit — "bound to localhost only" (`ocp lan`), "Proxy not responding after restart" (`cmd_restart`'s own verdict), "key may be invalid or revoked" and "proxy is reachable but chat completion did not succeed" (`cmd_connect`) — so a machine with no usable `curl` was told to change `CLAUDE_BIND`, or to regenerate a working key, or that a successful restart had failed. `cmd_restart`'s is the worst of the five: it is the check that decides whether a restart worked, and its `return 1` propagates into `_cmd_update_light`'s `restart_status` (#255) on the recovery path.
  - **Two of the five were not in any issue.** `cmd_connect`'s smoke-test pair (Step 8) was found by this change's own tests, not by reading. Three prior enumeration passes (#261, #273, #278) each believed they had the complete list; each was assembled by eye.
  - New `_curl_probe` serves the verdict-shaped sites with **three** states — succeeded / ran-and-failed / could-not-run — because for a caller that narrates its own conclusion, collapsing the last two into one nonzero *is* the defect. The payload-shaped sites keep `_curl_or_die`. The could-not-run predicate is now extracted to `_curl_is_local_fault` and shared, so the two helpers cannot drift.
  - Guarded **behaviorally**, not by grepping for `curl`: each case removes curl from `$PATH` (or exits 127 at one URL) and asserts what the command *says*, and each is paired with a control proving the genuine-remote diagnosis is still produced. A source-grep would pass the moment a bare `curl` reappeared in an unanticipated shape — which is exactly how the three prior passes missed sites.
  - `set -e` note: `_curl_probe` on its own line terminates the script on every failing case. Both call sites use `|| rc=$?`. This was a real bug in the first draft, caught by these tests.
- **`start.sh`'s Linux bind check ran a bare `ss`, the same restricted-PATH exposure #246 fixed for `lsof` on macOS (#298).** `resolveBinaryPath` was applied to the darwin branch only; the Linux branch emitted `ss -tlnp` with no absolute-path preference. `ss` lives at `/usr/sbin/ss` on Debian and Raspberry Pi OS, so a `PATH` without `/usr/sbin` loses it — and this is the branch that runs on the majority of this project's own serving hosts. Both branches now resolve, with the same fallback direction (absolute when present, bare name otherwise, so an unusual layout is never worse off than before). `classifyBindCheck` threads the new `ssPath` through; without that the resolution would exist but be unreachable from `setup.mjs`, its only caller.
  - The netstat cross-check is still deliberately **not** extended to Linux: GNU netstat's `-p` means "show PID/program name", not BSD/macOS's "protocol filter" `-p tcp`, and reusing the darwin command shape there could make the first-ever start on a fresh Linux host refuse. Path resolution carries no such risk — it is a preference with a fallback to the exact prior behavior, not a new command shape.
  - The existing linux test asserted `cmd === "ss -tlnp"` with no `existsSyncFn` injected, which would have made it **host-dependent** under this change: green on macOS (no `/usr/sbin/ss`), red on a Debian runner. Replaced with both branches driven explicitly, mirroring the darwin pair.
  - Corrected while touching it: this file's header described the non-darwin branch as "still a bare `lsof` call". It never was — the branch emits `ss`. The conclusion was right, the identifier wrong; #298's own filing had to correct the same slip in the review that prompted it.
- **`ocp update --target=vX.Y.Z` (equals form) was silently dropped by the Node CLI parser (#297).** `ocp`'s bash layer has understood both spellings since #272 and forwards the user's argv verbatim (`exec node .../upgrade.mjs "$@"`), but the Node side did `args.indexOf("--target")` — separate-token form only. So the equals form arrived as `target: undefined`: #272's refusal never fired on `fresh_install`, and on the full/cross-minor path #259's pin was silently not applied and the upgrade went to `doctor.latest_version`. That is exactly the "user believes they pinned a version and did not" failure #260 exists to prevent, reintroduced across a layer boundary.
  - Both spellings now go through one `parseFlagValue(args, flag)`, which reports `seen` separately from `value`. That split is load-bearing: `--target=` and a trailing `--target` used to collapse into "not passed" and drop a *typed* pin in silence. They are now refused, matching the guard `--post-flight-only` already carried and bash's own `_TARGET_SEEN`/`_TARGET_VAL` contract.
  - `--post-flight-only` uses the same parser. Its equals form is **not** reachable from the product (`ocp` only ever invokes it internally with separate tokens), so this is not a second instance of the defect — but two parsers for the same shape in one file is how they drift.
  - **Why #297 slipped is the part worth keeping:** #272's tests exercised the equals form at the bash layer, where its fix was, and every Node-side #272 test calls `runUpgrade({target})` directly. Nothing ever drove argv → parse → `runUpgrade`, so the boundary itself had no coverage. There is now a wiring test that spawns the real CLI, and it is discriminating for that gap alone — reverting only the entry point (leaving `parseFlagValue` correct) fails that test and nothing else.
- **`ocp-connect` under-declared four of the seven models it registers into a client's OpenClaw config (#309).** The write loop hardcoded `contextWindow: 200000` for every model; after #213/#307 declared `claude-opus-5`, `-4-8`, `-4-7` and `claude-sonnet-5` at a native 1M, that literal capped four of them at a fifth of their real window. **Not named in the issue, found while measuring:** `claude-sonnet-5`'s `maxTokens` was under-declared too — the family key `"claude-sonnet" -> 32000` was correct while `claude-sonnet-4-6` was the only sonnet, and `claude-sonnet-5` is 64000. Four of seven models were mis-declared; all seven now match `models.json` exactly.
  - **The issue's suggested fix does not work as stated.** `contextWindow` is not a family property — `claude-opus-5` (1M) and `claude-opus-4-6` (200k) share the `claude-opus` prefix — so "add a context-window column to `get_model_meta`" is only correct together with the longest-prefix-wins ordering the function already had. Specific ids are simply longer keys, so they win over their own family with no new matching logic.
  - Family rows keep `contextWindow: 200000` as the conservative floor, and the unknown-id fallback gets it too, following this file's own established rule: under-advertising caps a client lower than the model allows (safe), over-advertising promises capacity that does not exist. A window is raised only for an id verified against the SPOT.
  - **The suite was structurally blind to this.** Its model-registry test asserts `<=` — "never OVER-advertises" — and an under-advertisement passes that by definition. The new test asserts **equality** against `models.json` for both numeric columns, for every id the SPOT declares, so a model added there now fails until `ocp-connect` is updated too.
  - `contextWindow` also moved out of the write loop's pinned literal-constants snapshot and into its meta-derived assertions. It was pinned as a literal precisely *because* the classifier did not return it; leaving it there would have re-frozen the number this issue was about, in the place that made it invisible.
- **`ALIGNMENT.md`'s Hybrid note cited unrelated code, and the citation style that produced that is now changed (#292).** The note routed reviewers to `server.mjs` line 845–849 for the `anthropic-ratelimit-unified-*` extraction block; those lines are the `OCP_LOCAL_TOOLS` boot gate. This repo's entire enforcement model is line-cited evidence a reviewer is expected to open, so a citation that lands on unrelated code is a defect in the enforcement mechanism itself, not a typo. Verified stale as far back as v3.26.0. **The correction is anchored on a grep-able marker rather than a line number**, because #292's own corrected range (2349–2354) was *already stale again* by the time the fix was written — the block is at 2374–2379 today. `// ALIGNMENT:` appears exactly once in `server.mjs` and marks this block deliberately.
- **A forced `tool_choice` is now refused instead of silently answered with prose (#311, ADR 0013 — Proposed).** OCP accepts `tools`/`tool_choice` and emits no `tool_calls` at all (`grep -c 'tool_calls' server.mjs` → **0** before this change; it is 2 after, both occurrences inside the explanatory comment this change adds). Under a permissive `tool_choice` that is spec-conformant — the model *may* call a tool and text is a legal outcome — so those requests are **unchanged**. Under a forcing `tool_choice` (`"required"`, or a named function) the specification requires `finish_reason: "tool_calls"`, and answering with prose and `finish_reason: "stop"` is not a degraded answer but a **silently wrong** one: no 400, no warning field, and `"stop"` means the turn ended normally, so the client has nothing to branch on. Those now return `400` with the spec's own error shape (`code: "unsupported_parameter"`, `param: "tool_choice"`).
  - **All five spec-defined forcing forms are covered**, not just the two the first draft caught: `tool_choice: "required"`, `{type:"function"}`, `{type:"custom"}`, `{type:"allowed_tools", allowed_tools:{mode:"required"}}`, and the deprecated-but-accepted `function_call: {name}`. The three missing ones were found by cross-vendor review reading the installed OpenAI SDK types — `ChatCompletionToolChoiceOption` is a five-member union and `function_call` is a separate field entirely, so each miss left that request on the original silently-wrong path: the same defect through a different door. `error.param` names the field the client actually sent, so a legacy caller is not sent to fix `tool_choice` they never set.
  - Their permissive siblings stay permissive: `allowed_tools` with `mode: "auto"` only *narrows* the set the model may pick from, and `function_call: "auto"|"none"` permit a text answer. Refusing those would break a client that is merely constraining its tool list.
  - **The narrowness is the safety property, not a compromise.** Refusing whenever `tools` is present would have taken down every OpenClaw agent on this project's fleet the day it shipped — every OpenClaw turn carries a tool list and accepts a text answer. That case is pinned by a test and by a mutation that fails it.
  - **Why refuse rather than implement:** `claude` owns its agentic loop and executes its own tools; OpenAI's protocol is stateless and the *client* owns the loop. A CLI `tool_use` block is the CLI reporting what it is about to do itself — forwarding it as a `tool_call` would instruct the HTTP client to run `Bash`/`Read` while the CLI ran them anyway. Checked against CLI 2.1.220: no flag delegates execution back to the caller (`--allowedTools`/`--tools` select built-ins, `--mcp-config` points at servers the CLI itself calls, `--agents` defines subagents). ADR 0013 records the shape a real implementation would take and why it is its own feature.
  - Honest about what remains: a client that *offers* tools and genuinely wants them used still gets prose with no signal. This does not fix that; it removes the class where OCP's answer contradicts an explicit instruction.

### Changed

- **Adding a read-only field to a grandfathered Class B.2 endpoint no longer requires its own ADR (#288, ADR 0012 — Proposed, needs maintainer sign-off).** ADR 0006 froze the grandfathered B.2 surface and made "any change to the response shape" a new authorization request; a response gaining a field is a response-shape change, so by the text every additive field needed an ADR. Four shipped without one (`/cache/stats` `inflight`/`requesters`; `/health` `tui`, `auth.lastOutcome`/`consecutiveFailures`, `pool`), three of them riding along inside feature ADRs written for other reasons — so the rule was neither followed nor enforced. ADR 0012 supplies the authorization **once, standing**, bounded by six conditions (additive only, read-only, no new endpoint or method, same endpoint purpose, field names in the PR body and CHANGELOG, B.2 only), and retroactively records the four. What still costs an ADR — new endpoints, new methods, new subjects, changed semantics — is unchanged. The loosening is real and ADR 0012 says so explicitly, along with the revert path if the bet is wrong.
- **The request-body cap said "5MB" while counting characters (#310).** `MAX_BODY_SIZE` is compared against `body.length` — UTF-16 code units — because the accumulator is a JS string (`body += chunk`). The label rendered it as `MB`, so every reader reasoned in bytes. This is not cosmetic: it made two review rounds of #307 conclude a 3,000,000-character CJK prompt would be rejected with a 413, put that conclusion into ADR 0011 with a threshold computed against the wrong quantity, and nearly shipped operator guidance to raise a knob that did not need touching. Measured: 3,000,000 CJK characters is 9,000,000 bytes on the wire and sails past the 5,242,880-**character** cap.
  - Worse than misleading at the default: with `CLAUDE_MAX_BODY_SIZE=1000` the old label rendered **`max 0MB`**, because `Math.round(1000 / 1048576)` is `0`. Any cap below ~512 KB produced a nonsense message.
  - The comparison is **deliberately unchanged**. A byte cap of the same number is never more permissive (UTF-8 byte length ≥ UTF-16 unit length for every character class — ASCII 1:1, Latin-1 2:1, CJK 3:1, astral 4:2), so switching to bytes would reject bodies accepted today. That is a Class B.1 contract change, not a label fix. What changed is that the label and the 413 body now state the quantity they measured.
  - `README.md`'s environment-variable table and limits table said "bytes, default 5 MB" — the same misconception, propagated to the user-facing docs. Both corrected.
- **`warn_count`'s WARN-multiplicity gap is closed, and the second site is classified rather than papered over (#304).** Mutating either `warn_count` computation to `Math.min(1, …)` survived the whole suite, because every doctor shape the suite exercised had **at most one WARN**. `doctor.mjs` prints `Summary: N FAIL, M WARN` from that number, so a cap at 1 tells the operator that one of two real warnings does not exist.
  - The two sites survive for **different reasons**, and the distinction is the finding. `doctor.mjs:879` (the full run) is genuinely **uncovered** — a 2-WARN shape is reachable and nothing produced one; a new test combines #289's inconclusive-auth WARN with #220's two-enabled-units WARN on a fully mocked host and asserts `warn_count === 2`. `doctor.mjs:981` (`runOauthOnly`, the `--check oauth` path) is **unreachable by construction** — all three of its push sites push the same `oauth_ok` id on mutually exclusive branches, so its `checks` array can never hold two entries and a cap there is unobservable, not wrong.
  - The second test therefore pins the **premise**, not the cap: it drives all three branches and requires exactly one check from each. If a future change adds a second check to that path, the gap becomes real at that moment and this test says so — which is the only guard with any content there. Verified by mutation: adding a second `push()` inside `runOauthOnly` fails it by name.
- **`runPostFlightCheck`'s real probe lane laundered "could not run" into "unreachable" (#291).** A bare `catch { /* retry */ }` collapsed five distinct failures — curl missing (127), curl not executable (126), connection refused (7), HTTP error (22), timeout (28), and a non-JSON body — into one outcome, and the caller then printed `(unreachable)`: a claim about the **service**, made just as readily when the fault was that curl could not run on this machine. This is the exact conflation the #261 → #267 → #273 → #278 → #286 arc removed from sixteen bash sites, still sitting in the function those sites now **delegate their final verdict to** (`_cmd_update_light` and `_cmd_update_restart` both report whatever `--post-flight-only` says).
  - Each kind is now classified and rendered differently, and only the genuinely remote ones are narrated as remote. A local fault says so explicitly and tells the operator the upgrade may well have succeeded.
  - The `version-mismatch` branch — the one that was already correct and specific — keeps its message **byte-for-byte**, pinned by a test.
  - **The coverage note in the issue was the important half.** Every pre-existing test drove `opts.mockProbe`; the lane that ships is the `execSync` one, so a fix verified through the mock would have been verified on the lane that was never broken. `opts.execFn` makes the real lane drivable (mirroring `classifyBindCheck`'s injection convention), and all seven new tests use it — including one asserting the *success* path still works through the injected lane, so a broken injection cannot leave the group passing over a dead lane.
- **macOS restart resolution probed only the `gui` launchd domain, making a root LaunchDaemon a permanent refusal (#290).** `ocp` installs a per-user LaunchAgent, so `gui/<uid>` is the common case — but a root LaunchDaemon is a supported deployment (`scripts/doctor.mjs`'s own multi-unit-risk check looks for `/Library/LaunchDaemons`), and it is genuinely *not* registered in `gui`. The probe returned the "not-registered" sentinel, resolution reported `no-unit`, and the operator was told to bring a service under launchd that was already running. Fail-closed and loud — but on a false premise, and with no way out.
  - **The module's own comments were why nobody looked.** Two paragraphs stated there is "no multi-label ambiguity to resolve the way Linux has system-vs-user scope". That conflates two things and denies the real one: there is no multi-**label** ambiguity, but launchd has exactly the system-vs-user **domain** split those words dismissed. Corrected in place.
  - Both domains are probed now, and escalation is gated on the specific `""` signal ("gui says this label is not registered here") and **never** on `null` ("gui could not tell"). Escalating on `null` would convert an honest *unknown* — the verdict that makes resolution refuse rather than guess — into a confident claim about a domain with no evidence behind it.
  - **Scope, deliberately:** this detects and refuses *accurately*; it does not restart a root LaunchDaemon. That needs `sudo launchctl`, and running sudo from the upgrade path is a different operation with its own hazard surface. The refusal now says the service is running correctly and hands over the exact `sudo launchctl bootout system/… ` / `bootstrap system /Library/LaunchDaemons/…` pair, instead of blaming a service that is fine.
  - The escalation rule is extracted as `probeLaunchdDomains(run, expectedUnit)` — a pure function over an injected runner, matching `classifyLaunchdJob`/`classifyCmdlineOwner` — because a rule reachable only through a full `runUpgrade` is a rule nobody can pin.

- **`models.json` now declares each model's true context window, and the prompt truncation ceiling is derived per model instead of once globally (#213, ADR 0011).** The SPOT said `contextWindow: 200000` for every model, including `claude-opus-5`, `claude-opus-4-8`, `claude-opus-4-7` and `claude-sonnet-5`, which the CLI registry declares at a native 1M — a 5x understatement that made OCP truncate prompts those models would have accepted. It could not be corrected as metadata because ADR 0009 derived **one** ceiling as `max(contextWindow) × 3` across all entries, so a single 1M row would have raised the ceiling from 600k to 3,000,000 chars for *every* model, including `claude-haiku-4-5-20251001`, which really is 200k — converting clean OCP-side truncation into upstream API rejections. That coupling is now gone: `promptCharBudgetFor(models, modelId)` resolves the ceiling from the model the request actually named.
  - **Per model today:** 3,000,000 chars for the four native-1M models, 600,000 for `claude-opus-4-6`, `claude-sonnet-4-6` and `claude-haiku-4-5-20251001`. The 200k models' ceiling is **unchanged** — that is the property distinguishing this from simply raising the ceiling, and it is pinned end-to-end against a live server child by "integration (#213): the SAME oversized prompt is truncated for a 200k model and NOT for a 1M model", which also asserts the 1M model still truncates at exactly 3,000,000 rather than not at all.
  - **Windows verified id-anchored against the compiled CLI 2.1.220 binary**, each record bounded at the next `{id:"` separator. A fixed-width window after the anchor bleeds into the neighbouring record and reports `claude-opus-4-6`/`claude-sonnet-4-6` as `native_1m`, which they are not; cross-validated binary-wide (`native_1m:!0` 6x, `context:{window:1e6` 6x, same six records). The per-entry regression table now requires the registry value exactly — the previous "registry value **or** the deliberate 200000 cap" branch is removed, which makes that table discriminating for the first time (all seven rows used to resolve to 200000).
  - **`CLAUDE_MAX_PROMPT_CHARS` and `ocp settings maxPromptChars` are unchanged in kind — still absolute overrides, exactly as ADR 0009 specified — but they are now explicitly *global*:** setting either pins the ceiling to that one number for every model and suppresses the derivation. `GET /settings` still returns a plain number and, with no override set, still returns **600,000** (the fallback: smallest known window × 3), so the default-path response does not move. A Class B.2 contract change under ADR 0006, authorized by ADR 0011. There is deliberately no `PATCH` value that clears the override back to per-model derivation; restart without the env var.
  - Downstream: OpenClaw's compaction budget scales linearly off `contextWindow`, so `ocp update` will now sync a 1M window for those four models, diverging from OpenClaw's own bundled registry (which hardcodes 200000; [openclaw#22979](https://github.com/openclaw/openclaw/issues/22979) was closed not-planned). `/v1/models` is **not** affected — it emits only `{id, object, owned_by, created}` and has never carried `contextWindow`.

## v3.27.0 — 2026-08-02

Minor release. Two new tunables (`CLAUDE_AUTH_CHECK_INTERVAL_MS`, `CLAUDE_AUTH_CHECK_TIMEOUT_MS`) and two new `/health` fields (`auth.lastOutcome`, `auth.consecutiveFailures`) land — see Added — alongside the large CLI/doctor reliability batch below and one `server.mjs` correctness fix (#232) to the async auth-probe verdict.

37 PRs landed on `main` after v3.26.0 before this entry was written; only one of them (issue #232, merged as PR #275) got an `Unreleased` line at merge time, written by that PR's own author. The other 36 shipped silently — a coordination gap, not a quality gap: every one of these PRs carried an independent fresh-context reviewer (Iron Rule 10) and mutation-proven tests, and several went through two or three review rounds that caught real defects the first pass missed. This entry reconstructs them after the fact, grouped by story rather than by PR number.

Read together, most of this batch is one shape of bug, hit independently at a dozen call sites: **a check's own failure to run got treated as the answer it would have given if it had run and found nothing.** A hard-coded restart target got "restarted" whether or not it was the process actually holding the port. `cmd_restart` had no failure exit code at all. `lsof`/`curl`/`python3` simply being absent from `$PATH` looked identical, downstream, to each of them running cleanly and reporting an empty or negative result. The fix, repeated a dozen times across this batch, is always the same: distinguish "the command could not run" from "the command ran and told you something." First fixed at the request-diagnostics layer by #232 below, the same pattern turned out to also describe most of the control-plane tooling (`ocp update`, `ocp restart`, rollback, and the bash CLI's own status commands).

The other thing worth reading in its own right, because it changes exit codes on scripts people may already have automated around: **`ocp update --target` now behaves consistently everywhere it appears, and that consistency is enforced by refusing rather than by silently doing the old thing.** See Changed below before you script around it.

`server.mjs` is untouched by every PR in this entry except #232 (unchanged from the existing write-up below) — confirmed by diffing every one of the other 36 merge commits against `server.mjs` individually.

### Changed
